\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
This catalogue preserves the mathematical interfaces, counterexamples and
component validation records from the development of the concrete Helios
reduction and its deferred machine-execution implementation. The accompanying
\href{main.pdf}{formal reference} states the current symbolic and computational
secrecy results and their assumptions. In particular, polynomial-time
implementation of the complete computational reductions is an explicit external
assumption; the execution fragments collected here do not discharge it.
\end{abstract}
\paragraph{Historical provenance.}
The material is extracted from the development recorded at
source revision \texttt{9273cdc}.
The component results remain checked at their original source paths. Local
validation counts describe their recorded test campaigns. Section order follows
the construction of those components; it does not prescribe further work or
qualify the current secrecy theorem. Each section's interface and exclusions
are local to that component. The native-machine construction remains supporting
evidence, separate from the external efficiency argument in the formal reference.
{\setcounter{tocdepth}{2}\footnotesize\tableofcontents}
\clearpage
\section{Historical concrete proof and execution components}\label{sec:ballot-sigma}
The interactive disjunctive proof is installed in VCVio's $\Sigma$-protocol
interface.
A statement is $(g,h,C)$; a witness is a bit $b$ and nonce $r$ with
$C=\mathsf{Enc}_{g,h}(r,b)$.
The commitment contains the four group elements
from the two branches.
The response is $(c_0,z_0,z_1)$; verification reconstructs
$c_1=c-c_0$ from the total challenge $c$.
Honest commitment and response
construction agrees exactly with the repaired strong-FS ballot constructor.
\companchor{BallotSigma}{ballotTranscript_strongProveVote}

\begin{theorem}[Completeness and special soundness]
Let $F$ be a field and $G$ an additive commutative group with an $F$-module
structure and decidable equality. Honest transcripts verify for every valid
statement and witness. Two accepting transcripts for the same statement and
commitment, with distinct total challenges, yield a bit and nonce satisfying
that statement's encryption relation.
\end{theorem}
The extractor selects a branch $i$ with $c_i\ne c'_i$ and returns
$(i,(z_i-z'_i)/(c_i-c'_i))$.
No discrete-log hardness assumption or generator
injectivity is required for this algebraic result.
The finite-field protocol
keeps three independent nonzero prover coins and a uniform full-field verifier
challenge.
\companchor{BallotSigma}{ballotSigma_complete}
\companchor{BallotSigma}{ballotSigma_speciallySound}

\begin{theorem}[Honest-verifier simulation, compressed interface]
Suppose additionally that $F$ is finite, with $q=|F|$. For every valid statement
and witness, the statistical distance between the actual interactive transcript
and \code{ballotFullSimTranscript} is at most $3/q$.
\end{theorem}
The witness-free simulator samples $c,c_0,z_0,z_1$ uniformly from $F$ and
reconstructs both branch commitments from the verification equations.
Full-field prover coins give exact transcript equality.
For the historical
sampler, replacing a zero uniform draw with an independent nonzero draw has
exactly the required nonzero marginal; three replacements give the bound.
\companchor{BallotSigmaSimulation}{ballotSigma_full_hvzk}
\companchor{NonzeroSimulationBridge}{nonzeroRefill_eq}
\companchor{NonzeroSimulationBridge}{ballotSigma_hvzk}

\paragraph{Retained counterexamples and boundary.}
Equal total challenges or changing commitments need not extract a witness.
Exact equality with this full-field simulator is false: for a zero vote, its
fake-branch challenge can be zero, whereas the actual transcript excludes zero.
Independent multiplicative fixtures check both extraction branches and exact
small-field transcript distributions.
\companchor{BallotSigmaControls}{BallotSigmaControls.same_challenge_does_not_extract}
\companchor{BallotSigmaSimulationControls}{nonzero_transcript_ne_full_simulator}
These interactive properties are prerequisites.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Random-oracle programming prerequisites}\label{sec:programming}
A strong ballot-proof hash binds the complete statement $x=(g,h,C)$ and four
commitment elements $P$.
Simulation must arrange that the oracle answer at
$(x,P)$ equals the sampled total challenge.
A prior query at that point can
prevent this programming step.
The following results bound this collision and
identify the required challenge distribution; Section~\ref{sec:adaptive-simulation} derives cache budgets and composes
these steps across adaptive requests.

\begin{theorem}[Simulator commitment and prior-query bounds]
Let $F$ be a finite field, $q=|F|$, and $G$ an $F$-module with decidable
equality. Fix a statement $x=(g,h,C)$ with injective $r\mapsto r\bullet g$.
For every commitment $P$ and finite set $Q$ of statement--commitment pairs,
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \Pr[T.\mathsf{commit}=P]&\le 1/q,\\
 \Pr[(x,T.\mathsf{commit})\in Q]&\le |Q|/q,
 \qquad T\leftarrow\mathsf{ballotFullSimTranscript}(x).
\end{aligned}
\]
\end{theorem}
A single branch commitment coordinate is an injective translate of a uniform
response scalar.
Equality of the whole commitment implies equality of that
coordinate.
Summing over the prior query domain gives the second bound;
queries containing other statements remain in that domain.
No valid-witness
premise is needed for these two bounds.
\companchor{BallotSigmaProgramming}{ballotSim_commit_probability_le}
\companchor{BallotSigmaProgramming}{ballotSim_query_collision_le}

\begin{theorem}[Conditional challenge uniformity, compressed interface]
For every statement admitting a bit/nonce witness, every $P$ and $c\in F$,
\[
 \Pr[T.\mathsf{commit}=P\land T.\mathsf{challenge}=c]
 =\Pr[T.\mathsf{commit}=P]\,/q.
\]
Here $T$ is the same full-field simulator transcript. Generator injectivity
is not required for this identity.
\end{theorem}
Exact full-field honest-verifier simulation reduces the identity to the real
interactive protocol, whose challenge is sampled independently after its
commitment.
This is VCVio's \code{simChalUniformGivenCommit} property for the
actual ballot interface.
The $3/q$ historical sampling loss remains separate.
\companchor{BallotSigmaProgramming}{ballotSigma_simChalUniformGivenCommit}

\paragraph{Why the premises are retained.}
With zero generator, key and ciphertext, every commitment coordinate is zero;
the commitment probability is one.
With $F=\mathbb Z_3$, $g=1$, $h=2$ and
$C=(1,1)$ in additive notation, no bit/nonce witness exists.
Conditioning its
simulator on four zero commitment elements forces challenge zero, refuting
unconditional challenge uniformity.
Both are checked counterexamples.
\companchor{BallotSigmaProgrammingControls}{BallotSigmaProgrammingControls.zero_generator_refutes_unconditional_bound}
\companchor{BallotSigmaProgrammingControls}{BallotSigmaProgrammingControls.nonbit_refutes_unconditional_challenge_uniformity}
These results concern each fixed prior query domain.
Adaptive multiple-proof simulation also needs reachable cache budgets and
composition.
The next subsections supply these steps. Extraction is a separate result.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: The explicit ballot hash oracle}\label{sec:ballot-oracle}
The ballot prover, verifier and any attacker computation can now query one
shared lazy random oracle.
Its input is the complete pair $((g,h,C),P)$.
On a miss, it samples a uniform field challenge and caches it; on a hit, it
returns the stored answer.
Uniform prover randomness is forwarded separately,
so reusing a hash answer does not reuse a prover coin.
\companchor{StrongBallotOracle}{runBallotOracle_query}

\paragraph{Agreement with the existing algorithms.}
\code{strongBallotProofOracle} draws the historical three nonzero proof coins,
constructs the branch commitments, queries the oracle and computes the two
responses.
Interpreting every query through any fixed typed hash function
recovers exactly the existing \code{strongProveVote} constructor with those
same draws.
Verification consumes the full original proof, including both
branch challenges, and agrees with its existing validity predicate.
\companchor{StrongBallotOracle}{strongBallotProofOracle_function}
\companchor{StrongBallotOracle}{strongBallotVerifyOracle_function}
For every choice of prover coins, honest proofs verify in their resulting
oracle state for every initial cache and valid bit/nonce witness; no
fresh-hash-input premise is needed.
\companchor{StrongBallotOracle}{runBallotOracle_honest_valid}

\paragraph{A stateful witness-free simulator.}
\code{strongBallotSimOracle} draws the full-field simulated transcript from the
previous subsection.
If its input is fresh, it stores the simulated total
challenge and returns the proof with a false internal collision flag.
If the input is
already cached, it preserves that answer and returns a true flag.
The proof
is still returned on a collision; no execution is conditioned away.

\begin{theorem}[Simulator validity and answer preservation, compressed interface]
For every initial cache, every unflagged simulator output verifies against its
returned cache. Every simulator output preserves all existing answers.
Every reachable state of an arbitrary adaptive ballot-oracle computation also
preserves all answers from its initial cache. Consequently a proof valid for
a cached challenge remains accepted after such a computation.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{StrongBallotSimulator}{strongBallotSimOracle_unflagged_valid}
\companchor{StrongBallotSimulator}{strongBallotSimOracle_cache_le}
\companchor{StrongBallotOracle}{runBallotOracle_verification_preserved}
For a finite set $Q$ covering all initial cached inputs, generator injectivity
gives collision-flag probability at most $|Q|/|F|$.
This fixed-domain result assumes the cache cover; the adaptive bounds are established in later component results.
\companchor{StrongBallotSimulator}{strongBallotSimOracle_bad_probability_le}

\paragraph{Controls and interface boundary.}
Independent modulo-23 commitments are $((3,4),(18,12))$.
A fresh simulation
verifies under challenge 5; a conflicting prior answer 6 is retained, flagged,
and rejects that proof.
A changed statement with the same commitments has a
different hash input.
The independent constructor/cache harness checks 22,000
cases and detects resetting the verifier's cache in each one.
\companchor{StrongBallotOracleControls}{StrongBallotOracleControls.conflicting_proof_rejected}
The next subsection compares real and simulated one-proof executions.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: One-proof programming distance}\label{sec:programming-distance}
Let $S=(g,h,C)$ be a ballot statement, $w=(b,r)$ a bit/nonce witness and
$K$ the initial cache.
Write $\mathsf{Real}(S,w,K)$ for the actual nonzero-coin
oracle prover, with a false internal collision flag appended to its output.
Write $\mathsf{Sim}(S,K)$ for the implemented full-field simulator.
Each
experiment returns the full proof, internal flag and final cache.
Flags and
cache are reduction state, not additional public election observations.

\begin{theorem}[One-step state-inclusive distance, compressed interface]
Let $F$ be a finite field, $G$ an additive $F$-module and $q=|F|$. Assume
$C=\mathsf{Enc}_{g,h}(r,b)$, the map $x\mapsto xg$ is injective, and $Q$ is a
finite set of complete strong-hash inputs covering all populated entries of
$K$. Then
\[
 \mathsf{TV}\bigl(\mathsf{Real}(S,w,K),\mathsf{Sim}(S,K)\bigr)
 \le \frac{3}{q}+\frac{|Q|}{q}.
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{StrongBallotProgrammingDistance}{strongBallot_programming_step_distance_le}

\paragraph{Proof dependencies.}
Replace the actual three nonzero prover draws by full-field draws in the same
stateful runtime; the checked sampler bound costs at most $3/q$.
Outside the
event that the commitment key is already cached, this full-field execution
agrees with the interactive transcript mapped through the simulator's cache
update.
Exact full-field honest-verifier simulation identifies that mapped
distribution with the implemented simulator.
The checked query-collision bound
charges at most $|Q|/q$.
The triangle inequality combines the losses.
Neither
collisions nor the sampler discrepancy are conditioned away.

\paragraph{Independent controls.}
For the literal $q=11$ statement, Lean checks an empty-cache distance bound of
$3/11$.
For a populated finite-domain cache, Lean checks that the simulator's
flag is always true and the actual prover's flag is always false, so their
state-inclusive distributions differ.
\companchor{StrongBallotProgrammingControls}{StrongBallotProgrammingControls.empty_cache_distance_le}
\companchor{StrongBallotProgrammingControls}{StrongBallotProgrammingControls.populated_distributions_differ}
An independent multiplicative enumeration compares exact distributions for
$q=3,5,11$, both bits and four initial cache regimes: 24 comparisons with zero
failures or discarded cases.
Empty caches expose the nonzero-sampling
loss.
Fully populated valid commitment domains give distance one; for $q>3$
they refute omitting the collision term.
These finite checks validate fixtures,
not the universal bound.

\paragraph{Composition.}
The next subsection derives cache covers from query-bounded executions and
composes the one-step result.
This one-step
statistical theorem does not establish computational ballot secrecy.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Adaptive honest-proof simulation}\label{sec:adaptive-simulation}
The runtime now interleaves uniform draws, arbitrary strong-hash queries and
internal honest-proof requests $(b,r)$.
Under fixed generator $g$ and key $h$,
a request constructs $S=(g,h,\mathsf{Enc}_{g,h}(r,b))$ and returns its proof.
The program may choose its next request from previous proofs and hash answers.
The real implementation runs the existing historical nonzero-coin constructor.
The simulated implementation programs fresh points and retains old answers on
collisions.
Its internal collision flag stays true once set.
Programs receive
only proofs and hash answers; the flag and cache remain internal state.
\companchor{AdaptiveBallotSimulation}{runBallotProofReal_request}
\companchor{AdaptiveBallotSimulation}{runBallotProofSim_flag_sticky}

\paragraph{Derived cache budgets.}
A cache has budget $k$ when some finite set of at most $k$ complete keys covers
its populated entries.
Every supported output of an adaptive ballot-oracle
computation with at most $n$ hash queries has cache budget $k+n$.
Uniform draws
cost zero.
A simulated proof adds at most one key, even on a collision.
The
induction constructs the covers; it does not assume them for successor states.
\companchor{BallotOracleBudget}{runBallotOracle_cache_bound}
\companchor{BallotOracleBudget}{strongBallot_programming_after_queries_le}

\begin{theorem}[Adaptive simulation, compressed interface]
Let $F$ be a finite field, $G$ an additive $F$-module, $q=|F|$, and
$x\mapsto xg$ injective. Fix a program in the request interface above with at
most $s$ proof requests and $n$ hash-or-proof requests on every response branch.
For every common initial state whose cache has budget $k$, the distance between
its real and simulated executions, including final cache and flag, satisfies
\[
 \mathsf{TV}\bigl(\mathsf{Run}_{\rm real},\mathsf{Run}_{\rm sim}\bigr)
 \le \frac{s(3+k+n)}{q}.
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{AdaptiveBallotSimulation}{strongBallot_programming_distance_le}
Witness validity follows from constructing each requested statement by
encryption.
Each proof costs at most $3/q+(k+n)/q$ by the one-step theorem and
reachable cache bound.
Raw hash queries use the same handler in both runs.
Induction over the program, conditioning on supported outputs, combines these
losses through VCVio's TV bind inequalities.
Collision executions remain in
both distributions, including after subsequent fresh requests.

\paragraph{Controls and boundary.}
Lean checks an adaptive two-proof example at $q=11$ with bound $10/11$, exact
equality for a hash-only program, and a fresh proof following a prior collision
whose flag cannot be reset.
Independent fresh commitments are
$((18,9),(8,6))$ modulo 23.
A cache with a known entry refutes budget zero.
\companchor{AdaptiveBallotControls}{AdaptiveBallotControls.adaptive_two_proofs_distance_le}
\companchor{AdaptiveBallotControls}{AdaptiveBallotControls.fresh_after_collision_keeps_flag}
The independent cache harness checks 132,860 adaptive traces with zero failures
or discarded cases.
It checks cache policy, not the statistical theorem or
cryptographic hardness.
Internal proof requests in this component model honest challenger calls. Extraction of accepted malicious ballots and the complete election reduction are separate results.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Replay extraction for selected live targets}\label{sec:ballot-fork}
Let a ballot-oracle program output an arbitrary statement $S$ and full proof
$\pi$.
The replay wrapper logs fresh hash queries with complete keys
$(S,\mathsf{pc}(\pi))$, including all four commitments.
Its verification flag
checks the original full proof against an existing live cache entry; missing
entries produce false.
Forgetting the miss log and Unit key tag gives exactly
the original lazy ballot-oracle runtime, for every adaptive program and every
initial cache.
\companchor{BallotFork}{ballotForkRunTrace_eq}
\companchor{BallotFork}{ballotFork_runtime_eq}

\paragraph{Derived replay correspondence.}
The selector returns the target's first miss-log position when verification
succeeds and the position is below $n+1$.
Every selected position corresponds
to a real sampled challenge.
Two successful contextual replays selecting it
have the same complete statement and commitments.
The challenges recovered
from the physical replay logs are those that accepted the original proofs.
These facts follow from VCVio's shared-prefix replay machinery, not from
an assumed equality of targets or numeric positions alone.
\companchor{BallotFork}{ballotFork_target_eq}
\companchor{BallotFork}{ballotFork_selected_challenge}

\begin{theorem}[Selected-target extraction]
Let $F$ be a finite field of size $q$ and $G$ an additive $F$-module.
Every successful output $(S,(b,r))$ of the implemented replay extractor
satisfies $S.\mathsf{ciphertext}=\mathsf{Enc}_{S.g,S.h}(r,b)$.
If $a$ is the probability that the trace selector returns a position, then
\[
 \Pr[\mathsf{extractor\ succeeds}]
 \ \ge\ a\left(\frac{a}{n+1}-\frac1q\right).
\]
Subtraction is truncated at zero, as in the Lean ENNReal statement.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BallotFork}{ballotFork_extract_valid}
\companchor{BallotFork}{ballotFork_extraction_probability_le}
The private Unit-relation adapter supplies the upstream trace interface only;
its unused interactive methods and relation provide no security evidence.
Extraction uses the actual ballot verification equations and special soundness.
Full-proof validity justifies response normalization, including both challenges.

\paragraph{Controls and correspondence boundary.}
A real one-query honest prover at $q=11$ has $a=1$ and a strictly positive
extraction bound.
Literal challenges 5 and 6 extract nonce 3.
Unrelated traces
with index zero have different targets; changing the full proof's second
challenge rejects, although unchecked normalization erases that rejection.
\companchor{BallotForkControls}{BallotForkControls.honest_extraction_probability_positive}
\companchor{BallotForkControls}{BallotForkControls.unchecked_normalization_loses_rejection}
The independent multiplicative harness checks 2,420 extraction pairs and
242 normalization defects with no failures or discarded cases.
This theorem's
$a$ is selected live-target probability, not all ballot acceptance.
The next subsection derives query coverage and includes unqueried acceptance.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: From the selector to actual verification}\label{sec:fork-acceptance}
The live-only wrapper above assigns false to targets absent from its cache.
An actual verifier queries that target and can accept a guessed challenge.
To preserve this behavior, $\mathsf{Complete}(A)$ runs the arbitrary raw
ballot-oracle program $A$, queries the returned proof's complete hash key, and
returns the same statement and full proof.
This is the verifier's existing
query; the proof is not changed or normalized.

\paragraph{Exact runtime correspondence.}
Let $\mathsf{VerifyGame}(A)$ run $A$ followed by the original full-proof
verifier.
Interpreting the completed trace's uniform
queries gives exactly the same Boolean computation as running this game in
the original lazy ballot oracle from the empty cache.
Cache hits keep their
answers; misses draw the same uniform challenge.
Full-proof verification
checks both original branch challenges.
\companchor{BallotForkAcceptance}{ballotFork_complete_runtime}

\paragraph{Derived query coverage.}
If $A$ makes at most $n$ hash queries on every response branch, completion
makes at most $n+1$.
Uniform draws do not consume this budget.
Every actual
miss-log entry comes from one such query, and every populated cache key belongs
to the log.
The cache/log invariant is proved from the empty initial runtime.
Consequently every completed verified target has a selector position below
$n+1$, including a fresh query made only by the verifier.
\companchor{BallotFork}{ballotFork_log_bound}
\companchor{BallotForkAcceptance}{ballotFork_selector_of_bound}
\companchor{BallotForkAcceptance}{ballotFork_acceptance_probability_eq}

\begin{theorem}[Extraction under actual verification acceptance]
For a finite field $F$ of size $q$, an additive $F$-module $G$ and any raw
ballot-oracle program $A$ with structural hash-query bound $n$, set
$a=\Pr[\mathsf{VerifyGame}(A)\text{ accepts}]$. Then
\[
 \Pr[\mathsf{Extract}(\mathsf{Complete}(A),n)\text{ succeeds}]
 \ \ge\ a\left(\frac{a}{n+1}-\frac1q\right).
\]
Subtraction is truncated at zero. Every successful output satisfies the actual
bit/nonce encryption relation by the preceding extraction theorem.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BallotForkAcceptance}{ballotFork_acceptance_extraction_le}
No premise says the attacker queried its final target.
The $1/q$ term remains;
no guessed-challenge run is discarded.

\paragraph{Controls and boundary.}
A fixed unqueried valid transcript at $q=11$ accepts with probability $1/11$.
Completion preserves this probability, while the old live-only selector gives
zero.
For an already queried honest prover, completion leaves the entire
trace computation unchanged.
\companchor{BallotForkControls}{BallotForkControls.completed_unqueried_probability_preserved}
\companchor{BallotForkControls}{BallotForkControls.queried_completion_preserves_trace}
Independent multiplicative enumeration checks 5,324 verifier-completion cases
and detects 242 omitted-query defects, with zero failures or discarded cases.
The theorem covers arbitrary statements and full proofs in the raw ballot
oracle.
The board's weeding/provenance correspondence, established separately, connects prior programmed honest proof points to accepted complete ballots.
Trustee publications, full election observations and the DDH/PPT reduction
remain outside this result.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Proof targets, weeding and honest rejection}\label{sec:proof-provenance}
Actual repaired submission acceptance entails proof validity and expanded
weeding.
Therefore each accepted component or aggregate statement differs from
every covered statement on the previous accepted board, regardless of either
proof's commitments.
Both component-to-aggregate directions are included.
When both honest prefix decisions accept, Lean derives the actual two board
entries from execution.
Generated honest bit/nonce statements agree with the
corresponding ballot components and aggregate.
\companchor{BallotProofProvenance}{repairedSubmit_accepted_target_ne}
\companchor{BallotProofProvenance}{repairedCastHonestPair_board_of_accepted}
\companchor{BallotProofProvenance}{strongHonestBallot_covered_statement}

\begin{theorem}[Generated-target match bound]
Fix an injective generator map $x\mapsto xg$ over a finite field of size $q$,
a public key and either swapped vote. Draw the existing honest coin pair.
Allow any probabilistic continuation to choose a static hash and submission,
even given those coins. Then
\[
 \Pr\left[\begin{array}{l}
   \text{the submission is accepted and one covered statement}\\
   \text{matches a generated honest component or aggregate statement}
 \end{array}\right]\ \le\ \frac{9}{q-1}.
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BallotProofProvenance}{accepted_honest_target_match_probability_le}
Such a match implies an honest-prefix rejection: if both decisions accepted,
weeding would exclude the matched statement.
Rejection in turn implies a
covered-nonce collision, bounded by the \href{main.pdf}{formal reference\textquotesingle s concrete repair section}.
The continuation above is an auxiliary upper-bound experiment.
It adds no
public coin disclosure to the protocol.
All rejection and continuation-failure
outcomes are retained; no target freshness premise is assumed.

\paragraph{Why rejection matters.}
At $p=23,q=11$, use Alice's nonces $(1,2)$ with vote 0 and Bob's nonces $(4,2)$
with vote 1.
Bob repeats Alice's second zero ciphertext and is rejected.
A subsequent valid submission with nonces $(4,5)$ and vote 1 is accepted on
the retained board and has exactly Bob's generated first proof target.
That target is absent from every covered statement on the accepted board.
Thus unconditional freshness against all generated targets is false; this
counterexample concerns the proof invariant, not a demonstrated secrecy attack.
\companchor{BallotProofProvenanceControls}{BallotProofProvenanceControls.rejected_honest_target_can_recur}
A fresh third ballot with nonces $(6,9)$ is accepted, while a component copying
Alice's aggregate is rejected.
Independent multiplicative enumeration checks
120,000 target/weeding cases, including 10,700 recurrences after honest rejection;
none violates the rejection/collision implication.

\paragraph{Connection to simulation and remaining scope.}
A simulation step changes only keys carrying its requested statement.
Hence
simulation for a prior accepted ballot preserves an accepted submission's
covered target entry; actual weeding supplies the statement inequality.
\companchor{StrongBallotSimulator}{strongBallotSimOracle_other_statement}
\companchor{BallotProofProvenance}{repairedSubmit_accepted_simulation_preserves_target}
The following subsections give programmed-cache and honest-prefix composition.
The static continuation bound and this local step alone do not establish that
correspondence.
The full reduction additionally composes honest rejection losses, extraction and trustee/election observations, as described in the formal reference.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Replayable programmed-cache interpretation}\label{sec:programmed-cache}
The existing adaptive simulator can now run through the raw ballot oracle used
by replay extraction.
Its internal state contains a shadow cache $C$, sticky
collision flag $b$, and list $L$ of statements from actual proof requests.
The live oracle has a separate cache $R$.
A shadow hit returns its old answer;
a shadow miss queries the live oracle and stores its answer.
Honest requests
lift the existing simulator's uniform computation and record their statement,
even when a collision is flagged.
The program sees the original answers and
proofs; the extra caches and list are internal reduction state.

\begin{theorem}[Exact adaptive runtime interpretation]
Start both caches empty, with flag false and list empty. For every adaptive
program in the existing ballot-hash/honest-proof request interface, projecting
away the extra metadata gives exactly the original simulated computation,
including its final shadow cache and sticky flag. Every supported result obeys
\[
 R\le C,\qquad S\notin L\ \Longrightarrow\ C(S,pc)=R(S,pc).
\]
Here $R\le C$ means every recorded live answer is preserved in the shadow.
The invariant is derived from initialization and all query successors.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BallotProgrammedOracle}{ballotProgrammed_runtime_eq}
\companchor{BallotProgrammedOracle}{ballotProgrammed_request_provenance}
This proof reuses VCVio's state-composition and invariant-restricted projection
lemmas.
It establishes computation equality, rather than assuming two cache
implementations realize the same distribution.

\paragraph{Verification and query cost.}
At any supported final state, the original full-proof verifier has the same
Boolean computation in both caches when its statement is outside $L$.
This retains both proof challenges and the original checksum check.
A source
bound of $n$ hash-or-proof requests gives at most $n$ live hash queries in the
lowered program.
Simulated proof requests use only uniform coins; this is a
conservative hash-query bound, not a computational efficiency theorem.
\companchor{BallotProgrammedOracle}{ballotProgrammed_verify_agreement}
\companchor{BallotProgrammedOracle}{ballotProgrammed_query_bound}

\paragraph{Controls and correspondence boundary.}
The $p=23,q=11$ fixtures retain a fresh programmed-only answer, a conflicting
live answer, and a sticky flag after later fresh programming.
The full verifier
can disagree at a programmed statement, so dropping statement exclusion is
false.
Erasing the request list or starting with inconsistent caches breaks
the invariant.
Independent enumeration checks 22,621 adaptive dual-cache trace
prefixes, detects reset/overwrite defects, and reports no failures or gaveUp.
This gate checks cache policy, not cryptographic security.
\companchor{BallotProgrammedControls}{BallotProgrammedControls.programmed_target_can_disagree}
The next subsection relates the sampled honest-ballot program to the historical
constructor; Section~\ref{sec:board-oracle} composes actual board decisions.
Section~\ref{sec:prefix-rejection} bounds rejected prefixes in that execution.
Section~\ref{sec:submission-extraction} composes selected-proof replay with the
post-prefix attacker.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Sampled honest ballots and actual requests}\label{sec:honest-oracle}
The honest-ballot program now samples the original nonzero nonce pair and makes
three proof requests: the first candidate component, the second zero component,
and the implicit aggregate with the sum of their nonces.
It returns the original
two ciphertexts, full component proofs and aggregate proof.
With explicit coins, interpreting the hash by any static function exactly
recovers the existing historical ballot constructor.
\companchor{HonestBallotOracle}{strongHonestBallotWithCoinsOracle_function}

\begin{theorem}[Historical sampling correspondence]
For a finite field, fixed generator and public key, either honest vote, any
initial live cache and any initial flag, real execution of the sampled request
program has the same distribution as drawing the original historical coin
record and running the explicit-coin ballot oracle constructor. Both sides
include the final cache and unchanged flag.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{HonestBallotOracle}{strongHonestBallotOracle_real_eq}
The historical sampler groups witness, challenge and response triples before
drawing the nonces.
The request program draws nonces first, then coins proof by
proof.
Lean proves the independent-draw permutation, including the transpose of
the nine proof coins.
This gives distributional equality; the differently
ordered computation trees are not asserted equal.
No draw is discarded and no
retry removes a nonce collision.
Real request lowering also has an exact
runtime equality for arbitrary request programs.
\companchor{HonestBallotOracle}{drawHonestCoinsByProof_eq}
\companchor{HonestBallotOracle}{ballotRealRequest_runtime_eq}

\paragraph{Compositional request provenance.}
Every supported simulated execution prepends exactly the returned ballot's
aggregate and two component statements to the prior request list, in reverse
request order.
This holds with prior cached answers and metadata; the list
is derived from execution.
It can therefore describe the next honest ballot.
Cache consistency from empty initialization follows from the separate adapter invariant in Section~\ref{sec:programmed-cache}.
\companchor{HonestBallotOracle}{strongHonestBallotOracle_request_targets}
The program has a derived three-request budget.
For an injective generator and
initial cache budget $k$, its full output/cache/flag distributions satisfy
\[
 \operatorname{TV}(\mathrm{RealBallot},\mathrm{SimBallot})
 \ \le\ \frac{3(6+k)}{q}.
\]
\companchor{HonestBallotOracle}{strongHonestBallotOracle_simulation_le}

\paragraph{Controls and scope.}
At $q=11$, nonces $(1,10)$ are supported by the original nonzero sampler and
sum to zero.
The resulting honest abstention ballot has a valid aggregate proof
for the identity ciphertext.
Controls reject substituting the first nonce for
the sum and leaving proof coins in the wrong order.
Independent multiplicative
checks cover 2,048 permutations and 4,096 ballots over labelled draws
$\{1,2\}$, with no failures or gaveUp; they detect both injected defects.
The next subsection composes actual verification and the two honest decisions,
retaining rejection and public fields.
This single-ballot correspondence does
not prove adaptive malicious-ballot extraction, election privacy or efficiency.


\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Actual oracle submissions and the honest prefix}\label{sec:board-oracle}
The ballot verifier now queries and checks the original full proofs in order:
component 0, component 1, then aggregate, stopping on failure.
Submission returns
\code{invalidProof} before considering ciphertext reuse; a valid reused ballot
returns \code{reusedCiphertext}.
Both rejections retain the board.
A valid fresh
ballot is appended with its voter identity.
Interpreting the oracle by any fixed
hash function recovers the existing repaired submission exactly.
\companchor{RepairedBoardOracle}{repairedSubmitOracle_function}
Actual successful lazy verification supplies stored challenges and full validity
for all three unchanged proofs.
Honest construction derives this cached
validity even when proof targets collide; no caller validity premise is needed.
\companchor{RepairedBoardOracle}{strongBallotVerifyAllOracle_true_cached}
\companchor{RepairedBoardOracle}{strongHonestBallotOracle_real_cached}

\begin{theorem}[Historical honest-prefix correspondence]
For either vote and any initial cache and flag, real execution of the two
sampled honest voters and their submissions has the same distribution as
sampling the original honest coin pair and running the explicit-coin oracle
prefix. Decisions, returned board, final cache and flag are included.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{HonestPrefixOracle}{repairedCastHonestPairOracle_real_eq}

\begin{theorem}[Derived exclusion after actual acceptance]
Run the sampled honest prefix through the programmed adapter from its empty
initial state. Suppose both actual honest decisions accept. Every recorded
statement then belongs to a covered ciphertext of a ballot on the returned
board. After any raw attacker computation and its actual accepted submission,
none of the submitted ballot's three covered statements is in the recorded
list. Freshness and board membership are derived from these executions.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{HonestPrefixOracle}{repairedCastHonestPairOracle_programmed_targets_of_accepted}
\companchor{HonestPrefixOracle}{repairedHonestPrefix_accepted_target_exclusion}
Raw attacker and verification queries preserve the list.
This discharges
statement exclusion on the accepted-prefix event.
It does not remove the
honest-acceptance premise: a rejected honest target can recur later.

\paragraph{Controls and scope.}
Preloaded-cache fixtures retain fresh acceptance, valid cross-kind reuse,
invalid-proof priority and aggregate-only failure; no empty-cache reachability
is claimed by those fixtures.
Independent full-proof checks cover all 16
validity/freshness cases, detecting seven priority defects and two omitted
aggregate checks, with no failures or gaveUp.
Static-prefix controls retain both
honest acceptance and rejection.
The next subsection bounds actual prefix
rejection and its simulation loss.


\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Actual prefix rejection and simulation loss}\label{sec:prefix-rejection}
The accepted-case exclusion theorem retains an honest-acceptance premise.
A rejected honest target can recur, so that premise cannot simply be removed.
The rejected event is now bounded in the same actual oracle execution.
Every returned covered first coordinate is the generator multiplied by its
original nonce, including the aggregate sum.
Full honest proofs validate against
their stored answers even if targets collide.
Thus expanded nonce separation
forces both actual submissions to accept, for any initial cache.
\companchor{HonestPrefixRejection}{repairedCastHonestPairWithCoinsOracle_accepts_of_collisionFree}

\begin{theorem}[Real honest-prefix rejection]
Fix an injective generator over a finite field of size $q$, any public key,
either vote and any initial cache and flag. In the actual real sampled prefix,
\[
 \Pr[\text{the two honest decisions are not both accepted}]
 \ \le\ \frac{9}{q-1}.
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{HonestPrefixRejection}{repairedCastHonestPairOracle_real_rejection_le}
The proof uses historical sampling correspondence and the existing nonce
collision bound.
It derives validity and freshness; neither is a caller premise.
No rejection, zero aggregate nonce, cache state or flag outcome is discarded.

\paragraph{Derived request cost and simulated rejection.}
The source prefix makes six honest proof requests and at most six verification
hash queries.
Nonce draws consume no hash/proof budget.
From initial cache
budget $k$, the already checked adaptive simulation gives
\[
 \operatorname{TV}(\mathrm{RealPrefix},\mathrm{SimPrefix})
 \ \le\ \frac{6(15+k)}{q},
\]
including final values, cache and sticky flag.
The query budget is derived from
the actual short-circuit program; it is not a PPT theorem.
\companchor{HonestPrefixRejection}{repairedCastHonestPairOracle_query_bounds}
\companchor{HonestPrefixRejection}{repairedCastHonestPairOracle_simulation_le}
Consequently the actual replayable programmed prefix, from empty initialization,
has rejection probability at most $9/(q-1)+90/q$.
Its exact runtime correspondence
transfers the simulated bound.
Both nonzero-sampling and programming-collision
losses remain; these upper bounds may exceed one for small fields.
\companchor{HonestPrefixRejection}{repairedCastHonestPairOracle_programmed_rejection_le}

\paragraph{Controls and composition boundary.}
At $q=101$, real rejection is at most $9/100$ and programmed rejection at most
$9909/10100<1$.
Lean proves positive probability of an accepted programmed
prefix.
Conversely, a zero generator causes certain real rejection, refuting
the claim with injectivity omitted; this is an invalid-parameter control.
\companchor{HonestPrefixRejectionControls}{HonestPrefixRejectionControls.programmed_acceptance_positive}
\companchor{HonestPrefixRejectionControls}{HonestPrefixRejectionControls.zero_generator_rejection_certain}
Independent multiplicative checks exhaust 40,000 nonzero-nonce/world/answer-policy
cases with fixed proof coins.
They retain 11,200 rejections and 7,600 zero-aggregate
prefixes, with no failures or gaveUp.
The next subsection composes this split
with a post-prefix attacker, full-proof verifier and selected-proof replay.


\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Selected-proof extraction after actual submission}\label{sec:submission-extraction}
The source program now runs the sampled honest prefix, gives its actual public
decisions and retained board to a raw oracle attacker, and executes the original
submission.
It returns that view, the submitted ballot, decision and final board.
This component starts empty and places attacker queries after the honest prefix. It does not model earlier queries or the complete election interface.

\paragraph{Derived full verification and target selection.}
When both honest decisions and the attacker submission accept, every covered
proof validates in the live cache with its original challenges and commitments.
Actual intermediate execution supplies cache consistency; submission supplies
full cached validity; accepted-target exclusion supplies live/shadow agreement.
No cache invariant or freshness premise is left to this theorem's caller.
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionOracle}{repairedSubmissionOracle_accepted_live}
For a fixed component or aggregate index, the replay target retains that full
proof on joint acceptance.
Otherwise it returns an internal always-invalid
proof.
The public protocol result is unchanged, and even the invalid proof's
hash query remains.
Actual live verifier acceptance is exactly joint acceptance;
a valid fallback on rejected executions would falsely inflate this quantity.
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionExtraction}{repairedSubmissionTargetOracle_acceptance_eq}

\begin{theorem}[Composed selected-proof replay bound]\label{thm:selected-replay-bound}
Fix an injective generator over a finite field of size $q$. Let every attacker
continuation have raw hash-query bound $n$, and let $B$ be its actual submission
acceptance probability in this programmed execution. Put
\[
 \delta=\frac{9}{q-1}+\frac{90}{q},\qquad a=(B-\delta)_+.
\]
For any fixed covered index, the composed extractor satisfies
\[
 a\left(\frac{a}{n+16}-\frac1q\right)_+
 \ \le\ \Pr[\text{extractor returns a witness}].
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionExtraction}{repairedSubmission_extract_le}
The source and lowered query bound is $n+15$; verifier completion adds one.
The sharper bound uses joint prefix/submission acceptance instead of $a$.
The proof composes the existing replay theorem with derived live verification,
query coverage and the actual rejection bound.
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionExtraction}{repairedSubmissionTargetOracle_query_bound}
A successful witness satisfies the actual bit/nonce encryption relation and is
for the selected ciphertext of some supported accepting source submission.
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionExtraction}{repairedSubmission_extract_origin}

\paragraph{Controls and scope.}
Independent programmed-prefix/attacker fixtures check 20,000 cases: 2,720 joint
acceptances and 2,840 valid submissions excluded after honest rejection.
Lean
controls retain unchanged accepted proofs, the invalid guard and its real hash
query; a valid unguarded proof demonstrates the inflation defect.
This is not
an exhaustive replay-probability test or a computational efficiency proof.
The source-origin conclusion here is existential.
Section~\ref{sec:source-retention}
now recovers the first complete submission from the existing rich fork path.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Retaining the first complete submission}\label{sec:source-retention}
The statement/witness projection loses the complete source ballot.
That loss
can be repaired using the pinned libraries' existing data: VCVio's
\code{contextForkWitness} retains both typed execution paths, and PolyFun's
\code{Path.pullMap} recovers a path before pure output projection.
No new trace
format or cryptographic target is needed.

\paragraph{Exact projection and source recovery.}
Run the original source, append the existing completion query, and retain its
source value.
Mapping its logged result to the old trace gives exactly the old
query tree.
Pulling the rich fork's first path through that map recovers the
source value from the first execution.
The original full proof and its target
are the ones in that first trace; choosing any supported source with the same
ciphertext is insufficient.
\companchor{BallotForkSource}{ballotForkSource_trace_eq}
\companchor{BallotForkSource}{ballotForkSourceFirst_trace}
The source-retaining extractor maps the existing rich witness to this source
value and the existing extracted bit/nonce witness.
Dropping the source gives
exactly the previous extractor, including its failure outcomes.
\companchor{BallotForkSource}{ballotForkSourceExtract_project}

\begin{theorem}[First-submission extraction]
At the scope and under the assumptions of Section~\ref{sec:submission-extraction},
let $E_{\rm src}$ return the first complete submission alongside the extracted
statement and witness, and let $E$ be the previous extractor. Then
\[
 \operatorname{map}(\operatorname{dropSource}, E_{\rm src})=E,
 \qquad
 \Pr[E_{\rm src}\ne\mathsf{none}]=\Pr[E\ne\mathsf{none}].
\]
Every supported $\mathsf{some}(B,S,w)$ has $B$ accepted, $S$ equal to the
selected covered statement of $B$'s submitted ballot, and $w$ satisfying
that statement's actual bit/nonce encryption relation. The returned $B$ is
from the first source execution retained by this fork.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionSource}{repairedSubmission_extractSource_project}
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionSource}{repairedSubmission_extractSource_valid}
Consequently Theorem~\ref{thm:selected-replay-bound}'s bound holds without additional loss.
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionSource}{repairedSubmission_extractSource_le}

\paragraph{Controls and composition boundary.}
A tagged honest prover has positive extraction probability.
Its recovered tag
follows the first trace's full proof.
Valid same-target transcripts at challenges
5 and 6 can have different tags, refuting equality of replay source values.
\companchor{BallotForkControls}{BallotForkControls.tagged_first_value}
\companchor{BallotForkControls}{BallotForkControls.replay_can_change_source_tag}
Independent multiplicative fixtures check 2,420 replay pairs and detect 440
wrong-second-tag cases; prior full-proof and completion controls remain.
Section~\ref{sec:joint-replay} constructs the three-witness algorithm for one
original ballot.
Its joint success and efficiency argument is outside this component's guarantee; separate
selected-proof bounds do not establish it.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Joint-witness contract and the scalar modulus}\label{sec:joint-witness-contract}
Joint extraction returns witnesses for both components and the
implicit aggregate of the same ballot on success.
The algebraic consumer of those
witnesses is now checked.
It uses the original encryption and aggregate
operations; it does not assume that accepted proofs already reveal witnesses.

\begin{theorem}[Consistency of three covered witnesses]
Let $w_0=(b_0,r_0)$ and $w_1=(b_1,r_1)$ witness the two component ciphertexts,
and let $w_a=(b_a,r_a)$ witness their actual aggregate. If
$r\mapsto r\mathbin{\bullet}g$ is injective, then
\[
 r_a=r_0+r_1,\qquad [b_a]_F=[b_0]_F+[b_1]_F.
\]
If $2\ne0$ in $F$, the corresponding integer bit counts satisfy
\[
 [b_0]_{\mathbb N}+[b_1]_{\mathbb N}=[b_a]_{\mathbb N}\le1.
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BallotWitnessConsistency}{Ballot.covered_witnesses_sum}
\companchor{BallotWitnessConsistency}{Ballot.covered_witnesses_atMostOne}
The first ciphertext coordinate identifies the sum of nonces.
Cancelling its
public-key contribution in the second coordinate identifies the sum of scalar
plaintexts.
Checking the possible bits then gives the integer count, with
abstention retained.
This algebra needs no secret key or hardness assumption.
For the historical scalar field $\mathbb Z_q$ with prime $q>2$, the nonzero-two
condition is derived from the modulus.
\companchor{BallotWitnessConsistency}{Ballot.covered_witnesses_atMostOne_prime}

\paragraph{Checked parameter counterexample.}
A field-size condition $|F|>2$ alone is insufficient.
Mathlib's
\code{GaloisField 2 2} has four elements, and generator 1 has an injective scalar
action, but $1+1=0$.
Two component votes of one can therefore have aggregate bit
zero.
The control constructs all three original strong proofs, proves their
full validity and retains the three encryption witnesses.
The general
characteristic-two construction also passes original submission on an empty
board.
\companchor{BallotWitnessConsistencyControls}{BallotWitnessConsistencyControls.four_element_counterexample}
\companchor{BallotWitnessConsistencyControls}{BallotWitnessConsistencyControls.wrapped_submission_accepts}
This refutes an overgeneralization of the abstract field model.
It is not an
attack on the documented large prime-order protocol parameters, and it changes
no verification, hash or sampling rule.

\paragraph{Controls and extraction boundary.}
Honest and abstention witnesses remain checked.
Independent multiplicative
fixtures compare 10,648 aggregate candidates, find 363 consistent witness triples
and detect 121 double-vote cases if the aggregate constraint is omitted.
An independent four-element additive model retains 16 wrapped double votes.
These finite controls check the algebraic contract, not replay probabilities.
The algorithm in Section~\ref{sec:joint-replay} derives these witnesses on
success; its quantitative bound follows below. This algebraic result does not establish reduction efficiency.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Joint replay from one original submission}\label{sec:joint-replay}
The joint extractor samples one original execution, replays the selected query
for each component and the aggregate, and retains the original complete
submission.
It returns witnesses only if all three conditional attempts succeed;
every failure remains an explicit $\mathsf{none}$ outcome.

\begin{theorem}[Joint extraction output contract]
For the actual programmed honest prefix, subsequent raw attacker and original
submission, let $J$ be \code{repairedSubmission_joint_extract}. If $g\ne0$,
every supported $\mathsf{some}(B,w)$ output has $B$ accepted and
\[
 \forall i\in\{0,1,a\},\quad
 \mathsf{coveredStatement}(B,i).\mathsf{Witnesses}(w_i).
\]
The returned $B$ occurs in supported original programmed execution. If scalar
action on $g$ is injective and $2\ne0$ in $F$, these derived witnesses satisfy
\[
 r_a=r_0+r_1,\qquad [b_0]_{\mathbb N}+[b_1]_{\mathbb N}=[b_a]_{\mathbb N}\le1.
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionJoint}{repairedSubmission_joint_extract_valid}
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionJoint}{repairedSubmission_joint_extract_consistent}
\paragraph{Actual accepting paths have all three locations.}
Let $A(p)$ be joint acceptance on original path $p$.
With nonzero generator and
attacker hash bound $n$, each bounded selector satisfies
$\mathsf{Sel}_i(p).\mathsf{isSome}=\mathbf1_{A(p)}$.
The exact original live cache, actual acceptance theorem and source query bound
supply the cache/log evidence.
Each selected ordinal has a physical occurrence
on its projected original path.
No per-selection completion is appended.
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionCoverage}{repairedSubmissionPath_selector}
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionCoverage}{repairedSubmissionPath_locations}
Thus simultaneous original selection has probability exactly $\Pr[A]$, including
both honest decisions and original attacker submission.
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionCoverage}{repairedSubmissionPath_selection_probability}

\paragraph{Connection to the existing fork.}
VCVio's rich fork factors into a first path and conditional completion.
Pure
projection retains the source and answers; averaging one conditional attempt
gives exactly the raw selected-proof extractor.
\companchor{BallotReplayPath}{ballotReplayAtPath_bind}
\companchor{BallotReplaySource}{ballotReplaySourceAttempt_bind}

\paragraph{Controls and the quantitative argument.}
A repeated honest target has positive joint replay probability, with its tag tied
to the original proof even when a second completion changes it.
\companchor{BallotJointReplayControls}{BallotJointReplayControls.tagged_joint_positive}
Independent fixtures retain 31,944 three-proof replay cases and add 60,000
original selector checks, including 8,160 enabled selections and detected
omitted-miss-target defects.
These finite controls are not an attacker bound.
Separately positive marginals can have disjoint success sets.
\companchor{BallotJointReplayControls}{BallotJointReplayControls.disjoint_marginals_no_shared_success}
Enabling all original selectors is not successful replay of all proofs.
The
common-path success bound follows in Section~\ref{sec:joint-replay-bound}.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Quantitative joint replay}\label{sec:joint-replay-bound}
The bound uses one shared original path.
Threshold $\delta$ is an analysis parameter only.

\begin{theorem}[Joint replay with retained rejection loss]
Let $F$ be a finite field of cardinality $q$, with the uniform challenge oracle,
and let scalar action $r\mapsto r\cdot g$ be injective. Fix the public key and
honest vote. Let the subsequent raw attacker make at most $n$ hash queries for
every honest-prefix view. In the actual programmed prefix and original
submission, write $B$ for attacker-submission acceptance probability and
$\alpha$ for joint acceptance including both honest decisions. Set
\[
 Q=n+16,\qquad \ell=\frac9{q-1}+\frac{90}q,\qquad a=(B-\ell)_+.
\]
For every $\delta\in\mathbb R_{\ge0}\cup\{\infty\}$, the actual joint extractor $J$
satisfies, with all subtraction truncated at zero,
\[
 (a-3Q\delta)_+(\delta-1/q)_+^3
 \ \le\ (\alpha-3Q\delta)_+(\delta-1/q)_+^3
 \ \le\ \Pr[J.\mathsf{isSome}].
\]
This is a finite probabilistic statement for the two-candidate, post-prefix
submission interface.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionJointBound}{repairedSubmission_joint_extract_accepted_le}
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionJointBound}{repairedSubmission_joint_acceptance_loss}
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionJointBound}{repairedSubmission_joint_extract_le}

\paragraph{Conditional mass and reconstruction.}
At a reachable ordinal, context mass is the probability that its completion
selects it again.
Exact split/completion reconstruction bounds originally selected
contexts of mass at most $\delta$ by probability $\delta$ per ordinal.
The union
over three selections and $Q$ ordinals costs at most $3Q\delta$.
\companchor{BallotReplayContext}{ballotReplay_bad_context_fixed_le}
\companchor{BallotJointReplayBound}{ballotJointReplay_bad_context_le}
A uniform focused answer equals the original challenge with probability at most
$1/q$.
On each good original path, the actual three conditional binds therefore
give success at least $(\delta-1/q)_+^3$.
Averaging gives the displayed bound.
\companchor{BallotReplayCollision}{ballotReplayAtPath_mass_le}
\companchor{BallotJointReplayBound}{ballotJointReplay_selection_le}

\paragraph{Controls and scope.}
A pure selected output with no query refutes dropping occurrence reachability.
For the honest tagged control with $q=11$, $Q=2$ and $\delta=1/8$, the lower bound
is $27/2725888>0$; this control repeats one target three times.
\companchor{BallotReplayContextControls}{BallotReplayContextControls.missing_occurrence_refutes_bound}
\companchor{BallotReplayContextControls}{BallotReplayContextControls.tagged_joint_quantitative}
\paragraph{Oracle-interaction cost.}
With canonical finite scalar sampling and at most $m$ total attacker interactions
per honest-prefix view, the actual joint bound is $16(m+19)$: at most $m+19$
source requests, four interactions per handler and four runs for shared replay.
Uniform draws and failures are included.
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionCost}{repairedSubmission_joint_extract_canonical_total_bound}
Uniformity alone does not fix sampling cost: a checked padded sampler refutes
one-query accounting.
Pure local functions remain uncharged by this metric.
\companchor{RepairedSubmissionCostControls}{RepairedSubmissionCostControls.uniform_sampler_cost_counterexample}
This interaction bound does not include executable local computation or encoded sizes.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Finite live-cache implementation}\label{sec:finite-cache}
The proof-facing cache is a function from full statement/commitment keys to
optional scalar answers.
The implementation uses Mathlib's association list
\code{AList}, whose entries have unique keys.
Lookup interprets this finite
list as the original cache; insertion implements the same semantic update.
\companchor{BallotFiniteCache}{BallotFiniteCache.denote_insert}

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite-cache correspondence]
Use the same scalar sampler and decidable full-key equality in both interpreters.
Let $P$ be any raw ballot-oracle program and $C$ any finite initial cache. With
$D(C)$ denoting its lookup function, the finite interpreter satisfies
\[
 \mathsf{map}\bigl((x,C')\mapsto(x,D(C'))\bigr)
       (\mathsf{runFinite}(P,C))
 = \mathsf{runBallotOracle}(P,D(C)).
\]
Equality is of oracle computations, retaining the output, explicit random draws
and final cache. If $P$ makes at most $n$ hash queries on every branch, every
supported finite output $(x,C')$ satisfies $|C'|\le |C|+n$.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BallotFiniteCache}{runBallotFiniteCache_eq}
\companchor{BallotFiniteCache}{runBallotFiniteCache_length_le}

\paragraph{Connection to the actual repaired source.}
Applying the finite interpreter to the actual repaired source from an empty
cache preserves the complete original submission, all decisions and its final
live cache.
The actual source query bound gives at most $n+15$ live entries from
attacker hash bound $n$ per honest-prefix view.
\companchor{RepairedFiniteCache}{repairedSubmissionFiniteCache_eq}
\companchor{RepairedFiniteCache}{repairedSubmissionFiniteCache_length_le}
A hit returns the old answer without drawing; a miss samples and stores one answer.
Repeated queries and new entries preserve earlier answers.
Equal commitments
with different full statements remain distinct keys.
\companchor{BallotFiniteCacheControls}{BallotFiniteCacheControls.hit_preserves_runtime}
\companchor{BallotFiniteCacheControls}{BallotFiniteCacheControls.miss_samples_and_stores}
\companchor{BallotFiniteCacheControls}{BallotFiniteCacheControls.full_key_separation}

\paragraph{Representation and backend boundary.}
The following subsections supply the finite shadow cache and saved replay tape.
Continuation execution and encoded costs are outside this component's guarantee.
List length is not encoded bit length
or lookup time; key equality, group operations and extraction remain uncharged.
The pinned optional VCVio complexity backend provides small exact machine
certificates but no general oracle compiler or adequacy theorem.
Its ElGamal
example also stops at structural interaction accounting.
Those artifacts do not
establish standard-machine PPT for this extractor.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Finite programmed shadow cache}\label{sec:finite-programmed}
The shadow state is $s=(C,b,L)$: a finite association list, sticky collision flag
and ordered list of requested statements.
Let $D(s)=(D(C),b,L)$ interpret only
its cache as a lookup function.
The finite handler uses the existing four-draw
ballot transcript sampler.
On an occupied programming key it retains the old
answer and sets $b$ to true, even for an agreeing challenge.
On a fresh key it
inserts the sampled challenge and retains $b$.
Every proof request prepends its
statement to $L$, including duplicates and flagged requests.

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite shadow and complete runtime correspondence]
Let $F$ be a field with the existing scalar sampler and $G$ an additive
commutative group with an $F$-module structure and decidable equality. For any
$g,pk\in G$, proof-oracle program $P$ and finite initial shadow state $s$,
\[
 \mathsf{map}\bigl((x,s')\mapsto(x,D(s'))\bigr)
   (\mathsf{runFiniteShadow}_{g,pk}(P,s))
 =\mathsf{runShadow}_{g,pk}(P,D(s)).
\]
Composing with the finite live interpreter, from empty initialization,
\[
 \mathsf{map}\bigl(((x,s),R)\mapsto((x,D(s)),D(R))\bigr)
    (\mathsf{runBothFinite}_{g,pk}(P))
 =\mathsf{runProgrammed}_{g,pk}(P).
\]
Both are equalities of oracle computations. They preserve the actual output,
random draws, both caches, flag and exact history; no caller supplies a
reachability or cache-consistency premise.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BallotFiniteProgrammed}{runBallotFiniteProgrammed_eq}
\companchor{BallotFiniteProgrammed}{runBallotFiniteProgrammed_runtime}

\paragraph{Actual source and controls.}
For the repaired submission source (finite $F$, decidable scalar equality),
projecting away internal state gives exactly the existing raw source.
Its
replay and probability results therefore apply to this finite representation.
The full repaired runtime retains all decisions and boards, and the live
storage bound remains $n+15$ from attacker hash bound $n$ per prefix view.
\companchor{RepairedFiniteProgrammed}{repairedSubmissionFiniteSource_eq}
\companchor{RepairedFiniteProgrammed}{repairedSubmissionFiniteProgrammed_eq}
\companchor{RepairedFiniteProgrammed}{repairedSubmissionFiniteSource_length_le}
Literal controls retain conflicting and agreeing collisions, the returned
proof's sampled challenge, duplicate history and sticky failure after a fresh
request.
A raw hit returns the cached answer without drawing or changing state.
\companchor{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls}{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls.conflict_retains_old_answer}
\companchor{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls}{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls.agreeing_hit_flagged}
\companchor{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls}{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls.fresh_after_bad_sticky}
\companchor{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls}{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls.raw_hit_preserves_runtime}

\paragraph{Implementation boundary.}
The next subsection supplies finite saved replay input and reconstructs the
existing typed continuations.
Finite caches alone do not supply continuation
execution, bit encodings, local-operation costs or a machine-adequacy theorem.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Finite saved replay tapes}\label{sec:replay-tape}
PolyFun's existing trace carrier is a finite list of tagged operation answers,
$T=\operatorname{List}(\Sigma t,\operatorname{Answer}(t))$.
The saved input contains
no continuation function.
The decoder traverses the source program, checks each
tag before transporting its answer, and requires an empty tape at termination.
The carrier already enforces answer types; byte decoding is a separate boundary.

\begin{theorem}[Exact saved-path reconstruction and joint replay]
For a free oracle program $P$ with decidable operation-tag equality, every typed
path $p$ and typed tape $T$ satisfy
\[
 \mathsf{read}(P,\mathsf{trace}(P,p))=\mathsf{some}(p),\qquad
 \mathsf{read}(P,T)=\mathsf{some}(p)\ \Longrightarrow\ \mathsf{trace}(P,p)=T.
\]
Direct tape collection equals the existing path-producing execution followed by
trace erasure. For every raw ballot source $P$, selection function $s$ and hash
budget parameter $n$, using the existing scalar sampler and key equality,
\[
 \mathsf{jointReplayTape}(P,s,n)=\mathsf{jointReplay}(P,s,n).
\]
This is equality of oracle computations, retaining the original result, all
three attempts, selected physical occurrences, rejection and randomness. No
caller supplies tape validity, path correspondence or a probability premise.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BallotReplayTape}{ballotReplayReadTape_trace}
\companchor{BallotReplayTape}{ballotReplayReadTape_exact}
\companchor{BallotReplayTape}{ballotReplayCollectTape_eq}
\companchor{BallotJointReplayTape}{ballotJointReplayTape_eq}

\paragraph{Actual repaired source and storage.}
The repaired tape extractor uses the finite shadow source and equals the existing
joint extractor exactly.
Its witness and probability theorems transfer without
added loss.
Every saved tape has at most $4(m+19)$ entries when the scalar sampler
uses one primitive query and the attacker uses at most $m$ total queries per
prefix view.
Canonical finite scalar sampling retains the existing $16(m+19)$
interaction bound.
Entries are not encoded bits; local decoding remains uncharged.
\companchor{RepairedReplayTape}{repairedSubmissionTape_extract_eq}
\companchor{RepairedReplayTape}{repairedSubmissionTape_saved_length_le}
\companchor{RepairedReplayTape}{repairedSubmissionTape_canonical_total_bound}
\companchor{BallotReplayTape}{ballotReplayCollectTape_length_le}

\paragraph{Controls and implementation boundary.}
Literal controls reject mismatched tags, truncation, trailing events, reordering
and dropped uniform-labelled answers.
Decoding and locating the second repeated
hash tag preserves its two-entry prefix, including the intervening other query.
\companchor{BallotReplayTapeControls}{BallotReplayTapeControls.rejects_truncation_and_trailing}
\companchor{BallotReplayTapeControls}{BallotReplayTapeControls.physical_second_occurrence}
Reconstruction still traverses the source \code{OracleComp} and produces the
existing typed continuations.
The next subsection integrates finite logging in
all four runs, preserving the ordered miss log.
This replay interface does not establish bit encodings, local execution costs or machine adequacy.
Saving an entire branching computation tree would not follow from a
polynomial path-length bound.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Finite logging in every replay run}\label{sec:finite-logging}
The finite logging state is $(C,L)$: the existing association-list cache and the
chronological list of full-key misses.
Projection $D(C,L)$ interprets cache lookup
and leaves $L$ unchanged.
A uniform query forwards without state changes.
A hash
hit returns the old answer without drawing or logging.
A miss draws one wrapped
challenge, inserts the answer and appends the full key to $L$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite logging and complete replay]
Use the same decidable full-key equality. For every raw ballot source $P$ and
finite initial state $(C,L)$,
\[
 \mathsf{map}(\operatorname{id}\times D)
   (\mathsf{runFiniteLogged}(P,(C,L)))
 =\mathsf{runLogged}(P,D(C,L)).
\]
For the established field/module setting, scalar sampler, proof selection $s$
and hash-budget parameter $n$, the finite first execution and three finite
residual attempts satisfy
\[
 \mathsf{finiteJointReplay}(P,s,n)=\mathsf{jointReplay}(P,s,n).
\]
These are exact equalities of oracle computations. They preserve the original
result, complete proofs, physical occurrences, acceptance tests, both challenges,
cache answers, miss-log order and randomness. Later attempts still execute after
an earlier failure. No caller supplies a state or replay correspondence premise.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BallotFiniteLogged}{runBallotFiniteLogged_eq}
\companchor{BallotFiniteLogged}{ballotFiniteReplaySourceRun_eq}
\companchor{BallotFiniteReplay}{ballotFiniteSourceAttempt_trace}
\companchor{BallotFiniteReplay}{ballotFiniteJointReplay_eq}

\paragraph{Actual repaired extractor and bounds.}
The actual extractor also uses the finite shadow source.
It equals the established
joint extractor exactly, so the witness and probability theorems transfer without
loss.
For finite $F$, an attacker hash bound $n$ per prefix view gives at most
$n+15$ original miss-log entries.
More generally the finite log grows by at most
the source hash budget.
Canonical finite scalar sampling retains the
$16(m+19)$ total-interaction bound from attacker total bound $m$.
\companchor{RepairedFiniteReplay}{repairedSubmissionFinite_extract_eq}
\companchor{RepairedFiniteReplay}{repairedSubmissionFinite_log_length_le}
\companchor{RepairedFiniteReplay}{repairedSubmissionFinite_canonical_total_bound}
\companchor{BallotFiniteLogged}{runBallotFiniteLogged_log_length_le}

\paragraph{Controls and remaining costs.}
Literal controls preserve hit answers without draws or duplicate entries, append
misses in chronological order and forward uniform queries without state changes.
Reversed logs, duplicate-hit logs and merged statements are distinguished.
\companchor{BallotFiniteLoggedControls}{BallotFiniteLoggedControls.hit_no_draw_no_log}
\companchor{BallotFiniteLoggedControls}{BallotFiniteLoggedControls.miss_appends}
\companchor{BallotFiniteLoggedControls}{BallotFiniteLoggedControls.detects_log_mutations}
All replay runs now use finite cache state and saved tapes.
Continuations are
still constructed and traversed as \code{OracleComp}.
The next subsection derives cache/log record bounds. These component equalities do not bound the rest of the state, bit encodings or local execution costs, and do not establish machine adequacy.
These equalities
do not discharge full election integration, DDH or computational ballot secrecy.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Replay storage and complete key records}\label{sec:replay-storage}
Let $c(C)$ count entries in the finite replay cache and $|L|$ count chronological
miss-log entries.
Counts refer to stored elements, with no bit encoding assumed.

\begin{theorem}[Derived cache/log balance]
For a finite scalar field $F$, any source oracle computation $P$, finite initial
state $(C,L)$ and supported output $(a,(C',L'))$ of its finite logged execution,
\[
 c(C')+|L|=c(C)+|L'|.
\]
A miss inserts one entry and appends one full key; a hit or uniform query
preserves both counts. Empty initialization therefore derives $c(C')=|L'|$.
With scalar sampling and a source hash-query bound $n$, cache growth is at most
$n$. No initial cache/log equality is a premise.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BallotReplayStorage}{runBallotFiniteLogged_storage_balance}
\companchor{BallotReplayStorage}{runBallotFiniteLogged_empty_sizes}
\companchor{BallotReplayStorage}{runBallotFiniteLogged_cache_length_le}

\paragraph{Fixed full-key layout.}
For statement $(g,pk,(c_0,c_1))$ and commitment pairs $(a_0,b_0),(a_1,b_1)$,
the record is $[g,pk,c_0,c_1,a_0,b_0,a_1,b_1]$.
It has eight group elements.
Decoding this record returns exactly the key; successful decoding also implies
that the input is exactly that key's record.
Thus packing is injective, and
inputs of other lengths reject.
\companchor{BallotKeyRecord}{ballotKeyOfRecord_roundTrip}
\companchor{BallotKeyRecord}{ballotKeyOfRecord_exact}
\companchor{BallotKeyRecord}{ballotKeyRecord_injective}
\companchor{BallotKeyRecord}{ballotKeyOfRecord_wrong_length}

\paragraph{Actual repaired source.}
For the existing two-candidate source over finite $F$, group $G$ with its scalar
action, any $g,pk$, vote and attacker with hash-query bound $n$ on every prefix
view, each supported empty-start finite logged output satisfies
\[
 c(C')=|L'|\le n+15,\qquad
 |\mathsf{GroupRecord}(C',L')|\le16(n+15),\quad
 |\mathsf{ScalarRecord}(C',L')|\le n+15.
\]
The group record packs all cache and log keys; the scalar record retains cache
answers.
Its exact lengths are $8(c(C')+|L'|)$ and $c(C')$ respectively.
These bounds count those projections, not shadow state, outputs or tape payloads.
\companchor{RepairedReplayStorage}{repairedSubmissionFinite_storage_le}
\companchor{RepairedReplayStorage}{repairedSubmissionFinite_record_cells_le}

\paragraph{Controls and boundary.}
An actual pure execution from a one-entry cache and empty log refutes
unconditional equal counts.
An actual fresh query records one key and answer.
The literal key $[1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8]$ fixes field order independently of round trips;
swapped fields change the key, and seven/nine-field records reject.
\companchor{BallotReplayStorageControls}{BallotReplayStorageControls.preloaded_refutes_equal_counts}
\companchor{BallotReplayStorageControls}{BallotReplayStorageControls.fresh_query_records_once}
\companchor{BallotReplayStorageControls}{BallotReplayStorageControls.literal_key_layout}
\companchor{BallotReplayStorageControls}{BallotReplayStorageControls.detects_key_layout_mutations}
The next subsection bounds the retained shadow entries.
This storage count does not bound encoded payloads or local execution costs and does not establish machine adequacy.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Retained shadow cache and request history}\label{sec:shadow-storage}
The finite shadow state consists of an association-list cache $C$, sticky
collision flag $b$ and ordered proof-request history $H$.
History retains every
requested statement, including repeated or colliding requests.
Let $c(C)$ count
cache entries.
These are entry counts rather than bit sizes or peak heap usage.

\begin{theorem}[Additive shadow storage]
Use the actual programmed interpreter's field $F$, group $G$, scalar action,
decidable key equality and scalar sampler. For any source $P$ over the proof
oracle, total-query bound $m$, finite initial shadow state $(C,b,H)$ and supported
output $(a,(C',b',H'))$ of that interpreter's execution,
\[
 c(C')\le c(C)+m,\qquad |H'|\le |H|+m.
\]
Raw requests add at most one cache entry and leave history unchanged. Each proof
request adds exactly one history entry and at most one cache entry. Induction on
the actual source computation composes these bounds, including adaptive query
arguments. No caller supplies a state invariant or equal cache/history sizes.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{RepairedShadowStorage}{runBallotFiniteProgrammed_storage_le}

\paragraph{Actual repaired source and random execution.}
For the existing two-candidate source over finite $F$, any $g,pk$, vote and
attacker with total-query bound $m$ for every prefix view, the previously derived
source bound is $m+19$.
From the actual empty shadow state, every supported output
therefore has at most $m+19$ cache entries and $m+19$ history entries.
The same
conclusion holds for the complete probabilistic execution with the finite live
oracle.
Pinned VCVio's support-inclusion theorem derives the latter from the
source bound: an output of the simulated live execution is an output in source
support.
No source-correspondence premise is assumed.
\companchor{RepairedShadowStorage}{repairedSubmissionFinite_shadow_storage_le}
\companchor{RepairedShadowStorage}{repairedSubmissionFinite_shadow_runtime_storage_le}
The bound charges retained entries per source request.
It does not require a
one-query scalar sampler and does not bound the sampler's execution cost.

\paragraph{Controls and a validation correction.}
Repeated programming of the same key leaves one cache entry and two history
entries, including on a collision.
A subsequent fresh request leaves two and
three.
These states refute equality of the two sizes and history deduplication.
The original retained-answer and sticky-flag controls remain in force.
\companchor{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls}{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls.storage_collision_offset}
\companchor{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls}{BallotFiniteProgrammedControls.fresh_storage_growth}
The independent dictionary/list harness retains its 69,904 transition comparisons
and checks 17,472 further size-offset transitions from four initial states.
A duplicate-insertion mutant initially escaped comparison because dictionary
projection erased multiplicity.
Comparing stored entry counts corrects this
validation weakness.
All four overwrite, duplicate-insertion, flag-reset and
history-deduplication mutants are now detected before the main enumeration.
These finite checks do not validate cryptographic transcript distributions.

\paragraph{Computational interface.}
The next subsection gives output bounds and a tape-size counterexample.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Submission output and tape operands}\label{sec:output-operands}
\begin{theorem}[Actual retained output bounds]
For the existing finite-field two-candidate source, any $g,pk$, vote and raw
attacker, every supported submission output has at most two honest-prefix board
entries and three final-board entries. No acceptance, collision-free or
attacker-query-bound premise is required. This holds in source support and in
the complete probabilistic execution through the finite shadow and live oracles.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{RepairedOutputStorage}{repairedSubmission_output_board_lengths}
\companchor{RepairedOutputStorage}{repairedSubmissionFinite_output_board_lengths}
Each actual submission appends at most one entry; proof and reuse rejection
retain the board.
Each two-candidate ballot stores two ciphertext pairs and three
proofs, each with two branches.
Its group record retains 16 ciphertext/commitment
coordinates; its scalar record retains 12 challenges/responses.
Thus for prefix
board $H$, submitted ballot and final board $B$, the projected counts are
\[
 16(|H|+1+|B|)\le96,\qquad 12(|H|+1+|B|)\le72.
\]
All three decisions and at most five scheduled voter identifiers remain in the
structured output.
These projections are element lists, not a binary serializer
or a cost bound for evaluating finite-index ballot functions.
\companchor{RepairedOutputStorage}{submissionRecord_lengths}
\companchor{RepairedOutputStorage}{repairedSubmissionFinite_output_cells_le}

\paragraph{Literal records and rejection controls.}
Independent records fix group fields $1,\ldots,16$ and scalar fields
$21,\ldots,32$.
Changing an aggregate response changes the scalar record even
when group records agree.
Existing actual-oracle controls retain accepted append
and both rejection cases.
The independent 27-history board fixture detects
appending on rejection and dropping prior entries; decisions are supplied in
this finite shape test, rather than derived cryptographically.
\companchor{RepairedOutputStorageControls}{RepairedOutputStorageControls.literal_records}
\companchor{RepairedOutputStorageControls}{RepairedOutputStorageControls.scalar_mutation_observed}

\begin{counterexample}[Event count does not bound binary uniform tags]
For each natural $k$, the computation querying \code{unifSpec} at index $2^k$
once, then returning unit, has total-query bound one. Its actual tape collection
supports the one-event tape $[(2^k,0)]$. That tag has $k+1$ bits in Mathlib's
binary natural representation. Hence no function of event count alone bounds
the binary tag sizes of all these supported tapes.
\end{counterexample}
\companchor{BallotTapeOperandControls}{BallotTapeOperandControls.wide_tape}
\companchor{BallotTapeOperandControls}{BallotTapeOperandControls.no_count_only_tag_bound}
The small coin fixture has the single tag bit $[\mathsf{true}]$.
Independent
integer-width fixtures cover powers of two and their predecessors for exponents
0--256.
The counterexample concerns actual tape collection and binary tag width;
it does not assert a lower bound for every possible compressed representation.

\paragraph{Execution interface.}
The next subsection encodes uniform operands.
Their construction and reading
still require costs through the concrete attacker/machine interface.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Canonical binary uniform operands}\label{sec:uniform-codec}
The actual uniform event type is a range index $n$ and answer $a\in\mathsf{Fin}(n+1)$.
The codec preserves that type and imposes no new range restriction.
Write $b(n)$
for binary width and $\mathsf{bits}_{LE}(n)$ for Mathlib's little-endian digits.
Define the natural record and event record by
\[
 N(n)=1^{b(n)}\mathbin\Vert[0]\mathbin\Vert\mathsf{bits}_{LE}(n),
 \qquad U(n,a)=N(n)\mathbin\Vert N(a).
\]
Here $\Vert$ concatenates bit lists.
The unary prefix records the binary width,
not the natural value.
Zero has record $[0]$.
The reader counts prefix ones,
reads that many payload bits and rejects a noncanonical binary payload.

\begin{theorem}[Exact prefix and complete event decoding]
For every natural $n$ and suffix $s$, reading $N(n)\Vert s$ returns $(n,s)$.
Conversely, whenever reading word $w$ returns $(n,s)$, $w=N(n)\Vert s$.
The complete event decoder reads two such records, checks $a<n+1$ and rejects
any remaining bits. For every actual uniform event $e$,
\[
 \mathsf{decode}(U(e))=\mathsf{some}(e),\qquad
 \mathsf{decode}(w)=\mathsf{some}(e)\Longrightarrow w=U(e).
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{UniformOperandCodec}{uniformNatRead_encode}
