\companchor{UniformOperandCodec}{uniformNatRead_exact}
\companchor{UniformOperandCodec}{uniformEventDecode_encode}
\companchor{UniformOperandCodec}{uniformEventDecode_exact}
The inverse uses the existing \code{Nat.bit} constructor, with its digit round
trip proved by Mathlib binary induction.
No finite-type enumeration is used.
The exact bit lengths are
\[
 |N(n)|=2b(n)+1,\qquad |U(n,a)|=2b(n)+2b(a)+2.
\]
\companchor{UniformOperandCodec}{uniformNatEncode_length}
\companchor{UniformOperandCodec}{uniformEventEncode_length}

\paragraph{Independent controls.}
The zero event encodes as $00$; coin replies zero and one encode as $1010$ and
$101101$.
Checked controls retain arbitrary suffix bits at a natural-record
boundary and reject truncation, padded zero, an answer above its declared range,
and ignored trailing input.
The supported wide-tag event $(2^k,0)$ has exactly
$2k+4$ encoded bits and decodes back exactly.
\companchor{UniformOperandCodecControls}{UniformOperandCodecControls.literal_events}
\companchor{UniformOperandCodecControls}{UniformOperandCodecControls.malformed_events}
\companchor{UniformOperandCodecControls}{UniformOperandCodecControls.supported_wide_event}
An independent parser gate checks 2,145 valid events with range indices 0--64
and all 8,191 words of length at most 12, after detecting three deliberately
permissive decoders.
It validates finite fixtures, not cryptographic sampling.

\paragraph{Backend choice and interface.}
The pinned optional backend supplies unary naturals and fixed-width bit vectors,
but no binary-natural constructor or general oracle-machine adequacy.
The narrow
implementation here uses Mathlib binary operations in the root build, without
new dependencies.
The next subsection completes scalar and saved-tape encoding.
Section~\ref{sec:prime-group} supplies the primitive group codec; assembled state
records, local costs and adequacy are outside this component's guarantee.
Exact decoding does not
certify polynomial time or discharge the full election/DDH secrecy reduction.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Historical scalars and complete saved bit tapes}\label{sec:replay-bits}
For $q>0$, a scalar $a\in\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ encodes as $N(a.\mathsf{val})$.
The decoder rejects trailing bits and values at or above $q$, rather than
reducing them modulo $q$.
It is a round trip, every accepted word is canonical,
and scalar length is at most $2b(q-1)+1$.
\companchor{ScalarCodec}{scalarDecode_encode}
\companchor{ScalarCodec}{scalarDecode_exact}
\companchor{ScalarCodec}{scalarEncode_length_le}
The actual lowered entropy specification has uniform draws and Unit-indexed
scalar challenges; its tapes contain no group keys.
Define event payloads by
$E(n,a)=0\Vert U(n,a)$ and $E(c)=1\Vert N(c.\mathsf{val})$, respectively.
For tape $T=[e_1,\ldots,e_k]$, with $l_i=|E(e_i)|$, define
\[
 B(T)=N(k)\Vert\mathop{\Vert}_{i=1}^k\bigl(N(l_i)\Vert E(e_i)\bigr),
 \quad |B(T)|=2b(k)+1+\sum_{i=1}^k(2b(l_i)+1+l_i).
\]
The decoder checks tags, payload lengths, event count and complete consumption.
\begin{theorem}[Canonical complete entropy tape]
For positive $q$ and every actual tape $T$, decoding $B(T)$ returns
$\mathsf{some}(T)$. Whenever decoding an arbitrary word $w$ returns
$\mathsf{some}(T)$, $w=B(T)$.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ReplayBitsCodec}{replayTapeDecode_encode}
\companchor{ReplayBitsCodec}{replayTapeDecode_exact}
\companchor{ReplayBitsCodec}{replayTapeEncode_length}

\paragraph{Integration with the actual extractor.}
The direct collector performs each source query and collects encoded frames and
a count.
It equals encoding the old typed collector's result.
The new extractor
decodes this saved word and resumes all three existing finite replay attempts.
For prime $q$, an additive group $G$ with a $\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ module action
and decidable equality, the existing scalar sampler, every $g,pk$, vote, attacker
and replay bound $n$, the new and existing repaired extractors are exactly equal
as oracle computations.
This retains outputs, randomness and failure behavior;
there is no caller-supplied codec-validity premise.
\companchor{ReplayBitsExecution}{replayBitsCollect_eq}
\companchor{ReplayBitsExecution}{repairedSubmissionBits_extract_eq}

\paragraph{Controls and interface.}
The empty tape is $0$; a single zero challenge is $1011100110$.
Literal controls
also reject the scalar modulus, wrong counts, truncated frames and trailing bits.
\companchor{ReplayBitsControls}{ReplayBitsControls.literal_frames}
\companchor{ReplayBitsControls}{ReplayBitsControls.malformed_frames}
An independent gate checks 4,760 tapes, 14,278 malformed variants and 32,767 words
through length 14, after detecting three decoder mutants.
Continuations remain
\code{OracleComp}; the next subsection addresses sampler enumeration.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Explicit prime scalar and nonce sampling}\label{sec:prime-samplers}
Pinned VCVio already supplies a computable \code{FinEnum} instance for
$\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$, using Mathlib's explicit \code{Fin} equivalence.
For
prime $q$, the existing scalar sampler uses at most one uniform-index query.
The actual repaired extractor consequently retains its bound $16(m+19)$ when
every attacker view has total query bound $m$, with this existing sampler and
no additional sampler-cost premise.
Group/module and decidable-equality
assumptions are unchanged.
\companchor{PrimeSamplers}{primeScalarSampler_total_bound}
\companchor{PrimeSamplers}{repairedSubmission_joint_extract_prime_total_bound}

\paragraph{Historical nonzero nonces.}
The generic historical sampler chooses a field unit through a noncomputable
finite-type equivalence.
The explicit prime-field replacement is
\[
 S_q:\quad i\gets\mathsf{uniform}(\mathsf{Fin}(q-1));\quad
 \mathsf{return}\ ((i.\mathsf{val}+1):\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z).
\]
The correspondence bijection maps $i$ to the unit with value $i+1$; its inverse
is the canonical residue value minus one.
Units are used in the proof only;
the executed output map performs successor and cast.

\begin{theorem}[Contextual nonce-distribution preservation]
Let $q$ be prime. For every result type $A$, continuation
$K:\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z\to\mathsf{ProbComp}(A)$ and result $x$,
\[
 \Pr[S_q\mathbin{\gg=}K=x]
 =\Pr[\mathsf{sampleNonzero}(\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z)\mathbin{\gg=}K=x].
\]
The same statement holds for two independent uses of $S_q$, the actual
historical \code{drawNoncePair}, and every continuation from a scalar pair.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeSamplers}{samplePrimeNonzero_probability}
\companchor{PrimeSamplers}{samplePrimeNonzero_bind}
\companchor{PrimeSamplers}{drawPrimeNoncePair_bind}
The explicit nonce sampler has at most one uniform-index interaction.
\companchor{PrimeSamplers}{samplePrimeNonzero_total_bound}

\paragraph{Controls and integration boundary.}
At $q=5$, explicit indices enumerate residues $1,2,3,4$.
Zero is absent; the
independent pair $(1,2)$ has probability $1/16$.
Reusing one uniform nonce for
both positions gives that pair probability zero.
\companchor{PrimeSamplersControls}{PrimeSamplersControls.literal_enumeration}
\companchor{PrimeSamplersControls}{PrimeSamplersControls.independent_pair}
\companchor{PrimeSamplersControls}{PrimeSamplersControls.reused_pair_rejected}
An independent gate checks 38 residues and 412 ordered pairs over five small
primes, after detecting four sampler mutants.
The next subsection integrates
the explicit sampler into the submission source.
Distribution equality does not assert identical enumeration order or query trees, so this sampler lemma alone does not justify replay replacement.
Both samplers still use ideal
uniform-index draws.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Explicit nonces in the complete submission source}\label{sec:prime-submission}
Fix prime $q$, an additive group $G$ with a $\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z$ module action
and decidable equality.
The existing prime scalar sampler is used throughout.
Factor the two honest nonce-pair draws in the submission source into a parameter
$D$.
All proof requests, verification, adaptive attacker calls and rejection
branches use their existing definitions.
Supplying the historical
\code{drawNoncePair} recovers the original source as exact oracle-computation
equality.
Supplying the explicit sampler defines the new source $P_{\mathrm{exp}}$.
\companchor{PrimeSubmission}{repairedSubmissionWithNoncePairs_original}

\paragraph{Complete finite runtime.}
Let $R_{s,c}(P)$ execute $P$ through the actual finite programmed interpreter and
finite live-cache interpreter, starting from shadow state $s$ and live cache $c$.
Its result includes the submission, final shadow state and final live cache.
Shadow state contains its physical cache entries, sticky collision flag and
ordered proof-request history.
The submission includes both honest decisions,
the retained prefix, malicious ballot, submission decision and final board.

\begin{theorem}[Contextual full-runtime preservation]
For every generator and public key, vote, adaptive raw attacker, initial states
$s,c$, result type $A$, probabilistic continuation $K$ on the complete runtime
result and result $x$,
\[
 \Pr[R_{s,c}(P_{\mathrm{exp}})\mathbin{\gg=}K=x]
 =\Pr[R_{s,c}(P_{\mathrm{old}})\mathbin{\gg=}K=x].
\]
No acceptance, cache-consistency or freshness premise is imposed on $s,c$.
In particular the actual empty-initial-state runtime has the same complete law.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeSubmission}{repairedSubmissionPrime_runtime_bind}
\companchor{PrimeSubmission}{repairedSubmissionPrime_runtime}
The proof derives state transparency of uniform draws through both interpreters,
then uses the nonce-pair continuation theorem at the two real sampling positions.
\companchor{PrimeSubmission}{drawPrimeNoncePair_finite_state}

\paragraph{Lowered raw source.}
The computable definition \code{repairedSubmissionPrimeSourceOracle} lowers this
source with the finite programmed handler.
Its returned submission and live-cache
distribution agree with the old finite source, by projection of the stronger
complete-state result.
\companchor{PrimeSubmission}{repairedSubmissionPrimeSource_runtime}

\paragraph{Controls and replay boundary.}
Actual nonce execution retains a prior collision, duplicate request history and
both cached answers; the empty-state control remains unflagged and empty.
\companchor{PrimeSubmissionControls}{PrimeSubmissionControls.nonce_state_retained}
\companchor{PrimeSubmissionControls}{PrimeSubmissionControls.empty_nonce_state}
Independent synthetic continuation fixtures cover 23,136 nonce tuples and detect
correlated pairs, state resets and discarded rejection branches.
They validate
that bounded continuation, not the historical cryptographic algorithms.
The next subsection proves replay directly for the explicit source.
Output-law
equality is used for cached validity, not as a replay correspondence.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Joint replay for the explicit source}\label{sec:prime-replay}
The existing generic replay theorem is applied to the explicit source itself.
Fix prime $q$, $g\ne0$, the group/module structure and decidable group equality.
An attacker with per-view raw hash bound $n$ gives the actual explicit source a
syntactically derived bound $n+15$; uniform nonce draws do not consume it.
\companchor{PrimeReplay}{repairedSubmissionPrimeSource_query_bound}
The checked result/cache law transfers supported cached-proof validity.
On the
explicit source's own original paths, each selected proof verifies exactly on
joint acceptance.
The derived budget enables its selector, and generic
reachability yields its physical replay occurrence.
No old path is supplied.
\companchor{PrimeReplay}{repairedSubmissionPrimePath_selector}
\companchor{PrimeReplayExtraction}{repairedSubmissionPrimePath_locations}

\paragraph{Actual saved-bit extractor.}
\code{repairedSubmissionPrimeBits_extract} collects encoded entropy from this
source and runs all three finite replay attempts.
It equals generic joint replay
of this same source.
Each successful output contains its original accepted
submission and witnesses for both components and their aggregate.
For injective
$r\mapsto r\mathbin\bullet g$ and $q>2$, those witnesses satisfy nonce addition
and the integer at-most-one-vote constraint.
\companchor{PrimeReplayExtraction}{repairedSubmissionPrimeBits_extract_eq}
\companchor{PrimeReplayExtraction}{repairedSubmissionPrimeBits_extract_valid}
\companchor{PrimeReplayExtraction}{repairedSubmissionPrimeBits_extract_consistent}

\begin{theorem}[Explicit-source joint extraction bound]
Assume generator exponentiation is injective and the per-view attacker hash
bound is $n$. Let $B$ be the historical programmed submission's actual malicious
acceptance probability, $\nu_q=1/(q-1)$, and
\[
 \ell=9\nu_q+\operatorname{ofReal}(90/q),\qquad a=(B-\ell)_+.
\]
For every $\delta\in\mathbb R_{\ge0}\cup\{\infty\}$,
\[
 (a-3(n+16)\delta)_+\,(\delta-1/q)_+^3
 \ \le\ \Pr[\text{explicit-source saved-bit extractor succeeds}].
\]
Subtractions and multiplication use extended nonnegative-real arithmetic.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeReplayExtraction}{repairedSubmissionPrimeBits_extract_le}
The three-selector probability is proved equal to actual joint acceptance;
the historical rejection and simulation loss is retained.
The proof applies
the generic three-attempt bound anew, without asserting old/new replay equality.
The threshold $\delta$ is not an algorithm input.

\paragraph{Interaction cost and remaining boundary.}
For per-view attacker total interaction bound $m$, the explicit original program,
lowered source and saved-bit extractor use at most $m+19$, $4(m+19)$ and
$16(m+19)$ interactions.
The last bound includes all three attempts and uses the
existing computable scalar sampler, with no sampler-cost premise.
\companchor{PrimeReplayCost}{repairedSubmissionPrimeBits_extract_total_bound}
Independent nonce/state/rejection controls and the context/tape gates are
retained, including physical-index, repeated-occurrence and failed-attempt cases.
These are interaction bounds, not machine-time bounds.
The following subsections
implement finite fair-bit sampling with explicit error.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Finite fair-bit sampling boundary}\label{sec:coin-denominators}
The current scalar and nonce samplers use ideal uniform finite-index draws.
Their implementation cannot silently replace these by a terminating fair-bit
program with the same law when the range has odd prime size.

\begin{theorem}[Derived dyadic probabilities]
Let $P:\mathsf{OracleComp}\;\mathsf{coinSpec}\;A$ be any actual terminating
program whose only queries return a fair Boolean. There exists a natural query
bound $n$ for $P$. For every such bound and every $x:A$, there exists $k\in\mathbb N$
satisfying
\[
 \Pr[P=x]\,2^n=k.
\]
Thus for every prime $q>2$ and every $x$, $\Pr[P=x]\ne 1/q$.
The maximum coin count is derived from $P$, not supplied as a premise of the
impossibility result.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CoinDenominators}{coinProgram_has_bound}
\companchor{CoinDenominators}{coinProgram_dyadic}
\companchor{CoinDenominators}{coinProgram_not_prime_uniform}
Induction takes the maximum of the two continuation bounds at a coin query.
If $\Pr[P=x]=1/q$, the integer-numerator result implies $q\mid 2^n$, hence
$q\mid2$, contradicting primality and $q>2$.

\paragraph{Independent controls.}
One fair bit is exactly uniform on two outcomes; two independently drawn bits
are exactly uniform on four ordered pairs.
Mapping their encoded values
$0,1,2,3$ modulo three yields masses $(1/2,1/4,1/4)$, not a uniform distribution.
The impossibility result specializes to every coin-only program returning
$\mathsf{Fin}\;3$.
\companchor{CoinDenominatorsControls}{CoinDenominatorsControls.uniform_two}
\companchor{CoinDenominatorsControls}{CoinDenominatorsControls.uniform_four}
\companchor{CoinDenominatorsControls}{CoinDenominatorsControls.modulo_biased}
\companchor{CoinDenominatorsControls}{CoinDenominatorsControls.uniform_three_impossible}
Independent rational fixtures enumerate binary-tree masses through depth four
and rejection cutoffs through eight attempts.
They detect uniform-modulo and
zero-cutoff-failure mutations.
These finite checks supplement the general proof.

\paragraph{Representation decision.}
This is a representation obstruction, not a ballot-secrecy counterexample.
Exact rejection sampling permits unbounded execution tails and needs a different
representation.
A finite rejection cutoff requires an explicit loss and fallback.
The selected route uses extra fair bits followed by modulo reduction, with
its statistical error retained.
It returns an in-range value without adding a
public abort.
The next two subsections prove its law, contextual error and
adaptive transfer into the actual extractor.
The historical ideal sampling law
and rejection behavior remain fixed.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Actual extra-bit modulo sampler}\label{sec:fair-bit-sampler}
For width $w$, the program draws $w$ independent fair bits and encodes them in
little-endian order with Mathlib's computable binary-digit equivalence.
It
returns an index in $\mathsf{Fin}(2^w)$ with exact point mass $2^{-w}$.
\companchor{FairBitSampler}{sampleFairBitIndex_probability}
For a positive range $q$, the actual modulo sampler returns this index modulo
$q$, with its range proof.
It always returns a value; no public abort is added.

\begin{theorem}[Exact residue law and sampling error]
Let $q>0$, $M=2^w$, $d=\lfloor M/q\rfloor$ and $r=M\bmod q$. For every $y<q$,
\[
 \Pr[\mathsf{sampleFairBitModulo}(q,w)=y]
 =\frac{d+\mathbf1_{y<r}}{M}.
\]
Its total variation distance from the ideal uniform distribution $U_q$ is at
most $r/M$. With $w=\mathsf{size}(q)+s$, where size is binary width,
\[
 \operatorname{TV}(\mathsf{sampleFairBitRange}(q,s),U_q)\le2^{-s}.
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{FairBitSampler}{sampleFairBitModulo_probability}
\companchor{FairBitSampler}{sampleFairBitModulo_tv_le}
\companchor{FairBitSampler}{sampleFairBitRange_tv_le}
The proof refills the final incomplete block with an independent ideal draw.
That proof-only distribution is exactly uniform.
It agrees with the actual
sampler off the final block, whose mass is $r/M$.
The executed sampler performs
modulo reduction throughout.
Since $q<2^{\mathsf{size}(q)}$, the selected width
gives the stated bound without assuming a sampler law.

\paragraph{Contextual meaning and query cost.}
For every common probabilistic continuation $K:\mathsf{Fin}\;q\to\mathsf{SPMF}\;A$,
the same $2^{-s}$ bound holds after binding both distributions to $K$.
It covers
$K$'s complete output, including retained state and observations.
The actual sampler uses at most $\mathsf{size}(q)+s$ fair-bit requests.
Arithmetic, finite-function encoding and machine costs are separate obligations.
\companchor{FairBitSampler}{sampleFairBitRange_bind_tv_le}
\companchor{FairBitSampler}{sampleFairBitRange_query_bound}

\paragraph{Controls.}
Literal two-bit inputs fix digit order.
Range four with width two is exactly
uniform.
Range three with slack three has masses $11/32,11/32,10/32$; it retains
bias while meeting the five-bit and $1/8$ error bounds.
\companchor{FairBitSamplerControls}{FairBitSamplerControls.literal_digit_order}
\companchor{FairBitSamplerControls}{FairBitSamplerControls.power_of_two_exact}
\companchor{FairBitSamplerControls}{FairBitSamplerControls.extra_bits_still_biased}
\companchor{FairBitSamplerControls}{FairBitSamplerControls.literal_budget}
Independent rational fixtures check 1,377 masses, 4,590 events and 3,341 width
cases, detecting exact-modulo, reused-bit, missing-high-bit and unspent-slack
mutations.
These finite controls supplement the general proofs.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Adaptive transfer to the joint extractor}\label{sec:prime-fair-bits}
The interpreter replaces every requested $\mathsf{Fin}(t+1)$ draw by the sampler
with width $\mathsf{size}(t+1)+s$.
The next range may depend on previous answers.
For any actual $P:\mathsf{ProbComp}\;A$ with total query bound $N$,
\[
 \operatorname{TV}(\mathsf{runFairBitUniform}(s,P),P)\le N2^{-s}.
\]
Independent support induction proves that every returned output was reachable
in $P$.
Boolean observations inherit the error bound and its truncated-subtraction
form.
Neither a range-size bound nor a correspondence is a caller premise.
\companchor{FairBitUniformOracle}{runFairBitUniform_tv_le}
\companchor{FairBitUniformOracle}{runFairBitUniform_support_subset}
\companchor{FairBitUniformOracle}{runFairBitUniform_event_loss}
An actual two-request control chooses the second range from its first answer;
its complete pair meets the two-step bound and retains the selected range.
Six independent adaptive fixtures supplement this control.
\companchor{FairBitUniformControls}{FairBitUniformControls.adaptive_distance}
\companchor{FairBitUniformControls}{FairBitUniformControls.adaptive_range_preserved}

\paragraph{Actual Helios replay implementation.}
The explicit extractor still distinguishes uniform-index and scalar-challenge
queries.
Its existing entropy interpreter lowers both to finite-range draws,
with exact distribution preservation and a derived one-query step bound.
Thus the earlier $16(m+19)$ interaction bound applies after lowering.
The new fair-bit extractor applies the interpreter above to this actual program.
For prime $q$, the existing group/module structure and decidable group equality,
\[
 \operatorname{TV}(\mathsf{FairExtractor}_s,\mathsf{IdealExtractor})
 \le16(m+19)2^{-s},
\]
where $m$ bounds every attacker-prefix view's total queries.
\companchor{PrimeFairBits}{repairedSubmissionFairBits_extract_tv_le}
For $g\ne0$, every successful output still contains its original accepted ballot
and all three valid witnesses.
Generator injectivity and $q>2$ retain nonce
addition and the integer at-most-one-vote constraint.
\companchor{PrimeFairBits}{repairedSubmissionFairBits_extract_valid}
\companchor{PrimeFairBits}{repairedSubmissionFairBits_extract_consistent}

\begin{theorem}[Fair-bit extraction success]
Assume generator injectivity, per-view hash bound $n$ and total bound $m$.
With $a$ and the nonnegative extended-real $\delta$ from
Section~\ref{sec:prime-replay}, set $E_s=\operatorname{ofReal}(16(m+19)2^{-s})$.
Then
\[
 \Bigl((a-3(n+16)\delta)_+(\delta-1/q)_+^3-E_s\Bigr)_+
 \le\Pr[\mathsf{FairExtractor}_s\text{ succeeds}].
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeFairBits}{repairedSubmissionFairBits_extract_le}
The historical ideal game, observations and rejection behavior stay fixed;
the sampling loss remains explicit.
This bound is not a PPT or privacy theorem.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Public group coordinates and canonical records}\label{sec:prime-group}
The group representation needed by encoded keys, caches and boards now uses
Mathlib's $q$th roots of unity in $\mathbb Z_p$, with the additive wrapper used
by the existing protocol.
Write $C(x)$ for the public residue coordinate.
No discrete-log index is stored.
For $q>0$,
\[
 C(0)=1,\qquad C(x+y)=C(x)C(y),\qquad C(r\mathbin{\bullet}x)=C(x)^{\operatorname{val}(r)}.
\]
The coordinate map is injective; for prime $p$, $C(-x)=C(x)^{-1}$.
\companchor{PrimeGroup}{primeGroupCoordinate_injective}
\companchor{PrimeGroup}{primeGroupCoordinate_smul}
\companchor{PrimeGroup}{primeGroupCoordinate_neg}
For prime $q$ and $C(g)\ne1$, scalar action on $g$ is injective.
If $p$ is
also prime, the subgroup has exactly $q$ elements, derived from this generator.
\companchor{PrimeGroup}{primeGroup_generator_injective}
\companchor{PrimeGroup}{primeGroup_card}
The actual encryption algorithm specializes, for prime $q$, to
\[
 (C(\mathsf{encryptWith}(g,pk,r,m)_1),C(\mathsf{encryptWith}(g,pk,r,m)_2))
 =\bigl(C(g)^{\operatorname{val}(r)},
 C(g)^{\operatorname{val}(m)}C(pk)^{\operatorname{val}(r)}\bigr).
\]
\companchor{PrimeGroup}{primeGroup_encrypt_coordinates}

\begin{theorem}[Internal group records]
Let $p,q>0$. Encode a group element by the canonical scalar record of $C(x)$.
The decoder requires an in-range residue $a$ with $a^q=1$. Then
\[
 D(E(x))=\mathsf{some}(x),\qquad
 D(w)=\mathsf{some}(x)\Longrightarrow w=E(x),\qquad
 |E(x)|\le2\,\mathsf{size}(p-1)+1.
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeGroupCodec}{primeGroupDecode_encode}
\companchor{PrimeGroupCodec}{primeGroupDecode_exact}
\companchor{PrimeGroupCodec}{primeGroupEncode_length_le}
Canonical nonmembers and out-of-range naturals reject; accepted encodings are
injective.
Identity coordinate one remains valid.
\companchor{PrimeGroupCodec}{primeGroupDecode_nonmember}
\companchor{PrimeGroupCodec}{primeGroupDecode_outOfRange}
\companchor{PrimeGroupCodec}{primeGroupEncode_injective}

For $p=23,q=11,g=2$, the checked instance has $pk=8$ and ciphertext $(16,4)$
for message one and nonce four.
Controls accept identity and reject zero,
nonmember $22$, alias $24$ and trailing input.
Independent integer arithmetic
validates the same equations and three decoder mutations on small prime groups.
\companchor{PrimeGroupControls}{PrimeGroupControls.literal_group}
\companchor{PrimeGroupControls}{PrimeGroupControls.literal_encryption}
\companchor{PrimeGroupControls}{PrimeGroupControls.literal_records}
\companchor{PrimeGroupControls}{PrimeGroupControls.malformed_records}
This is an internal codec for the existing typed subgroup.
The historical
raw-input policy, encoded execution costs and machine
adequacy are outside this component's guarantee.
Mathlib supplies modular-power algorithms; this result
does not supply their bit-machine costs or prove computational ballot secrecy.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Complete key and finite-cache bit records}\label{sec:cache-codec}
For $p,q>0$, the eight-field key layout from Section~\ref{sec:replay-storage}
now composes with the public-coordinate codec.
The ordered cache record stores
every complete key and scalar answer.
Its decoder checks unique keys and rejects
duplicates even when their answers agree.
No deduplication occurs.
\companchor{BallotCacheCodec}{ballotKeyBits_roundTrip}
\companchor{BallotCacheCodec}{ballotKeyBits_exact}
\companchor{BallotCacheCodec}{ballotCacheBits_duplicate}

Let $E_C,D_C$ encode and decode the actual finite association list.
Then
\[
 D_C(E_C(c))=\mathsf{some}(c),\qquad
 D_C(w)=\mathsf{some}(c)\Longrightarrow w=E_C(c).
\]
The encoder is injective as a complete ordered cache object.
In particular,
different entry orders cannot share an encoding even if their lookups agree.
\companchor{BallotCacheCodec}{ballotCacheBits_roundTrip}
\companchor{BallotCacheCodec}{ballotCacheBits_exact}
\companchor{BallotCacheCodec}{ballotCacheBits_injective}
A shared list/pair framing layer stores field counts and payload lengths using
the existing binary natural codec and requires complete input consumption.
Its concrete instances discharge both canonicality laws.
\companchor{BitRecordCodec}{bitFieldsDecode_exact}

Encoded lookup decodes the cache and full key, then performs the original finite
lookup.
Malformed input returns $\mathsf{none}$; a valid cache miss returns
$\mathsf{some}(\mathsf{none})$.
Encoded insertion also decodes its scalar answer
and returns exactly the encoding of the original cache insertion.
\companchor{BallotCacheCodec}{ballotCacheBitsLookup_encode}
\companchor{BallotCacheCodec}{ballotCacheBitsInsert_encode}

\paragraph{Complete record bounds.}
Write $F(b)=2\,\mathsf{size}(b)+1+b$ for payload framing, and set
\[
\begin{aligned}
 L(p)&=2\,\mathsf{size}(p-1)+1,& K(p)&=9+8F(L(p)),\\
 E(p,q)&=5+F(K(p))+F(L(q)),&
 B(p,q,n)&=2\,\mathsf{size}(n)+1+nF(E(p,q)).
\end{aligned}
\]
The key, entry and $n$-entry cache lengths are bounded by $K(p)$, $E(p,q)$ and
$B(p,q,n)$ respectively, including all framing and scalar answers.
\companchor{BallotCacheBitSize}{ballotKeyBits_length_le}
\companchor{BallotCacheBitSize}{ballotCacheEntryBits_length_le}
\companchor{BallotCacheBitSize}{ballotCacheBits_length_le}
For prime $p,q$ and attacker per-view hash bound $n$, every supported execution
of the explicit-nonce source through the finite logged interpreter has an
encoded cache of length at most $B(p,q,n+15)$.
The source's own query budget
derives the entry bound; no old/new replay-path correspondence is assumed.
\companchor{BallotCacheBitSize}{repairedSubmissionPrime_cache_bits_le}

Literal controls retain all eight key fields, cache hits and misses, agreeing
duplicate rejection, entry order and trailing-input rejection.
Independent
integer fixtures check the framing and bounds and detect three decoder defects.
\companchor{BallotCacheCodecControls}{BallotCacheCodecControls.literal_lookup}
\companchor{BallotCacheCodecControls}{BallotCacheCodecControls.entry_order_preserved}
Sections~\ref{sec:state-codec}--\ref{sec:output-codec} assemble state and output
records.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Complete finite interpreter data}\label{sec:state-codec}
The logged-state codec stores its cache and chronological full-key miss log.
The programmed-state codec stores the shadow cache, sticky flag and statement
history in source order (most recent first), including repetitions.
The
both-cache codec adds the separate live cache.

For $p,q>0$, these complete objects round-trip and admit only canonical words.
Unit log tags have one possible value; Boolean records require one bit.
\companchor{BallotStateCodec}{ballotLoggedBits_roundTrip}
\companchor{BallotStateCodec}{ballotLoggedBits_exact}
\companchor{BallotStateCodec}{ballotProgrammedBits_roundTrip}
\companchor{BallotStateCodec}{ballotProgrammedBits_exact}
\companchor{BallotStateCodec}{ballotBothCachesBits_roundTrip}
\companchor{BallotStateCodec}{ballotBothCachesBits_exact}

\paragraph{Actual programming transition.}
For prime $q$, encoded programming decodes state and applies the original
sampled-transcript transition, returning its typed proof and encoded successor.
Statement/transcript inputs remain typed.
Occupied keys retain old answers,
set the flag and record each request, even on challenge agreement.
\companchor{BallotStateCodec}{ballotProgrammedBitsApply_encode}

\paragraph{Complete data bounds.}
With $F,L,K,B$ from Section~\ref{sec:cache-codec}, write
\[
 P(a,b)=5+F(a)+F(b),\qquad V(a,n)=2\,\mathsf{size}(n)+1+nF(a).
\]
For fixed $p,q$, define the statement, logged, programmed and both-cache bounds
\[
\begin{aligned}
 S&=P(P(L(p),L(p)),P(L(p),L(p))),\\
 J(c,l)&=P(B(p,q,c),V(K(p),l)),\\
 T(c,h)&=P(B(p,q,c),P(1,V(S,h))),\\
 U(c,h,l)&=P(T(c,h),B(p,q,l)).
\end{aligned}
\]
Every count, field prefix, payload and flag is included.
For prime $p,q$ and
per-view attacker hash bound $n$, actual supported execution of the explicit
replay source through the finite logged interpreter yields encoded state
length at most $J(n+15,n+15)$.
\companchor{BallotStateBitSize}{repairedSubmissionPrime_logged_bits_le}
With total bound $m$ as well, the complete explicit-nonce probabilistic runtime
yields both-cache data length at most $U(m+19,m+19,n+15)$.
Its own syntax and
runtime support derive the shadow, history and live-cache counts.
\companchor{BallotStateBitSize}{repairedSubmissionPrime_bothCaches_bits_le}

Controls decode an occupied-key transition: answer three remains when seven
was sampled, with true flag and repeated history.
Further controls distinguish
erased flags/live caches, reversed logs and malformed flags.
Independent
fixtures cover fresh/occupied keys, both initial flags and every small-field challenge.
\companchor{BallotStateCodecControls}{BallotStateCodecControls.encoded_occupied_transition}
\companchor{BallotStateCodecControls}{BallotStateCodecControls.live_cache_not_erased}
\companchor{BallotStateCodecControls}{BallotStateCodecControls.chronological_log_not_reversed}
These records cover finite interpreter data; Section~\ref{sec:output-codec}
assembles protocol outputs.
Continuation execution and machine adequacy are outside this component's guarantee.
Record sizes do not establish election integration, DDH or all-PPT secrecy.

\subsection{Historical component: Complete outputs and public observations}\label{sec:output-codec}
For $p,q>0$, canonical bit records now cover complete proofs, two-component
ballots, ordered voter-identified boards and submission results.
Both branches
of every component and aggregate proof remain present.
Decisions have three
distinct two-bit tags; the unused tag rejects.
For each codec $(E,D)$,
\[
 D(E(x))=\mathsf{some}(x),\qquad D(w)=\mathsf{some}(x)\Longrightarrow w=E(x).
\]
\companchor{BallotOutputCodec}{ballotBits_roundTrip}
\companchor{BallotOutputCodec}{ballotBits_exact}
\companchor{BallotOutputCodec}{ballotBoardBits_roundTrip}
\companchor{BallotOutputCodec}{ballotSubmissionBits_exact}
Public prefix/result records also retain parameters, key proof, ordered
candidate/voter lists, fingerprint, honest decisions, encrypted tallies, shares,
decryption proofs and optional decoded tallies.
Decoding failure differs from
zero.
Complete public-result encoding is injective.
\companchor{PublicObservationCodec}{ballotPublicPrefixBits_roundTrip}
\companchor{PublicObservationCodec}{ballotPublicResultBits_roundTrip}
\companchor{PublicObservationCodec}{ballotPublicResultBits_exact}
\companchor{PublicObservationCodec}{ballotPublicResultBits_injective}

For prime $q$ and positive $p$, encoded submission inputs run the original
shared-oracle verifier, preserving its queries, rejection priority and board.
The finishing wrapper uses the original typed hashes and private inputs.
\companchor{BallotOutputCodec}{repairedSubmitBitsOracle_encode}
\companchor{PublicObservationCodec}{finishRepairedElectionBits_encode}
These internal interfaces leave the historical raw-input policy open.
Well-typed invalid ballots decode and are still rejected by the verifier.
\companchor{BallotOutputCodecControls}{BallotOutputCodecControls.representation_not_validity}

\paragraph{Actual runtime data bound.}
For fixed $p,q$, use $L,P,V,U$ from the preceding sections and abbreviate
\[
\begin{gathered}
 C=P(L(p),L(p)),\quad R=P(C,P(L(q),L(q))),\quad H=P(R,R),\\
 A=P(P(C,C),P(P(H,H),H)),\quad B_o(n)=V(P(5,A),n),\\
 S_o(n,k)=P(P(P(2,2),B_o(n)),P(A,P(2,B_o(k)))).
\end{gathered}
\]
For prime $p,q$, any $g,pk$, vote and per-view attacker with hash bound $n$
and total query bound $m$, every supported word of the actual internal
bit-result runtime has length at most
\[
 P\bigl(S_o(2,3),\ U(m+19,m+19,n+15)\bigr).
\]
Actual source support derives both board counts.
Decoding the bit-result
program exactly recovers the original probabilistic computation, including
both caches, history and flag.
These private data remain internal.
\companchor{BallotOutputBitSize}{repairedSubmissionPrime_output_bits_le}
\companchor{SubmissionRuntimeCodec}{repairedSubmissionRuntimeBits_decode}
\companchor{SubmissionRuntimeCodec}{repairedSubmissionRuntimeBits_length_le}
Independent integer fixtures detect twelve public-field changes and check
36 submission-size cases.
Arbitrary natural metadata retains its own width;
no uniform fingerprint bound is assumed.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Concrete cache execution primitives}\label{sec:key-machine}
Comparison and copying use Mathlib TM2 with finite local callbacks and
already-loaded inputs.
Write $T$ for the actual step, fixing halted configurations.

\paragraph{Complete-key comparison.}
The comparator uses two binary stacks and nine local states.
It pops both
heads, continuing only on matching nonempty heads.
For arbitrary words $x,y$
and memory $v$, the configuration $c=T^{|x|+1}(I(x,y,v))$ satisfies
\[
 c.\mathsf{label}=\mathsf{none}\ \land\ a(c)=\mathsf{decide}(x=y),
\]
where $a(c)$ compares the final saved optional heads.
\companchor{BitCompareMachine}{BitCompareMachine.run}
\companchor{BitCompareMachine}{BitCompareMachine.memory_card}
For $p,q>0$ and arbitrary original typed keys $u,v$, canonical full-key
injectivity and the bound $K(p)$ of Section~\ref{sec:cache-codec} derive some
fuel $n\le K(p)+1$ whose terminal answer is $\mathsf{decide}(u=v)$.
\companchor{BitCompareMachine}{BitCompareMachine.key_run}

\paragraph{Source-preserving copying.}
Comparison consumes its inputs.
Copying uses three binary stacks, two labels
and optional-bit memory: move source to empty scratch, then restore source and
push each bit onto destination.
Let $C(\ell,x,y,z,v)$ contain label, source,
destination, scratch and memory.
For arbitrary $x,y,v$,
\[
 T^{2|x|+2}(C(\mathsf{collect},x,y,[],v))
 = C(\mathsf{halt},x,x\mathbin{+\!+}y,[],\mathsf{none}).
\]
\companchor{BitCopyMachine}{BitCopyMachine.run}
This conclusion preserves exact stack contents, including order and suffix.
For positive $p,q$, a complete encoded key can therefore be copied with fuel
at most $2K(p)+2$.
\companchor{BitCopyMachine}{BitCopyMachine.key_copy}

For prime $p,q$, any $g,pk$, vote and attacker with per-view hash bound $n$, take
an actual supported result of the empty-initialized explicit-nonce finite logged
source.
Its complete encoded cache has the same copy guarantee with some fuel
at most $2B(p,q,n+15)+2$, derived from the source's query/storage bound.
\companchor{BitCopyMachine}{BitCopyMachine.source_cache_copy}

\paragraph{Controls and scope.}
Controls retain late mismatches, strict prefixes, nonpalindromic copying,
suffixes, empty inputs and the unfinished first pass.
\companchor{BitCompareMachine}{BitCompareMachine.prefix_control}
\companchor{BitCopyMachine}{BitCopyMachine.copy_control}
\companchor{BitCopyMachine}{BitCopyMachine.first_pass_not_copy}
\companchor{BitCopyMachine}{BitCopyMachine.no_erasure_or_reversal}
Separate exhaustive campaigns each check 16,129 pairs of words of length at
most six.
They detect prefix acceptance, reversed copying and erased-source
mutants before checking the correct programs, with no failures or discards.

These bounds count concrete TM2 transitions.
Later sections execute binary
record parsing and source-cache lookup (Section~\ref{sec:cache-lookup}).

\subsection{Historical component: Concrete parsing of binary-natural prefixes}\label{sec:nat-parser}
Cache counts and field lengths use the existing natural record
$E(n)=1^{\mathsf{size}(n)}\,0\,\mathsf{bits}(n)$, with little-endian payload.
The concrete TM2 parser uses four binary stacks, three labels and optional-bit
memory.
It stores width markers, consumes one payload bit per marker, checks
the most significant bit and reverses scratch into output.
Zero has an empty
payload.
All local callbacks have finite domains.

Let $I(w)$ load input $w$ with empty workspace/output and initial width control;
let $T$ be the actual step, fixing halted configurations.
The readout $O(c)$
returns output bits and remaining input only after successful halt.
Write $D$
for the original \texttt{uniformNatRead}.

\begin{theorem}[Every raw input, machine-checked]
For every bit word $w$, there exists $t\le 3|w|+3$ such that $c=T^t(I(w))$
satisfies
\[
 c.\mathsf{label}=\mathsf{none},\qquad
 O(c)=\mathsf{map}\bigl((n,s)\mapsto(\mathsf{bits}(n),s),D(w)\bigr).
\]
There is no well-formedness or successful-decoding premise.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{NatPrefixMachine}{NatPrefixMachine.total_run}
For every natural $n$ and suffix $s$, execution on $E(n)\mathbin{+\!+}s$ with
fuel $3\,\mathsf{size}(n)+3$ reaches the complete successful configuration:
input $s$, output $\mathsf{bits}(n)$ and empty count/scratch stacks.
\companchor{NatPrefixMachine}{NatPrefixMachine.encoded_run}
The last-bit test is derived from the original codec's canonicality laws;
the existing decoder and format are unchanged.
\companchor{NatPrefixFormat}{uniformNatRead_frame}
General rejection proofs cover absent delimiters, insufficient payload and
noncanonical high-zero padding.
These cases exhaust malformed inputs.
\companchor{NatPrefixMachine}{NatPrefixMachine.no_delimiter_run}
\companchor{NatPrefixMachine}{NatPrefixMachine.truncated_run}
\companchor{NatPrefixMachine}{NatPrefixMachine.noncanonical_run}

\paragraph{The actual cache count prefix.}
The complete cache encoder supplies its outer count field; parsing it preserves
every remaining entry and framing bit.
\companchor{NatPrefixMachine}{NatPrefixMachine.cache_header_run}
For prime $p,q$, any $g,pk$, vote and attacker with per-view hash bound $n$, take
a supported result of the empty-initialized explicit-nonce finite logged source.
The actual cache header has a concrete parsing run with some fuel at most
$3\,\mathsf{size}(n+15)+3$, returning its exact entry count as binary digits and
the untouched remainder.
Source support derives the count bound.
\companchor{NatPrefixMachine}{NatPrefixMachine.source_cache_header}

Independent enumeration compares all 8,191 words of length at most twelve with

Kernel controls retain six and zero with suffixes,
malformed input and nonreversed output.
\companchor{NatPrefixMachine}{NatPrefixMachine.suffix_control}
\companchor{NatPrefixMachine}{NatPrefixMachine.malformed_controls}

Output is binary; integer conversion is uncharged.
Later sections execute
decrement, record parsing, lookup and guarded insertion (Section~\ref{sec:cache-insert}).
Raw-word correctness covers internal framing; the historical
network/group-input policy is separate.

\subsection{Historical component: Canonical binary decrement for field reading}\label{sec:binary-counter}
The field parser needs to consume binary lengths without converting them to an
uncharged machine integer.
Decrement now uses the existing four-stack layout,
two labels and optional-bit memory.
The counter occupies the output stack;
scratch is empty initially.
Input and count stacks retain the surrounding data.

Borrow consumes each low zero and stores a one on scratch.
At the first one,
the machine inserts a zero only if higher bits remain, then restores scratch.
Thus one becomes canonical empty zero; three becomes the two-bit word
$[0,1]$.
Zero remains empty and reports underflow.
All local callbacks have
finite domains.

Let $C(\ell,b,z,s,f,v)$ contain label, counter, scratch, input, count stack and
local memory.
Write $T$ for the actual TM2 step, fixing halted configurations.
\begin{theorem}[Canonical counter execution, machine-checked]
For every natural $n$, arbitrary words $s,f$ and local memory $v$, there exists
$t\le 2\,\mathsf{size}(n)+1$ such that
\[
\begin{aligned}
&T^t(C(\mathsf{borrow},\mathsf{bits}(n),[],s,f,v))\\
&\qquad=C(\mathsf{halt},\mathsf{bits}(n-1),[],s,f,
  \mathsf{some}(\mathsf{decide}(n\ne0))).
\end{aligned}
\]
Here $n-1$ is truncated natural subtraction. The conclusion preserves the entire
configuration, including both surrounding stacks.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BitCounterMachine}{BitCounterMachine.run}
A chain of $k$ low zeros followed by a one has a concrete run with sufficient
fuel $2k+2$.
The bit transformation agrees with natural predecessor on canonical
words; it is not a Nat operation inside the machine.
\companchor{BitCounterMachine}{BitCounterMachine.borrow_chain_run}
\companchor{BitCounterMachine}{BitCounterMachine.predBits_bits}

\paragraph{Connection to the actual prefix parser.}
For any word with $D(w)=\mathsf{some}(n,s)$, the preceding parser theorem supplies
canonical digits, suffix $s$ and empty workspace.
The counter theorem applies
to that actual configuration after changing only its finite control label.
\companchor{BitCounterMachine}{BitCounterMachine.parsed_run}
This establishes the data interface between two concrete runs.
The next
subsection links them into a single-field program and charges its control
transitions; this counter theorem alone does not supply that composition.

Independent fixtures compare 4,341 naturals with integer subtraction and
canonical binary expansion, including borrow chains through 256 bits.
They first
detect retained-high-zero and lost-borrow mutants.
The correct campaign has no
failures or discards.

underflow, the distinct flags and unchanged surrounding stacks.
\companchor{BitCounterMachine}{BitCounterMachine.borrow_control}
\companchor{BitCounterMachine}{BitCounterMachine.one_control}
\companchor{BitCounterMachine}{BitCounterMachine.odd_control}
\companchor{BitCounterMachine}{BitCounterMachine.underflow_distinct}

\subsection{Historical component: Linked execution of length-prefixed fields}\label{sec:linked-field}
One finite-control TM2 program now parses a length and reads its payload.
It
uses the same four binary stacks and optional-bit memory.
Subroutine labels are
renamed and halt becomes a return jump; the proved transfer retains all data
and uses no more fuel than the original run.
Halted padding is removed before
caller execution.
\companchor{TM2ReturnLink}{TM2ReturnLink.run}

The field loop checks for exhausted input before decrement.
Consumed bits
accumulate on the count stack; when the binary counter reaches zero, restoration
puts the field in its original order.
Success preserves the unconsumed suffix
and clears both work stacks.
For declared count $n$ and input $s$, rejection of
$|s|<n$ takes at most $|s|(2\,\mathsf{size}(n)+2)+1$ transitions, leaving the
remaining binary count and reversed partial field.
There is no unary expansion
of the declared length.
\companchor{FieldReadMachine}{FieldReadMachine.run}
\companchor{FieldReadMachine}{FieldReadMachine.truncated_run}

Define $F(w)$ by the existing natural-prefix decoder: if it rejects, return
$\mathsf{none}$; if it returns $(n,s)$, return
$\mathsf{some}(\mathsf{take}_n(s),\mathsf{drop}_n(s))$ when $n\le|s|$, and
$\mathsf{none}$ otherwise.
Let $I(w)$ load $w$ with empty workspace and prefix
control, $T$ be the linked program's actual step fixing halted configurations,
and $O$ return the field/suffix only after a successful halt.
\begin{theorem}[Every raw field input, machine-checked]
For every bit word $w$, there exists
\[
 t\le 3|w|+5+(|w|+1)(2|w|+4)
\]
such that $c=T^t(I(w))$ satisfies
\[
 c.\mathsf{label}=\mathsf{none}\quad\land\quad O(c)=F(w).
\]
No well-formedness, successful-decoding or numeric-length bound is assumed.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{FieldPrefixMachine}{FieldPrefixMachine.total_run}
The bound includes prefix parsing, field execution, return jumps and final halt.
The original prefix exactness law bounds the count's bit width by input length.
For records accepted by the existing codec with exactly one field, dropping the
three-bit outer count header supplies this program's actual framing and yields
that field with an empty suffix.
\companchor{FieldPrefixMachine}{FieldPrefixMachine.singleton_record_run}

Independent fixtures check 6,867 count/input cases, including 257-bit counts,
and 8,191 raw words of length at most twelve.
Reverse-output and
truncation-acceptance mutants are detected; the correct campaigns have no
failures or discards.
Lean controls retain suffix/order, empty fields, actual
rejection halts and immediate empty-input rejection for a huge count.
\companchor{FieldReadMachine}{FieldReadMachine.no_padding_control}
\companchor{FieldReadMachine}{FieldReadMachine.huge_empty_control}
\companchor{FieldPrefixMachine}{FieldPrefixMachine.rejection_controls}

\paragraph{Execution boundary.}
Multi-field framing and the outer count loop are outside this component's guarantee, followed by cache
scan/update, loading, arithmetic, saved contexts and quantitative adequacy.
This internal field parser does not settle historical network-input policy or
full-election all-PPT secrecy.

\subsection{Historical component: Outer-state preservation and one record iteration}\label{sec:record-field-step}
Repeated field calls must retain the outer record count and already stored data.
The reader uses all four work stacks, so two extra binary stacks hold this state.
Stack relocation preserves every statement result, step and run exactly, with
unchanged fuel and extra-stack contents.
Saving these values inside the work
stacks instead would need an additional encoding and repeated save/restore costs.
\companchor{TM2StackFrame}{TM2StackFrame.run}

The six-stack program first calls the field reader in its original positions.
It then relocates decrement onto the extra outer-count stack, retaining the
just-read field and archive.
Both calls and their return jumps execute in shared
control.
The complete successful workspace is derived from the reader's execution,
rather than assumed from equality of its readout.
\companchor{RecordFieldStepMachine}{RecordFieldStepMachine.field_complete}
\companchor{RecordFieldStepMachine}{RecordFieldStepMachine.counter_run}

Write $S(\ell,s,c,z,b,o,a,v)$ for label, input, collected bits, scratch, field,
outer count, archive and local memory.
Let $I(w,o,a)$ start the linked program
with input $w$, outer count $o$, archive $a$ and empty work stacks.
\begin{theorem}[Executed record-field iteration, machine-checked]
For every field $b$, suffix $s$, archive $a$ and natural $n$, there exists
\[
 t\le 3\,\mathsf{size}(|b|)+10
      +|b|(2\,\mathsf{size}(|b|)+3)+2\,\mathsf{size}(n+1)
\]
such that the actual fixed-halt step $T$ satisfies
\[
\begin{aligned}
&T^t(I(E(|b|)\mathbin{+\!+}b\mathbin{+\!+}s,
          \mathsf{bits}(n+1),a))\\
&\qquad=S(\mathsf{halt},s,[],[],b,\mathsf{bits}(n),a,\mathsf{some}(1)).
\end{aligned}
\]
The initial outer count is positive; the archive is arbitrary and unchanged.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{RecordFieldStepMachine}{RecordFieldStepMachine.run}
Every raw field call also preserves the two extra stacks on rejection.
If the
single-field decoder rejects $w$, the program halts before decrement within
$3|w|+6+(|w|+1)(2|w|+4)$ transitions, with a non-success flag and unchanged outer
count/archive.
\companchor{RecordFieldStepMachine}{RecordFieldStepMachine.rejected_run}

Independent six-stack fixtures compare 10,220 raw-field/count/archive cases
against indexed decoding, slicing and integer subtraction, including 257-bit
outer counts.
Scratch/archive and counter/field alias mutants are detected;
there are no failures or discards in the correct campaign.
Lean controls retain
a complete 33-step field iteration with outer count $8\to7$, rejection frames,

\companchor{RecordFieldStepMachine}{RecordFieldStepMachine.iteration_control}
\companchor{RecordFieldStepMachine}{RecordFieldStepMachine.rejection_frame_control}

\paragraph{Repeated traversal.}
This iteration preserves the archive.
Section~\ref{sec:archived-field} records
original field frames and clears output for reuse.

loads the initial count and proves the repeated loop, including malformed later

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Original-frame accumulation and workspace reuse}\label{sec:archived-field}
Each successful input pop now copies its bit onto the archive stack.
This
retains the original length prefix and payload, in reverse, without a second
field codec.
Failed pops copy nothing.
A structural check on the actual program
proves that it never pushes to input or accesses archive, and that input-pop
callbacks store their head in finite local memory.
\companchor{ArchivedFieldMachine}{ArchivedFieldMachine.eligible}

For eligible code, the recorded run has exactly the original control, memory,
other stacks and TM2 fuel.
It derives a consumed prefix $d$ with original input
$d\mathbin{+\!+}\text{residual}$ and archive
$\mathsf{reverse}(d)\mathbin{+\!+}a$.
These copies execute as finite instructions
inside existing TM2 macro steps.
\companchor{TM2InputArchive}{TM2InputArchive.run}
The restriction on archive access is necessary: a checked program consumes true
and then branches on archive.
It chooses false with the original empty archive
and true after recording.
That program is checked to violate eligibility.
\companchor{TM2InputArchive}{TM2InputArchive.archive_dependency_control}

Let $R(b)=E(|b|)\mathbin{+\!+}b$ be the original field frame.
Use the six-stack
state notation $S$ from the preceding subsection.
The new start $I$ calls the
recorded iteration, checks its return flag, and clears output on success.
\begin{theorem}[Archived iteration with reusable workspace, machine-checked]
For every field $b$, suffix $s$, archive $a$ and natural $n$, there exists
\[
 t\le 3\,\mathsf{size}(|b|)+12
       +|b|(2\,\mathsf{size}(|b|)+4)+2\,\mathsf{size}(n+1)
\]
such that the actual fixed-halt step $T$ satisfies
\[
\begin{aligned}
&T^t(I(R(b)\mathbin{+\!+}s,\mathsf{bits}(n+1),a))\\
&\quad=S(\mathsf{halt},s,[],[],[],\mathsf{bits}(n),
          \mathsf{reverse}(R(b))\mathbin{+\!+}a,\mathsf{some}(1)).
\end{aligned}
\]
Count, scratch and output are empty; the suffix and old archive are retained.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ArchivedFieldMachine}{ArchivedFieldMachine.run}
For every rejected raw field $w$, the program halts with non-success flag and
unchanged outer count within $3|w|+7+(|w|+1)(2|w|+4)$ transitions.
Its archive
is the reversed consumed prefix followed by the unchanged old archive;
cleanup cannot accept a rejected input.
\companchor{ArchivedFieldMachine}{ArchivedFieldMachine.rejected_run}

Independent fixtures check 8,176 raw-field/count/archive combinations, including
257-bit counts.
Indexed decoding independently determines consumed-prefix length
on success and rejection.
Missing-header and erased-archive mutants are detected;
there are no failures or discards.
Lean controls retain a complete 37-step run,
an empty field's boundary, absent-input behavior and exact rejected-prefix data.
\companchor{ArchivedFieldMachine}{ArchivedFieldMachine.iteration_control}

\subsection{Historical component: Complete counted-record execution}\label{sec:record-parser}
One finite six-stack TM2 now parses the count into the outer binary stack,
records its header, repeats the archived field operation, and restores the
original word.
Zero count accepts only empty residual input.
Every call resets
local memory and checks the return flag.
Missing or malformed later fields
halt in rejection; no unary expansion of the numeric count occurs.
\companchor{RecordParserMachine}{RecordParserMachine.count_run}
\companchor{RecordParserMachine}{RecordParserMachine.loop_run}

Write $D$ for the original \code{bitFieldsDecode}, $I(w)$ for this machine's
start with input $w$ and empty workspace, and $T$ for its actual fixed-halt
TM2 step.
Readout $O(c)$ is the output stack when $c$ is halted with success
flag, and is none otherwise.
Define
\[
 U(B,h)=3B+16+(B+1)(2B+4)+2h.
\]
\begin{theorem}[All-input record framing, machine-checked]
For every binary word $w$, with $L=|w|$, there exists
\[
 t\le 3L+4+(L+1)U(L,L)
\]
such that $c=T^t(I(w))$ is halted and
\[
 O(c)=\mathsf{Option.map}\,(\lambda\_.\,w)\,(D(w)).
\]
Thus successful output retains the complete original encoding, including the
outer count and every field boundary.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{RecordParserMachine}{RecordParserMachine.total_run}

The loop invariant conserves remaining-input plus archive length.
A successful
call consumes at least the one-bit length prefix, even for an empty field.
This bounds repeated calls by raw input length.
The correspondence uses the
original recursive field reader, whose existing definitions and exactness laws
are exposed by visibility changes only.
\companchor{RecordFrameFormat}{recordBodyResult_original}

For an encoded cache, the same machine validates its framing and retains its
original word.
In a supported explicit-source result initialized with empty
cache/log, prime $p,q$ and the attacker's per-view hash-query bound $n$ derive
$B=\mathsf{cacheRecordBitBound}(p,q,n+15)$.
The fuel is at most
$3B+4+(B+1)U(B,B)$, without an added cache-size or execution certificate.
\companchor{RecordParserMachine}{RecordParserMachine.cache_run}
\companchor{RecordParserMachine}{RecordParserMachine.source_cache_run}

Independent indexed decoding checks 8,191 raw words of length at most 12 and
477 structured/directed records, including 257-bit counts.

\companchor{RecordParserMachine}{RecordParserMachine.multiple_fields_control}

\subsection{Historical component: Restoring the query between comparisons}\label{sec:preserving-compare}
Repeated lookup now has a comparison operation that restores the query and
clears the candidate.
A five-label TM2 uses three binary stacks and the field
parser's $\mathsf{Option}\,\mathsf{Bool}$ memory.
It moves query heads to scratch,
compares candidate heads through literal-bit branches, then restores the query
and clears the remaining candidate.
Equality is retained in finite control.
\companchor{PreservingCompareMachine}{PreservingCompareMachine.supports}

Let $S(\ell,x,y,z,v)$ denote label, query, candidate, scratch and finite memory,
and let $T$ be this program's actual fixed-halt TM2 step.
\begin{theorem}[Restored-query comparison, machine-checked]
For every pair of binary words $x,y$ and initial finite memory $v$, there is
$t\le 2|x|+|y|+3$ such that
\[
 T^t(S(\mathsf{scan},x,y,[],v))
 = S(\mathsf{halt},x,[],[],\mathsf{some}(\mathsf{decide}(x=y))).
\]
The original query is restored and candidate/scratch are empty on both outcomes.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PreservingCompareMachine}{PreservingCompareMachine.run}

The scan invariant permits a consumed query prefix in scratch.
Restoration
prepends its reversal to the untouched query suffix, including after an early
mismatch.
Clearing the candidate tail prepares the next call's workspace.
\companchor{PreservingCompareMachine}{PreservingCompareMachine.scan_run}
For the original canonical encodings of arbitrary full keys $a,b$, assuming
nonzero $p,q$, injectivity gives the flag $\mathsf{some}(\mathsf{decide}(a=b))$
and bound $3\,\mathsf{keyRecordBitBound}(p)+3$.
\companchor{PreservingCompareMachine}{PreservingCompareMachine.key_run}

\paragraph{Concrete parser layout.}
The existing six parser stacks are extended with one query stack.
Candidate
uses the old output position and the comparison buffer uses scratch.
The exact
stack-layout theorem derives preservation of the input suffix, collected count,
outer count and archive at the same fuel.
No frame-preservation premise is
supplied by the caller.
Output and scratch are empty at return.
\companchor{PreservingCompareMachine}{PreservingCompareMachine.parser_run}
\companchor{PreservingCompareMachine}{PreservingCompareMachine.parser_supports}

Four Lean controls cover restored nonpalindromic equality, early mismatch with
a longer candidate tail, both strict-prefix directions, and literal surrounding
parser data.
Each asserts the full halted state.
Independent tuple equality
and original input snapshots check all 16,129 pairs with word lengths at most
six.
Mutants omit query restoration or ignore a trailing candidate; both are
detected, with zero failures or discards.
\companchor{PreservingCompareMachine}{PreservingCompareMachine.equal_control}
\companchor{PreservingCompareMachine}{PreservingCompareMachine.mismatch_control}
\companchor{PreservingCompareMachine}{PreservingCompareMachine.prefix_control}
\companchor{PreservingCompareMachine}{PreservingCompareMachine.parser_frame_control}

Section~\ref{sec:cache-key-field} connects the original entry header/key parser
to comparison; Section~\ref{sec:cache-entry} adds complete answer framing.

\subsection{Historical component: Executing the original entry header and key}\label{sec:cache-key-field}
One seven-stack TM2 checks the original pair count
$E(2)=[1,1,0,0,1]$, executes the existing key-field parser, and calls the
preserving comparator.
The five header checks execute literal-bit branches;
no input header is dropped outside the run.
Parser return checking and final
halt are also charged.
Successful workspace is derived from the parser's run.
\companchor{CacheKeyFieldMachine}{CacheKeyFieldMachine.header_run}
\companchor{CacheKeyFieldMachine}{CacheKeyFieldMachine.run}

Use $I(x,w,o,a)$ for this program's start with query $x$, input $w$, outer count
$o$, archive $a$ and empty workspace.
Its semantic prefix decoder $D$ uses the
original natural-prefix reader, requires count two, then uses the original field
decoder to obtain key $k$ and suffix $s$.
For halted $c$, readout $O(c)$ is none
when finite memory is none, and some $(b,\text{input}(c))$ when memory is some $b$.
\begin{theorem}[All-raw-prefix comparison, machine-checked]
For all binary words $x,w,o,a$, with $L=|w|$, there exists
\[
 t\le 3L+17+(L+1)(2L+4)+2|x|
\]
such that the actual TM2 run $c=T^t(I(x,w,o,a))$ is halted,
\[
 O(c)=\mathsf{Option.map}\,
       (\lambda(k,s).\,(\mathsf{decide}(x=k),s))\,(D(w)),
\]
and query, outer count and archive remain $x,o,a$.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheKeyFieldMachine}{CacheKeyFieldMachine.total_run}
Malformed header/key-field framing returns none; a valid unequal key returns
some false with its residual suffix.
Wrong/truncated count headers halt within
five steps.
For valid framing, count, scratch and output are empty on return.
\companchor{CacheKeyFieldMachine}{CacheKeyFieldMachine.header_rejected}
\companchor{CacheKeyFieldMachine}{CacheKeyFieldMachine.rejected_run}

\paragraph{Original typed cache entries.}
For arbitrary full query/entry keys $x,k$ and scalar answer $r$, with nonzero
$p,q$, start on the original \code{ballotCacheEntryBitCodec} encoding of $(k,r)$.
Execution returns the encoded query, equality flag $\mathsf{some}(\mathsf{decide}(x=k))$, empty
workspace and exactly the scalar-answer frame
$E(|\mathsf{scalarEncode}(r)|)\mathbin{+\!+}\mathsf{scalarEncode}(r)$ as residual
input.
The existing key codec derives, for $K=\mathsf{keyRecordBitBound}(p)$,
\[
 t\le 3\,\mathsf{size}(K)+17+K(2\,\mathsf{size}(K)+4)+2K.
\]
\companchor{CacheKeyFieldMachine}{CacheKeyFieldMachine.entry_run}

\subsection{Historical component: Complete entry framing and retained comparison}\label{sec:cache-entry}
The key operation now returns to an answer parser in the same seven-stack TM2.
Match/mismatch is retained in a finite control label while local memory is
reset and reused.
Failed answer framing or nonempty residual input halts invalid.
A successful answer parse returns the earlier comparison bit and original answer
bytes.
Both match and mismatch require a complete entry.
\companchor{CacheEntryMachine}{CacheEntryMachine.answer_run}
\companchor{CacheEntryMachine}{CacheEntryMachine.answer_rejected}

Let $D(w)$ be the original \code{bitFieldsDecode} followed by its exactly-two-fields
case, returning $(k,a)$; other cases return none.
The sequential execution
specification is proved equal to $D$, including rejection and full consumption.
\companchor{CacheEntryMachine}{CacheEntryMachine.decode_original}
Write $I(x,w,o,r)$ for start with query $x$, entry input $w$, outer counter $o$,
archive $r$ and empty workspace.
Halted readout $O$ is some $(b,\text{output})$
when memory contains some $b$, and none for invalid memory.
\begin{theorem}[Complete raw entry framing, machine-checked]
For all binary words $x,w,o,r$, with $L=|w|$, some
\[
 t\le 6L+26+2(L+1)(2L+4)+2|x|
\]
reaches an actual halted TM2 configuration $c=T^t(I(x,w,o,r))$ with
\[
 O(c)=\mathsf{Option.map}\,
   (\lambda(k,a).\,(\mathsf{decide}(x=k),a))\,(D(w)),
\]
retaining query $x$, outer counter $o$ and archive $r$.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheEntryMachine}{CacheEntryMachine.total_run}

For an original two-field encoding, the complete terminal state has empty
source/count/scratch, original answer bytes in output and the correct comparison
bit.
For lengths $X=|x|,K=|k|,A=|a|$, define its structured bound
\[
\begin{aligned}
 C(X,K,A)={}&3\,\mathsf{size}(K)+3\,\mathsf{size}(A)+26+2X\\
           &+K(2\,\mathsf{size}(K)+4)+A(2\,\mathsf{size}(A)+3).
\end{aligned}
\]
\companchor{CacheEntryMachine}{CacheEntryMachine.run}
For nonzero $p,q$, the typed entry instance returns the canonical scalar answer
and correct key-equality flag within $C(K_p,K_p,A_q)$ transitions.
Here
$K_p=\mathsf{keyRecordBitBound}(p)$ and $A_q=\mathsf{groupRecordBitBound}(q)$.
\companchor{CacheEntryMachine}{CacheEntryMachine.entry_run}

Independent strict indexed decoding checks 57,337 raw query/input pairs
(input length at most twelve, query length at most two) and 4,725 structured
cases.
Overwritten-comparison, ignored-tail and skipped-answer mutants are
detected; failures and discards are zero.
Five Lean controls retain answer/frame
data, mismatch through answer success, empty versus absent answer framing, and
trailing rejection.
\companchor{CacheEntryMachine}{CacheEntryMachine.mismatch_control}
\companchor{CacheEntryMachine}{CacheEntryMachine.empty_answer_control}
\companchor{CacheEntryMachine}{CacheEntryMachine.missing_answer_control}

\subsection{Historical component: Complete lookup in source-generated caches}\label{sec:cache-lookup}
One eight-stack TM2 copies the original cache, reads its binary outer count,
and repeats the existing field and entry operations.
Relocation preserves the
unprocessed tail during each entry call.
A mismatch clears the answer and
executes binary decrement before another iteration; a hit returns the encoded
scalar.
The query and entire original cache survive, including entries after an
early hit.
The repeated run equals the original \code{List.dlookup}; every
successor workspace is derived from execution.
Readout $O$ distinguishes invalid
none, a miss some none, and a hit some (some answer bits).
\companchor{CacheLookupMachine}{CacheLookupMachine.loop_run}

Write $K=\mathsf{keyRecordBitBound}(p)$, $A=\mathsf{groupRecordBitBound}(q)$,
$E=\mathsf{cacheEntryBitBound}(p,q)$ and use the preceding entry bound $C$.
Define
\[
\begin{aligned}
 F&=3\,\mathsf{size}(E)+7+E(2\,\mathsf{size}(E)+3),\\
 I(N)&=F+C(K,K,A)+A+2\,\mathsf{size}(N)+6.
\end{aligned}
\]
\begin{theorem}[Executed typed-cache lookup, machine-checked]
Let $p,q$ be nonzero, $H$ a typed finite cache with $N$ entries, and $x$ a full
query key. Let $w$ and $u$ be their original codec encodings, with $W=|w|$.
Starting on $u,w$ and empty workspace, some
\[
 t\le 2W+3\,\mathsf{size}(N)+N I(N)+8
\]
reaches an actually halted configuration $c$ satisfying
\[
 O(c)=\mathsf{some}\bigl(\mathsf{Option.map}\,
            \mathsf{scalarEncode}\,(H.\mathsf{lookup}(x))\bigr),
 \qquad \mathsf{query}(c)=u,\quad \mathsf{saved}(c)=w.
\]
The run includes the original copy, count parse and all control transfers.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheLookupMachine}{CacheLookupMachine.run}

For prime $p,q$, actual supported repaired-source executions with per-view
attacker hash-query bound $n$ derive $N\le n+15$ and
$W\le B=\mathsf{cacheRecordBitBound}(p,q,n+15)$.
The same machine then has bound
\[
 2B+3\,\mathsf{size}(n+15)+(n+15)I(n+15)+8.
\]
No caller supplies a frame, cache-size or execution-cost certificate.
\companchor{CacheLookupMachine}{CacheLookupMachine.source_cache_run}

Independent fixtures check 3,845 ordered caches/queries and four malformed
frames.
Missing-copy, first-entry-only and lost-query mutants are detected;
failures/discards are zero.
Lean controls retain head/late hits (answers five
and three), nonempty/empty misses, and actual malformed rejection.
\companchor{CacheLookupMachine}{CacheLookupMachine.early_hit_control}
\companchor{CacheLookupMachine}{CacheLookupMachine.late_hit_control}

\companchor{CacheLookupMachine}{CacheLookupMachine.empty_control}

An early hit need not validate later raw

\subsection{Historical component: Writing original field and count prefixes}\label{sec:frame-writer}
Cache insertion needs new length prefixes.
The writer computes them from loaded
payload bits, using the original $E(n)=1^{\mathsf{size}(n)}0\,\mathsf{bits}(n)$
format.
It does not receive a freely constructed complete entry.

\paragraph{Executed binary increment.}
A two-label TM2 uses the existing four binary stacks for carry and restoration.
For every $n$, source and frame, some $t\le 2\,\mathsf{size}(n)+2$ changes the
counter from $\mathsf{bits}(n)$ to $\mathsf{bits}(n+1)$, clears scratch and reaches
halted success while preserving both other stacks.
The digit-successor law is
proved from the original natural bits representation.
\companchor{BitIncrementMachine}{BitIncrementMachine.succBits_bits}
\companchor{BitIncrementMachine}{BitIncrementMachine.run}

\paragraph{Complete framing writer.}
One five-stack TM2 counts collected payload bits through linked increments,
preserving source, buffered payload and destination.
It restores payload order,
writes the little-endian counter digits, then the delimiter and width ones.
All callbacks use finite local data; each returned workspace is derived.
\begin{theorem}[Executed field construction, machine-checked]
For all payload words $w$ and destination suffixes $s$, let $L=|w|$. Starting
with payload $w$, destination $s$ and empty workspace, some
\[
 t\le L(2\,\mathsf{size}(L)+5)+3\,\mathsf{size}(L)+5
\]
reaches a halted successful configuration with output
\[
 E(L)\mathbin{+\!+}w\mathbin{+\!+}s
\]
and every other stack empty. The payload length is computed by the machine.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{FrameWriteMachine}{FrameWriteMachine.run}

The prefix entry point writes $E(n)\mathbin{+\!+}s$ from canonical
$\mathsf{bits}(n)$ in exactly $3\,\mathsf{size}(n)+3$ transitions, again clearing
workspace.
\companchor{FrameWriteMachine}{FrameWriteMachine.prefix_run}
For nonzero moduli, the existing full-key/scalar codecs derive the field-writer
bounds by substituting $K_p=\mathsf{keyRecordBitBound}(p)$ or
$A_q=\mathsf{groupRecordBitBound}(q)$ for $L$ in the monotone bound above.
\companchor{FrameWriteMachine}{FrameWriteMachine.key_field_run}
\companchor{FrameWriteMachine}{FrameWriteMachine.scalar_field_run}

Eight Lean controls cover carry, zero, nonpalindromic payload/suffix data,
length-boundary behavior and the required empty-field delimiter.
Independent
integer framing and stack execution check 1,028 increments and 6,141 fields,
including 257-bit carry chains.
Broken carry, reversed payload and omitted
delimiter mutants are detected; failures/discards are zero.
\companchor{BitIncrementMachine}{BitIncrementMachine.carry_control}
\companchor{FrameWriteMachine}{FrameWriteMachine.field_control}
\companchor{FrameWriteMachine}{FrameWriteMachine.boundary_control}
\companchor{FrameWriteMachine}{FrameWriteMachine.empty_not_omitted}

\subsection{Historical component: Guarded insertion in source-generated caches}\label{sec:cache-insert}
One nine-stack TM2 links lookup, count parsing/increment, operand copying and
framing.
An occupied key retains the original cache.
On a miss, the machine
constructs the key/scalar pair, frames it before the old tail and writes the
incremented count.
Lookup derives freshness and six empty work stacks.
\companchor{CacheLookupMachine}{CacheLookupMachine.miss_run}

\begin{theorem}[Executed guarded insertion, machine-checked]
Let $p,q\ne0$, $H$ be any typed finite cache, $x$ any typed full key and
$a\in\mathbb Z_q$. Write $N=|H.\mathsf{entries}|$, $W=|\mathsf{encode}(H)|$,
and use the existing key, scalar and entry bounds $K_p,A_q,E_{p,q}$.
With $I(N)$ the lookup iteration bound and $J(L)=L(2\,\mathsf{size}(L)+5)+3\,\mathsf{size}(L)+5$, define
\[
 \begin{split}
 D(N)={}&5\,\mathsf{size}(N)+3\,\mathsf{size}(N+1)+2A_q+2K_p\\
       &+J(A_q)+J(K_p)+J(E_{p,q})+21.
 \end{split}
\]
From the encoded cache/key/scalar and empty workspace, some
$t\le 2W+3\,\mathsf{size}(N)+NI(N)+9+D(N)$ reaches an actually halted
configuration retaining the original key and scalar, with readout
\[
 \begin{cases}
 \mathsf{some}(\mathsf{true},\mathsf{encode}(H.\mathsf{insert}(x,a))),
          &H.\mathsf{lookup}(x)=\mathsf{none},\\
 \mathsf{some}(\mathsf{false},\mathsf{encode}(H)),&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
The bound charges all linked calls and control transfers.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheInsertMachine}{CacheInsertMachine.run}

For prime $p,q$, support in the actual repaired source started at the empty
cache/log and per-view hash budget $n$ derive $N\le n+15$ and
$W\le\mathsf{cacheRecordBitBound}(p,q,n+15)$; substituting these gives the
source bound without supplied freshness, frame or cost certificates.
\companchor{CacheInsertMachine}{CacheInsertMachine.source_cache_run}
For the actual programmed source, arbitrary state and supplied transcript,
the resulting cache agrees exactly and its sticky flag is
$\mathsf{oldBad}\lor\neg\mathsf{inserted}$.
Occupied keys raise the flag even
when challenges agree.
This is the cache/flag projection.
\companchor{CacheInsertMachine}{CacheInsertMachine.programmed_cache_run}

Independent fixtures check 11,535 canonical and four malformed cases; five
count, field-order, tail-loss and occupied-key mutants are detected, with zero
failures/discards.
Lean controls retain literal prepend order, empty insertion,
disagreeing/agreeing collisions and malformed rejection.
\companchor{CacheInsertMachine}{CacheInsertMachine.prepend_control}
\companchor{CacheInsertMachine}{CacheInsertMachine.head_collision_control}
\companchor{CacheInsertMachine}{CacheInsertMachine.agreeing_collision_control}
\companchor{CacheInsertMachine}{CacheInsertMachine.empty_control}
\companchor{CacheInsertMachine}{CacheInsertMachine.malformed_controls}

The scalar is already loaded.

\subsection{Historical component: Source request scheduling through executed cache operations}\label{sec:cache-hash}
The driver runs lookup before requesting entropy.
A hit returns the stored
scalar and old cache/log words.
A miss requests the original wrapped challenge,
writes its scalar prefix, runs guarded insertion and executes log append.
Explicit machine failure
returns none.
Insertion repeats lookup deterministically; it adds no request.

\paragraph{Executable fuel.}
For loaded cache word $w$, put $W=|w|$.
Insertion uses its bound $C(p,q,W,W)$;
parsed lookup uses the bound of Section~\ref{sec:cache-read} at $(p,q,W,W)$.
Original framing derives $N\le W$.
Monotonicity and fixed halted states give
exact readouts at these fuels for all typed caches and operands, with nonzero
$p,q$.
No caller supplies the entry count, freshness or fuel.
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.probe_encode}
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.insert_encode}

\paragraph{The original handler.}
The driver hash branch equals the original logged hash handler mapped to an
optional encoded result.
This equality retains the entire oracle request tree.
Hits execute prefix
parsing and then use the original binary-value interpretation law; misses
suspend for the original challenge before the next deterministic run.
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.hash_eq}
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.hash_hit}
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.hash_miss}

\begin{theorem}[Every source program, machine-checked]
Let $p,q\ne0$, $A$ be any result type, $P$ any original ballot-oracle program
returning $A$, and $(H,L)$ any typed initial cache and ordered miss log.
Write $E_H,E_L$ for their original codecs and define
$\varphi(a,H',L')=\mathsf{some}(a,E_H(H'),E_L(L'))$.
Then, as an equality of oracle computations,
\[
 \mathsf{driver}(P,E_H(H),E_L(L))
 =\mathsf{map}\;\varphi\;
       \mathsf{runBallotFiniteLogged}(P,(H,L)).
\]
Thus every continuation receives the original answer and exact successor state;
canonical source executions cannot produce the driver's failure result.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.run_eq}
The driver uses VCVio's existing computation recursor.
Uniform queries keep
their original range and response.
The induction derives successor encodings
without a caller-supplied correspondence or cache-validity invariant.

Four Lean controls retain literal hit/miss outputs and log order, exclude eager
hit resampling for every continuation, and exclude an immediate miss return
for every fabricated result.
Independent fixtures check 1,728 branches and
2,187 three-request sequences carrying actual returned cache words.
Four
sampling/log mutants are detected; failures/discards are zero.
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.hit_control}
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.miss_control}
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.hit_not_resampled}
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.miss_not_immediate}

OracleComp supplies suspension.
Section~\ref{sec:log-append} executes scalar
prefix writing and log append.
Loading/transfers, conversion to input digits,
numeric interpretation/range checking, fuel computation and continuation execution remain host operations
with unproved machine costs.
This is not a whole oracle TM or a PPT theorem.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Chronological log append and scalar-prefix execution}\label{sec:log-append}
The log codec erases Unit tags and writes a count followed by framed full keys.
One eight-stack TM2 parses and increments that count, copies/frames the new key,
transfers the old body before it in order, then writes the new count prefix.

\begin{theorem}[Executed chronological append, machine-checked]
For every list of bit words $S$ and key word $k$, put $N=|S|$, $K=|k|$,
$B=|\mathsf{bitFramesEncode}(S)|$ and let $J$ be the existing field-writer cost.
Starting with $k$, the original record encoding of $S$ and empty workspace,
some run of at most
\[
 5\,\mathsf{size}(N)+3\,\mathsf{size}(N+1)+2K+J(K)+2B+17
\]
transitions halts successfully with output $\mathsf{bitFieldsEncode}(S+[k])$,
retained key $k$ and every other stack empty. Every linked call and transfer
is included in this bound.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{LogAppendMachine}{LogAppendMachine.run}
For nonzero $p,q$, the typed instance uses the original log codec and key bound.
For prime $p,q$, actual supported repaired-source runs from empty cache/log with
per-view hash budget $n$ derive $N\le n+15$ and log length at most the existing
$\mathsf{bitListSize}(K_p,n+15)$.
No caller supplies a log invariant or fuel.
\companchor{LogAppendMachine}{LogAppendMachine.log_run}
\companchor{LogAppendMachine}{LogAppendMachine.source_log_run}

\paragraph{Scalar prefix from loaded digits.}
For nonzero $q$ and any $a\in\mathbb Z_q$, the existing prefix writer takes
$\mathsf{bits}(a.\mathsf{val})$ and suffix $s$ to
$\mathsf{scalarEncode}(a)\mathbin{+\!+}s$, clears workspace and halts in at most
$3\,\mathsf{size}(q-1)+3$ transitions.
No new scalar machine is needed.
\companchor{FrameWriteMachine}{FrameWriteMachine.scalar_prefix_run}
\companchor{FrameWriteMachine}{FrameWriteMachine.scalar_prefix_control}
\companchor{FrameWriteMachine}{FrameWriteMachine.scalar_zero_control}

\paragraph{Driver integration and controls.}
The existing driver now keeps the log encoded, executes this append and runs
the scalar prefix writer before insertion.
Fixed-fuel readout laws preserve
the full source theorem in Section~\ref{sec:cache-hash}.
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.append_encode}
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.scalarWord_encode}
Five append controls retain literal chronological/repeated keys, empty-field
framing, nonpalindromic transfer, exclusion of prepend and count-width growth.
\companchor{LogAppendMachine}{LogAppendMachine.order_control}
\companchor{LogAppendMachine}{LogAppendMachine.empty_control}
\companchor{LogAppendMachine}{LogAppendMachine.order_bits_control}
\companchor{LogAppendMachine}{LogAppendMachine.not_prepend_control}

Independent campaigns check 3,905 records, six count boundaries, 108 scalar
prefixes, 1,728 driver branches and 2,187 request triples.
Respectively five,
