\subsection{Historical component: Parsing the actual cached answer}\label{sec:cache-read}
A cache hit leaves its encoded answer in the archive stack.
The complete scan
now derives empty parser workspace, retaining the unread cache tail, entry
counter, query and saved original cache.
The existing prefix parser is relocated
onto that archive in the same eight-stack program; no answer reload is used.
\companchor{CacheLookupMachine}{CacheLookupMachine.hit_run}

\begin{theorem}[Executed cached-answer parsing, machine-checked]
For nonzero $p,q$, every original typed cache $C$ and full query $k$, let
$N=|C.\mathsf{entries}|$ and $W=|\mathsf{encode}(C)|$. Starting with their
original encoded words, some run of at most
\[
 2W+3\,\mathsf{size}(N)+N\,\mathsf{iterationCost}(p,q,N)
     +3\,\mathsf{size}(q-1)+13
\]
transitions halts with readout
$\mathsf{some}(C.\mathsf{lookup}(k).\mathsf{map}(a\mapsto
\mathsf{bits}(a.\mathsf{val})))$, retaining the exact query and saved cache.
The machine itself distinguishes hits from misses and requires full scalar-field
consumption. A zero answer is a hit with empty digits.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheReadMachine}{CacheReadMachine.run}
For prime $p,q$, supported repaired-source runs from empty cache/log and per-view
hash budget $n$ derive $N\le n+15$ and $W\le B(p,q,n+15)$.
Cost monotonicity
also supplies the driver's fixed fuel from the actual loaded cache length.
\companchor{CacheReadMachine}{CacheReadMachine.source_cache_run}
\companchor{CacheReadMachine}{CacheReadMachine.cost_mono}

\paragraph{Driver and controls.}
The existing driver's probe now executes this parsing.
Its typed boundary uses
the original binary-value interpretation and range check; the exact source
query-tree theorem in Section~\ref{sec:cache-hash} remains checked.
\companchor{UniformOperandCodec}{bitsValue_bits}
\companchor{CacheHashHandler}{CacheHashHandler.scalarValue_bits}
Four Lean controls retain nonpalindromic scalar six, zero distinct from absence,
a different-query miss and malformed/trailing scalar rejection.
\companchor{CacheReadMachine}{CacheReadMachine.hit_control}
\companchor{CacheReadMachine}{CacheReadMachine.zero_control}
\companchor{CacheReadMachine}{CacheReadMachine.miss_control}
\companchor{CacheReadMachine}{CacheReadMachine.malformed_controls}
Independent fixtures check 3,845 canonical cases and four malformed scalar
records, detecting five parsing/frame mutations.
Updated driver fixtures retain
1,728 branches and 2,187 request triples with four detected sampling/log

Numeric interpretation/range checking, initial digit loading, remaining physical

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Guarded subtraction for scalar range reduction}\label{sec:binary-subtract}
The existing fair-bit sampler applies modulus reduction as host arithmetic.
Binary long division
maintains $r<q$, so the next value $2r+b<2q$ needs at most one subtraction.
Repeated subtraction from the entire sampled integer would not give a
polynomial bit bound.
This section supplies the required local operation.

\paragraph{Loaded-word subtraction.}
For all little-endian words $x,y$, a six-stack finite-control program halts
within $3(|x|+|y|)+5$ transitions.
If their numeric values satisfy $Y\le X$,
it returns canonical digits of $X-Y$ with success true.
Otherwise it returns
the exact original word $x$ with success false.
Both branches retain $y$ and
clear all other workspace.
Thus noncanonical high zeros and underflow have
explicit behavior; no numeric comparison is performed by a host callback.
\companchor{BinarySubtractMachine}{BinarySubtractMachine.run}

\begin{theorem}[Executed remainder update, machine-checked]
For natural numbers $r,q$ and a bit $b$, assume $r<q$. Starting with canonical
loaded words $\mathsf{bits}(r)$ and $\mathsf{bits}(q)$, the same program executes
the shift, including its zero case, then guarded subtraction. Some run of at
most $6\,\mathsf{size}(q)+9$ transitions halts with canonical output
\[
 \mathsf{bits}\bigl((2r+\mathsf{toNat}(b))\bmod q\bigr),
\]
retained modulus digits, empty workspace and flag
$\mathsf{decide}(q\le 2r+\mathsf{toNat}(b))$.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BinarySubtractMachine}{BinarySubtractMachine.remainder_step}
The proof derives borrow from a binary-value conservation identity and removes
high zero bits on successful subtraction.
Its flag says whether subtraction
was taken, not whether the division update succeeded.

Six Lean controls retain a long borrow chain, underflow with padded input,
equal numeric values of different widths, nonpalindromic output, the update
$17\bmod 11=6$ and the canonical zero shift.
\companchor{BinarySubtractMachine}{BinarySubtractMachine.borrow_control}
\companchor{BinarySubtractMachine}{BinarySubtractMachine.underflow_control}
\companchor{BinarySubtractMachine}{BinarySubtractMachine.equal_control}
\companchor{BinarySubtractMachine}{BinarySubtractMachine.order_control}
\companchor{BinarySubtractMachine}{BinarySubtractMachine.remainder_control}
\companchor{BinarySubtractMachine}{BinarySubtractMachine.zero_control}
Independent integer fixtures check 16,129 raw operand pairs through six bits,
20 boundary cases through 258 bits and 4,160 remainder updates with $1\le q\le64$.
Five borrow/order/modulus/equality/underflow mutations are detected;
failures/discards are zero.

This is one division update.
This result neither changes the sampler law nor removes
its statistical loss, and it is not a computational ballot-secrecy theorem.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: One proof oracle for the sampled election}\label{sec:election-oracle}
The computational game now uses one oracle interface for ballot, public-key and
decryption proof challenges.
Its three tagged request types retain the original
arguments:
\[
 \mathsf B(s,a),\qquad \mathsf K(g,pk,a),\qquad
 \mathsf D(g,pk,ct,d,(a,b)).
\]
Here $s$ is the full ballot statement, $a$ its proof commitments in the first
case, and $d$ the decryption share in the last.
The tags represent the existing
three typed hash functions; no concrete byte-domain-separation theorem is
claimed.
The public fingerprint remains its original parameter function.
\companchor{ElectionOracle}{ElectionOracle.Key}
\companchor{ElectionOracle}{ElectionOracle.functionImpl}

An attacker receives the original prefix before casting and the complete result
before guessing, with private state between these callbacks.
Both callbacks
can query this oracle.
Preparation can also query before election sampling and
save state for the callbacks.
The original random draw order, rejected input,
retained board, encrypted tally, shares, trustee proofs and decoding failures
are preserved.
\companchor{ElectionOracle}{ElectionOracle.Adversary}
\companchor{ElectionOracle}{ElectionOracle.world}
\companchor{ElectionOracle}{ElectionOracle.preparedGame}

\begin{theorem}[Sampled-game correspondence, machine-checked]
Let $F$ be a finite field and $G$ a decidable additive commutative group with an
$F$-module structure. Let $h$ be arbitrary original proof hashes and fingerprint,
$g\in G$, and $A$ any stateful oracle attacker. Write $\mathcal I_h$
for fixed-function interpretation, and $A_h$ for interpretation of both
callbacks. Then, as probabilistic oracle computations,
\[
 \mathcal I_h(\mathsf{world}(h_{fp},g,A,v))
   =\mathsf{repairedBallotWorld}(h,g,A_h,v),
\]
and the same equality holds for the sampled guessing game. For any preparation
$P$, the prepared game interprets to sampling $i$ from $\mathcal I_h(P)$
and then executing the original repaired game with $(A(i))_h$.
\end{theorem}

\companchor{ElectionOracle}{ElectionOracle.world_function}
\companchor{ElectionOracle}{ElectionOracle.game_function}
\companchor{ElectionOracle}{ElectionOracle.preparedGame_function}
\companchor{ElectionOracle}{ElectionOracle.Adversary.interpret_ofOriginal}

With a \code{SampleableType F} instance, random interpretation uses the pinned
lazy oracle and one private cache.
A hit returns its saved answer; a miss
samples and inserts an answer at the full tagged key.
Continuations receive
the actual successor cache, including across preparation and election phases.
\code{randomGame} initializes this cache once and exposes only the winning bit
to the outer experiment; attacker observations remain the callback inputs.

For $p=23,q=11,g=2,pk=8$, proof nonce four gives key commitment 16;
the decryption request uses $ct=(3,13)$, share 4 and commitments $(16,12)$.
Challenge five gives response eight.
Changing the response to nine violates
both modular equations.
Lean checks the corresponding exponent queries and
transcripts, as well as hashes that distinguish changed public-key/share
coordinates.
These small arithmetic fixtures carry no hardness claim.

\paragraph{Component scope.}
This result establishes the sampled interface correspondence for the three-voter,
two-candidate, one-honest-trustee schedule.
It does not supply the general
challenge/corruption/election-family policy, oracle-aware attack instances,
full adaptive extraction, or a random-oracle secrecy reduction.
The full cryptographic reduction and its external implementation assumptions
are stated in the accompanying \href{main.pdf}{formal reference}.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: The actual prefix after earlier oracle queries}\label{sec:election-cache}
The ballot runtime can now be used inside the full election without resetting
earlier answers.
Let $\pi(C)$ project a full cache to its ballot entries, and
$R(C,B)$ replace only those entries by $B$, retaining the key/decryption entries.
For every ballot-oracle computation $M$ and full initial cache $C$,
\[
 \mathsf{run}(\mathsf{liftBallot}(M),C)=
 \bigl((x,B)\mapsto(x,R(C,B))\bigr)_*
 \mathsf{runBallotOracle}(M,\pi(C)).
\]
This is exact computation equality, including final state.
Projection,
reconstruction and fresh insertion are proved; the caller supplies no empty
cache, freshness or frame invariant.
Only decidable group equality and the
scalar sampler are required for this runtime interface.
\companchor{ElectionCache}{ElectionCache.liftBallot_run}
\companchor{ElectionCache}{ElectionCache.replace_project}
\companchor{ElectionCache}{ElectionCache.replace_update}

\begin{theorem}[Actual prefix rejection, machine-checked]
Under the finite-field/module assumptions above, let
$r\mapsto r\mathbin{\cdot}g$ be injective. The full prefix accepts both honest
ballots for collision-free historical coins, for every initial cache. With the
original nonzero coin sampler, any pre-election oracle computation $P$, any
initial cache $C$ and either challenge bit,
\[
 \Pr[\mathsf{honestDecisions}\ne(\mathsf{accepted},\mathsf{accepted})
       \mid\mathsf{run}(P;\mathsf{samplePrefix},C)]
 \le \frac{9}{|F|-1}.
\]
The generator, fingerprint and challenge bit are fixed inputs to
\code{samplePrefix}; all secret, key-proof, decryption-proof and honest-ballot
coins are sampled in the original order. Secret material in its result remains
challenger-local. Exact factoring of \code{ElectionOracle.world} connects this
prefix to the original attacker callbacks and trustee finish.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionCache}{ElectionCache.prefix_accepts}
\companchor{ElectionCache}{ElectionCache.world_eq_samplePrefix}
\companchor{ElectionCache}{ElectionCache.samplePrefix_rejection_le}
\companchor{ElectionCache}{ElectionCache.preparedPrefix_rejection_le}

Independent finite fixtures cover 729 cache states, 2,916 ballot-response
branches and 1,728 mixed three-request traces.
Four reset/erasure/overwrite
mutations are detected, with zero failures or discards.
Lean controls show that
resetting a saved ballot answer changes its distribution, while a ballot miss
retains the other proof domains.
In the 101-element field, the full sampled
prefix after an actual prior query has rejection probability at most $9/100$
and strictly positive acceptance probability.
The historical fixed-hash
collision fixture still rejects.
\companchor{ElectionCacheControls}{ElectionCacheControls.resetting_changes_answer}
\companchor{ElectionCacheControls}{ElectionCacheControls.miss_preserves_other_proofs}
\companchor{ElectionCacheControls}{ElectionCacheControls.rejection_nontrivial}
\companchor{ElectionCacheControls}{ElectionCacheControls.acceptance_positive}
\companchor{ElectionCacheControls}{ElectionCacheControls.original_rejection_retained}

This closes the real-prefix cache/acceptance interface.
No such
correspondence, standard-PPT adequacy or secrecy theorem is assumed.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Full-source replay and the actual submitted ballot}\label{sec:election-replay}
The full election can use the existing ballot replay engine without a second
fork implementation.
Every ballot request, including preparation and the final
attacker continuation, remains explicit.
Key/decryption proof requests instead
use a local lazy cache: hits retain their answer, and misses draw ordinary
uniform randomness.
Auxiliary draws are not ballot fork challenges.

Let $L(M,A)$ lower a full-oracle computation $M$ with auxiliary cache $A$,
and let $R(A,B)$ reconstruct a full cache from auxiliary and ballot entries.
With decidable group equality and the scalar sampler, for every $M,A,B$,
\[
 \bigl((x,A',B')\mapsto(x,R(A',B'))\bigr)_*
 \mathsf{runBallotOracle}(L(M,A),B)
 =\mathsf{run}(M,R(A,B)).
\]
This is exact computation equality with final state.
Its proof derives the
required domain/update conditions.
Lowering the entire source from empty state
preserves its full returned value; the existing ballot-only sources retain
exactly their query trees.
No final cache substitutes for earlier history.
\companchor{ElectionReplaySource}{ElectionReplaySource.lower_run}
\companchor{ElectionReplaySource}{ElectionReplaySource.source_runtime}
\companchor{ElectionReplaySource}{ElectionReplaySource.source_mem}
\companchor{ElectionReplaySource}{ElectionReplaySource.source_liftBallot}

\begin{theorem}[Actual-election conditional extraction, machine-checked]\label{thm:actual-election-extraction}
Let $F$ be a finite field with its sampler, $G$ an additive $F$-module, both
with decidable equality, and $g\ne0$. Fix any fingerprint, pre-election
computation $P$, stateful full-oracle adversary $\mathcal A$ and replay parameter
$n\in\mathbb N$. Let $E$ be \code{ElectionExtraction.preparedSource}, which
retains the actual public result alongside the winning bit. Erasing that
record gives exactly the original \code{ElectionOracle.preparedGame} query
computation, including preparation and the final guessing continuation.

If \code{ElectionExtraction.joint} returns $(o,w)$ in its support, where
$o=(V,b)$, let $S_i$ be the covered statement for component $i$ (or the
implicit aggregate for $i=\mathsf{none}$) of $V.\mathsf{submission}$, with
generator $g$ and the public key in $V.\mathsf{beforeTally}$. Then
\[
\begin{gathered}
 V.\mathsf{decision}=\mathsf{accepted},\\
 \forall i\in\{\mathsf{some}(0),\mathsf{some}(1),\mathsf{none}\},\quad
 S_i.\mathsf{Witnesses}(w_i),\\
 \exists C,\quad (o,C)\in\operatorname{support}(\mathsf{run}(E,\varnothing)).
\end{gathered}
\]
Thus all three witnesses concern one actual submitted ballot and its public
key in a complete original election execution. If $r\mapsto r\cdot g$ is
injective and $(2:F)\ne0$, their nonces sum consistently and their component
bits sum, as natural numbers, to the aggregate bit and to at most one.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionExtraction}{ElectionExtraction.worldSource_project}
\companchor{ElectionExtraction}{ElectionExtraction.preparedSource_project}
\companchor{ElectionExtraction}{ElectionExtraction.joint_valid}
\companchor{ElectionExtraction}{ElectionExtraction.joint_consistent}

The single and joint algorithms reuse the existing source extractors.
Rejected
records select an impossible first-branch proof, so acceptance is derived from
successful extraction rather than required of the caller.
\companchor{ElectionReplaySource}{ElectionReplaySource.extract_valid}
\companchor{ElectionReplaySource}{ElectionReplaySource.jointExtract_valid}

\paragraph{Controls and remaining probability argument.}
The mixed sequence queries a ballot proof, key proof, decryption proof, key
proof and ballot proof.
It retains both ballot requests and draws only twice
for the auxiliary misses.
Lean pins its exact
query tree and excludes a pure constant source.
Existing positive single/joint
replay fixtures embed unchanged; the unqueried live-selector control remains
zero.
These positive fixtures cover their embedded prover programs, not
arbitrary full-election attackers.
Independent fixtures cover 20,736 traces
and one directed five-request trace, detecting four history/cache/domain
mutations with zero failures or discards.

and extraction probability.
The real source in this component samples the secret key and honest witnesses, so it is not a reduction given only a DDH challenge. The later simulation comparisons preserve the complete public view and account for their losses.

\subsection{Historical component: Actual acceptance and joint replay probability}\label{sec:election-acceptance}
The preceding extractor now has a bound in terms of actual submission
acceptance in the complete real election.
The argument retains preparation,
key and decryption proofs, the public result and the final guessing callback.
Every full-oracle computation preserves previously cached answers.
Actual
submission acceptance therefore supplies three valid proofs in the final
cache, and exact full-source reconstruction transfers that certificate to the
replay cache.
The generator is derived from the actual setup.
\companchor{ElectionAcceptance}{ElectionAcceptance.run_cache_le}
\companchor{ElectionAcceptance}{ElectionAcceptance.world_cached}
\companchor{ElectionAcceptance}{ElectionAcceptance.prepared_cached}
\companchor{ElectionAcceptance}{ElectionAcceptance.source_cached}

\begin{theorem}[Real-election joint replay bound, machine-checked]
Let $F$ be a finite field with its sampler, $G$ an additive $F$-module, both
with decidable equality, and $g\ne0$. Fix the fingerprint, arbitrary preparation
$P$ and stateful full-oracle adversary $\mathcal A$. Suppose preparation makes
at most $n_p$ ballot requests, every casting callback makes at most $n_c$, and
every guessing callback makes at most $n_g$. These are structural bounds over
all callback inputs, not runtime or standard-PPT certificates. Define
\[
 N=n_p+n_c+n_g+15,\qquad
 a=\Pr[V.\mathsf{decision}=\mathsf{accepted}\mid
       \mathsf{run}(\mathsf{preparedSource},\varnothing)].
\]
For every $\delta\in\overline{\mathbb R}_{\ge0}$,
\[
 \bigl(a-3(N+1)\delta\bigr)
 \bigl(\delta-|F|^{-1}\bigr)^3
 \ \le\ \Pr[\mathsf{joint}(N)\ne\mathsf{none}].
\]
Subtraction is truncated nonnegative extended-real subtraction. The algorithm
is the actual \code{ElectionExtraction.joint}, with the successful-output
contract of Theorem~\ref{thm:actual-election-extraction}. Neither cache correspondence nor acceptance-to-selector
coverage is assumed.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionExtractionBound}{ElectionExtractionBound.joint_le}

The protocol contributes twelve ballot requests for the two honest constructors
and their validation, and at most three for the adversarial submission.
Key and
decryption requests contribute zero to this predicate.
Repeated ballot hits
still count as requests.
The full-source lowering preserves this bound.
\companchor{ElectionQueryBound}{ElectionQueryBound.prepared_bound}
\companchor{ElectionQueryBound}{ElectionQueryBound.source_bound}

For each original source path, all three proof selectors are enabled exactly
when its actual submitted ballot accepts.
Their simultaneous-selection
probability is therefore exactly $a$, on a common first execution.
The existing
joint replay theorem then charges the low-context loss $3(N+1)\delta$ and the
three equal-challenge losses in the displayed expression.
\companchor{ElectionExtractionBound}{ElectionExtractionBound.path_verified}
\companchor{ElectionExtractionBound}{ElectionExtractionBound.path_selector}
\companchor{ElectionExtractionBound}{ElectionExtractionBound.selection_probability}

\paragraph{Controls and boundary.}
An independently checked fresh-ballot verifier fixture remains valid after
arbitrary later full-domain queries; erasing its cache destroys that certificate.
Its preloaded cache is not an empty-cache reachability claim.
Mixed requests
require two ballot queries, so a budget of one fails.
A numerical control shows
that the bound can be positive; it does not assert acceptance probability one
for the actual election.
Independent modular fixtures cover 4,000 traces,
detect three cache/history mutants and refute the rejected-proof fallback,
with zero failures or discards.
\companchor{ElectionAcceptanceControls}{ElectionAcceptanceControls.late_queries_preserve_validity}
\companchor{ElectionAcceptanceControls}{ElectionAcceptanceControls.erased_cache_not_valid}
\companchor{ElectionAcceptanceControls}{ElectionAcceptanceControls.mixed_two_ballot_queries}
\companchor{ElectionAcceptanceControls}{ElectionAcceptanceControls.mixed_one_is_insufficient}
\companchor{ElectionAcceptanceControls}{ElectionAcceptanceControls.numerical_bound_positive}

This is a real-source replay bound.
That source uses the sampled secret key and
honest witnesses; a reduction given only a DDH challenge cannot simply execute
it.
No honest-proof simulation has been substituted, so its statistical losses
have not disappeared into this bound.

\subsection{Historical component: Trustee simulation after reachable prior queries}\label{sec:trustee-simulation}
The key and equality-of-logs proof simulators take public statements only.
For base $B$ and public key $Y$, sample $c,z$ uniformly from $F$ and set
$T=zB-cY$.
Return proof $(T,z)$ and program the actual hash key to $c$ only
if it has no prior answer.
A hit preserves the prior cache and sets a collision
flag, even if its answer agrees.
For key proofs $B=g,Y=\mathsf{pk}$; for
partial decryption $B=(g,\mathsf{ct}_1)$ and
$Y=(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{share})$.
The tags retain the generator, public key,
ciphertext, share and relevant commitments.
\companchor{TrusteeOracleSimulation}{TrusteeOracleSimulation.sim_valid}
\companchor{TrusteeOracleSimulation}{TrusteeOracleSimulation.sim_cache_le}

\begin{theorem}[Reachable-cache trustee simulation, machine-checked]
Let $F$ be a finite field with its sampler, $G$ an additive $F$-module, both
with decidable equality. Suppose $r\mapsto rg$ is injective. Let $P$ be any
full election-oracle computation with structural bound $n$ on hash requests
across all three proof domains, and $(a,C)$ any supported result of
$\mathsf{run}(P,\varnothing)$. For every secret $s$ and ciphertext
$\mathsf{ct}$, each original trustee proof step $R$ and corresponding
public-input simulator $S$ satisfy
\[
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}\bigl(R(C),S(C)\bigr)
 \le (1+n)|F|^{-1}.
\]
For the key step, $\mathsf{pk}=sg$; for the decryption step also
$\mathsf{share}=s\mathsf{ct}_1$. The compared output contains the proof,
collision flag and complete final cache; the original step returns flag false.
Each original step draws its historical nonzero nonce at the point of use.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{TrusteeReachableSimulation}{TrusteeReachableSimulation.key_after_queries_le}
\companchor{TrusteeReachableSimulation}{TrusteeReachableSimulation.partial_after_queries_le}
\companchor{TrusteeOracleSimulation}{TrusteeOracleSimulation.key_real}
\companchor{TrusteeOracleSimulation}{TrusteeOracleSimulation.partial_real}

The proof derives a cache cover of size at most $n$ from the supported prior
execution.
Uniform coin queries do not consume that budget; repeated hash hits
do.
No cover, freshness or tag-injectivity premise remains in the displayed
result.
The local programming bound charges $n/|F|$ for hits and $1/|F|$ for
replacing the historical nonzero nonce by a full-field nonce.
\companchor{ElectionCacheBudget}{ElectionCacheBudget.run_covered}
\companchor{TrusteeOracleSimulation}{TrusteeOracleSimulation.collision_le}
\companchor{TrusteeOracleSimulation}{TrusteeOracleSimulation.distance_le}
\companchor{TrusteeSimulation}{TrusteeSimulation.nonzero_distance}

Pinned VCVio's Schnorr transcript theorem supplies full-field equality by
mapping scalar commitments into the actual module.
The point-probability
bound needs only injectivity, derived for the product from its first coordinate.
\companchor{TrusteeSimulation}{TrusteeSimulation.full_eq}
\companchor{TrusteeSimulation}{TrusteeSimulation.commitment_probability_le}
\companchor{TrusteeSimulation}{TrusteeSimulation.partial_injective}

\paragraph{Controls and composition boundary.}
Literal key/decryption transcripts validate; changing the challenge invalidates
the key proof.
Checked counterexamples refute exact historical-nonce simulation
and product-group surjectivity.
Fresh programming stores the challenge; every
hit preserves and flags the old answer.
A mixed query tree requires five hash
requests, refuting a bound of four.
Independent multiplicative fixtures over
$p=23,q=11$ enumerate 242 full transcripts and 242 cached cases, with exact
historical distance $1/11$, maximum cached distance $2/11$, zero failures and
zero discards.

The actual election samples private decryption nonces
before its honest ballots.
The following result justifies moving those draws
to their use points.

\subsection{Historical component: The original private nonce schedule}\label{sec:nonce-schedule}
The historical world draws the secret, key-proof nonce, both decryption nonces
and honest coin records before publishing its prefix.
The equivalent schedule
keeps the honest records private, draws the key-proof nonce immediately before
its key query, and draws each decryption nonce immediately before that proof's
query.
Each draw retains its original independent nonzero distribution.
Submission validation, rejection, retained board and bounded decoding are the
original algorithms.

\begin{theorem}[Original-election scheduling, machine-checked]
Let $F$ be a finite field with its sampler and $G$ an additive $F$-module, both
with decidable equality. Fix any generator $g$, fingerprint function, preparation
computation $P$, stateful oracle adversary $\mathcal A$ chosen from its result,
and initial full cache $C$. Then
\[
 \mathcal L\bigl(\mathsf{run}(\mathsf{preparedSource}(P,\mathcal A),C)\bigr)
 =\mathcal L\bigl(\mathsf{run}(\mathsf{scheduled}(P,\mathcal A),C)\bigr).
\]
Here $\mathsf{scheduled}$ is \code{ElectionNonceSchedule.prepared}, using the
nonce-at-use order above. Both outputs contain the actual public result, winning
bit and complete final cache. The equality requires neither generator injectivity
nor honest acceptance, freshness or a query/runtime bound.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionNonceSchedule}{ElectionNonceSchedule.prepared_eq}

The world theorem retains the public result and guess, for either challenge
bit and any initial cache.
The finishing theorem commutes the two independent
nonce draws past submission validation and separates them around the actual
first proof query.
It admits an arbitrary later full-oracle continuation.
Pinned VCVio's independent bind-swap theorem supplies the distributional step.
Projection then preserves the original random-oracle game's winning-bit law.
\companchor{ElectionNonceSchedule}{ElectionNonceSchedule.world_eq}
\companchor{ElectionNonceSchedule}{ElectionNonceSchedule.finish_eq}
\companchor{ElectionNonceSchedule}{ElectionNonceSchedule.winning_eq}

\paragraph{Controls and boundary.}
A checked supported nonce pair has distinct components.
Reusing a single nonce
changes the joint law even though its marginals agree.
Sampling independent
private coins preserves an earlier cached answer; resetting that answer changes
its probability from one to $1/11$.
Independent finite query/proof fixtures cover
34,992 assignments over two initial caches, retain accepting and rejecting cases,
and detect shared-nonce and cache-reset variants in each cache.
They have zero
failures or discards and are not a differential implementation of the full
historical protocol.
\companchor{ElectionNonceScheduleControls}{ElectionNonceScheduleControls.distinct_pair_supported}
\companchor{ElectionNonceScheduleControls}{ElectionNonceScheduleControls.shared_nonce_changes_law}
\companchor{ElectionNonceScheduleControls}{ElectionNonceScheduleControls.old_answer_preserved}
\companchor{ElectionNonceScheduleControls}{ElectionNonceScheduleControls.reset_answer_probability}
\companchor{ElectionNonceScheduleControls}{ElectionNonceScheduleControls.retained_answer_probability}

This discharges the scheduling premise for composing the local trustee bounds.
It does not replace any proof by a simulator.
Equality of output
laws does not identify the two source query trees or their extractor replay
paths; the extraction argument must separately establish the needed properties
of its chosen simulated source.

\subsection{Historical component: Composing the actual trustee finish}\label{sec:trustee-finish}
The finishing simulator runs the original submission verifier and component
weeding, then simulates the two equality-of-logs proofs in the same full cache.
It takes the prefix public key and a supplied public-share function.
It returns
the original public fields, including rejection, board, encrypted tally, shares,
proofs and bounded decoding, plus a private sticky collision flag.
Both flags
contribute by disjunction.
Later queries receive the public result; they do not
receive the flag.

\begin{theorem}[Original finishing-phase simulation, machine-checked]
Let $F$ be a finite field with its sampler and $G$ an additive $F$-module,
both with decidable equality. Fix secret $s$, prefix $V$, arbitrary submitted
ballot $b$, and an arbitrary later full-oracle continuation $K$. Write
$g=V.\mathsf{parameters.generator}$ and assume
\[
 V.\mathsf{parameters.publicKey}=sg,\qquad
 (r\mapsto rg)\text{ is injective}.
\]
Let $P$ make at most $n$ hash requests across all proof domains and let $(a,C)$
be a supported output of $\mathsf{run}(P,\varnothing)$. Let $R$ be the original
finish, drawing its two independent nonzero nonces before submission validation,
then running $K$; append flag false to its output. Let $S$ be
\code{finishSim}, supplied shares $\mathsf{shareFor}(ct)=s\,ct_1$, followed by
\code{observe K}. Both retain the public result, $K$'s return value, private flag
and complete final cache. Then
\[
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}(R(C),S(C))\le (2n+9)|F|^{-1}.
\]
No cache cover, freshness, acceptance or bound on $K$'s queries is assumed.
The query bound on $P$ is structural, not a runtime certificate.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionTrusteeSimulation}{ElectionTrusteeSimulation.finish_after_queries_le}

The pair bound from a cache covered by $k$ keys is $(2k+3)/|F|$.
The first
simulated proof inserts at most one key; the second proof's loss includes that
successor cache.
Actual submission validation contributes at most three queries,
using the existing verified submission bound, giving $(2k+9)/|F|$ for the finish.
The prior execution derives $k\le n$.
Data processing retains the bound through
all later queries.
The original schedule correspondence supplies the
pre-validation nonce order in the theorem.
\companchor{ElectionTrusteeSimulation}{ElectionTrusteeSimulation.pair_distance_le}
\companchor{ElectionTrusteeSimulation}{ElectionTrusteeSimulation.finish_distance_le}

Actual prefix execution derives its generator and trustee public key regardless
of acceptance or earlier cache contents.
This supplies the local theorem's key
relation when the prefix comes from that execution.
The already checked private
sampling/cache equation is now shared by scheduling and finish normalization.
\companchor{ElectionTrusteeSimulation}{ElectionTrusteeSimulation.prefix_parameters}
\companchor{ElectionCache}{ElectionCache.run_liftProb}

\paragraph{Controls and boundary.}
A checked pair can return two fresh proofs with independently specified
commitments.
Separate controls retain a first or second collision flag;
publication retains rejection and the submitted ballot.
The zero-prior-query
bound is strictly below one for $|F|=11$.
Independent multiplicative enumeration
covers six initial-cache/ciphertext cases, including repeated proof contexts and
a later adaptive query.
Maximum observed distance is $49/121$; all cases satisfy
the pair bound.
Sticky-flag reset and challenge-overwrite mutants are detected,
with zero failures or discards.
\companchor{ElectionTrusteeSimulationControls}{ElectionTrusteeSimulationControls.two_fresh_supported}
\companchor{ElectionTrusteeSimulationControls}{ElectionTrusteeSimulationControls.first_flag_sticky}
\companchor{ElectionTrusteeSimulationControls}{ElectionTrusteeSimulationControls.second_flag_detected}
\companchor{ElectionTrusteeSimulationControls}{ElectionTrusteeSimulationControls.rejected_publication}
\companchor{ElectionTrusteeSimulationControls}{ElectionTrusteeSimulationControls.finish_bound_nontrivial}

This component simulates trustee \emph{proofs given correct shares}.
It does
not construct those shares without the secret inside a DDH reduction.
The next
two subsections connect key and honest-ballot simulation to the complete source.
Section~\ref{sec:programmed-extraction} supplies extraction from the simulated
source.

\subsection{Historical component: Key and honest-ballot simulation in the actual prefix}\label{sec:prefix-simulation}
The prefix simulator uses the existing key-proof and sampled honest-pair
simulators.
It constructs the original public parameters from the returned key
proof, evaluates the supplied fingerprint function on those parameters, and
retains both honest decisions and the board.
The ballot phase runs in the ballot
projection of the full cache; reconstruction retains every key and decryption
answer.
Its private initial collision flag is the key simulator's flag.

\begin{theorem}[Actual prefix simulation, machine-checked]
Let $F$ be a finite field with its sampler and $G$ an additive $F$-module, both
with decidable equality. Suppose $r\mapsto rg$ is injective. Fix any secret $s$,
vote $v$, fingerprint function and prior full-oracle program $P$ making at most
$n$ hash requests across all proof domains. For any supported output $(a,C)$ of
$\mathsf{run}(P,\varnothing)$,
\[
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}\bigl(R(C),S(g,sg,v,C)\bigr)
 \le (7n+91)/|F|.
\]
$R$ runs the actual fixed-secret prefix with the historical honest coin records
and nonzero key nonce, returning its complete public prefix, flag false and
final full cache. $S$ is the prefix simulator \code{sim}, with no secret-key
argument. Its output has the same fields and a private sticky collision flag.
The cache cover, six proof requests and validation-query budgets are derived;
no freshness or acceptance premise is assumed.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionPrefixSimulation}{ElectionPrefixSimulation.prefix_after_queries_le}
\companchor{ElectionPrefixSimulation}{ElectionPrefixSimulation.prefix_distance_le}

The key step contributes $(n+1)/|F|$.
Programming a key-proof context leaves
the ballot projection unchanged.
A finite ballot cover of size at most $n$
is derived as the preimage of the full cover under the injective ballot tag.
The existing honest-pair theorem contributes $6(n+15)/|F|$, with six proof
requests and at most six verification hashes.
Mapping its result back into
the full cache preserves every other domain and the incoming key flag.
Thus
the established pair simulation is reused without changing its historical
sampling loss or rejection behavior.

The real composed runner has the actual sampled-prefix distribution, with
independent draws moved past the key proof by the pinned bind-swap theorem.
Exact source factoring identifies this prefix inside the original scheduled
world.
An arbitrary later full-oracle continuation receives the public prefix,
while the flag stays private.
Data processing preserves the displayed bound,
retaining both the prefix and continuation result plus the final cache.
\companchor{ElectionPrefixSimulation}{ElectionPrefixSimulation.real_eq}
\companchor{ElectionPrefixSimulation}{ElectionPrefixSimulation.world_source}
\companchor{ElectionPrefixSimulation}{ElectionPrefixSimulation.continuation_after_queries_le}

\paragraph{Controls and remaining integration.}
Checked controls retain both non-ballot proof domains and refute reconstruction
that erases them.
A fingerprint depending on the actual key proof changes from
12 to 14 for two literal proofs.
Original rejection decisions remain observable;
a later cached query preserves an earlier collision flag.
The numerical bound
$91/101<1$ is nontrivial but does not assert a zero-query full-election history.
Independent finite checks cover 363 reachable mixed-cache traces and 726 flag
cases, detecting lost domains, a lost key flag, reset ballot answers and constant
fingerprints, with zero failures or discards.
These are boundary controls, not
an exhaustive distribution test of the complete prefix.
\companchor{ElectionPrefixSimulationControls}{ElectionPrefixSimulationControls.restored_other_domains}
\companchor{ElectionPrefixSimulationControls}{ElectionPrefixSimulationControls.lost_domain_counterexample}

The next result composes the prefix and finish bounds.

\subsection{Historical component: Complete prepared-election proof simulation}\label{sec:full-simulation}
The composed hybrid executes the original preparation, samples the original fair
challenge bit and trustee secret, runs the prefix proof simulator, then executes
the original casting callback, submission validation, simulated trustee proofs
and original guessing callback.
It retains the complete public result, winning
bit, private sticky collision flag and final full cache.
The secret is still
used to compute correct shares; this hybrid is not a DDH reduction.

\begin{theorem}[Complete proof-simulation bound, machine-checked]
Let $F$ be a finite field with its sampler and $G$ an additive $F$-module, both
with decidable equality. Suppose $r\mapsto rg$ is injective. Fix a fingerprint
function, preparation program $P$ and a family of adversaries indexed by its
returned state. Suppose $P$ makes at most $p$ hash requests and every casting
callback makes at most $c$ hash requests, counting all three proof domains.
Let $R$ run the original \code{preparedSource} from the empty full cache and
attach flag false. Let $S$ be \code{preparedSim}, returning the fields above.
Then
\[
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}(R,S)\le (11p+2c+126)/|F|.
\]
The comparison imposes no acceptance, freshness or supplied cache-cover premise.
Guessing callbacks may make arbitrary later queries. The budgets are structural
query bounds, not runtime certificates.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionFullSimulation}{ElectionFullSimulation.prepared_distance_le}
\companchor{ElectionFullSimulation}{ElectionFullSimulation.world_distance_le}

The simulated prefix preserves its public generator/key relation on every
supported branch.
Its ballot cache grows by at most twelve requests.
Combining
that cover with the full cache after the key proof gives a conservative successor
cover of $2k+13$ from an initial $k$-entry cover.
The actual casting callback adds
at most $c$ entries; submission verification and trustee simulation then cost
$(4k+2c+35)/|F|$.
Adding prefix loss $(7k+91)/|F|$ gives the displayed bound.
Preparation derives $k\le p$; mapping guesses to the winning bit preserves it.
All other-domain entries and earlier private flags survive the composition.
\companchor{AdaptiveBallotSimulation}{runBallotProofSim_cache_bound}
\companchor{ElectionPrefixSimulation}{ElectionPrefixSimulation.sim_covered}
\companchor{ElectionPrefixSimulation}{ElectionPrefixSimulation.sim_parameters}

A checked instance over $\mathbb Z_{257}$ makes an actual preparation query and
uses casting/guessing callbacks that query the retained cache.
Its loss is at
most $139/257<1$.
The guessing callback distinguishes saved states, and another
control proves that an earlier prefix collision survives finishing and guessing.
Independent reachable-cache enumeration adds 363 growth cases and detects a
mutant that omits key-proof growth; the earlier transcript and rejection controls
remain.
These fixtures do not enumerate the full election distribution.

The hybrid has already interpreted its hash queries.
The next subsection gives

Section~\ref{sec:programmed-extraction} accounts for rejection and

\subsection{Historical component: Replayable full-source correspondence}\label{sec:programmed-source}
The source retains the original preparation and attacker callbacks.
Raw ballot
requests pass through the existing programmed-ballot handler, which keeps a
live oracle and a shadow cache.
Key/decryption contexts use the auxiliary full
cache.
Honest proof requests record their actual statements; raw callbacks
cannot add or erase that record.
Trustee simulation preserves the ballot
projection and carries earlier collision flags.

\begin{theorem}[Replayable complete source, machine-checked]
Let $F$ be a finite field with its sampler and $G$ an additive $F$-module, both
with decidable equality. Fix a fingerprint function, $g$, an arbitrary
preparation program $P$, and an adversary family $A$ indexed by its returned
state. Let $R$ evaluate \code{ElectionProgrammedSource.prepared} with empty
initial source state and empty live ballot cache. Its result is $((a,s),L)$,
where $a$ contains the complete public result and winning bit, and $s$ contains
the full shadow cache $C$, private flag $b$, and recorded statements $T$.
For $\pi((a,s),L)=((a,b),C)$, suppressing the fixed fingerprint and $g$ arguments,
\[
 \mathsf{map}(\pi,R)
 =\code{ElectionFullSimulation.preparedSim}(P,A).
\]
The equality is of probabilistic computations, not merely an equality of
winning probabilities. Every supported output additionally satisfies
\[
 L\le\mathsf{project}(C),\qquad
 \forall(\mathit{stmt},c),\quad \mathit{stmt}\notin T
 \Rightarrow C(\mathsf{ballot}(\mathit{stmt},c))=L(\mathit{stmt},c).
\]
No cache invariant, acceptance, freshness, query bound or target-exclusion
premise is supplied to this complete-source result.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionProgrammedSource}{ElectionProgrammedSource.prepared_runtime_eq}
\companchor{ElectionProgrammedSource}{ElectionProgrammedSource.prepared_inv}

The local raw and honest-pair phase correspondences reuse the previously
checked ballot runtime and auxiliary-domain lowering.
Their intermediate
invariants are derived by the enclosing preparation and prefix execution.
The existing invariant-preservation proof now applies at those intermediate
states; its empty-state corollary reuses it.
The complete source composes the
actual key proof, honest pair, casting, submission, trustee proofs and guessing.

A fresh raw ballot request remains exactly a live source query.
Over
$\mathbb Z_{11}$ it has the same returned-scalar law as ordinary uniform
sampling.
The latter has zero live hash requests; the former cannot satisfy a
zero-query bound.
Thus equality of output laws does not preserve query trees
or available replay locations.

Independent deterministic enumeration checks 5602 reachable mixed raw/programmed
cache histories of length at most four, with adaptive query choices and both
incoming flag values.
Four mutants erase auxiliary entries, reset the incoming
flag, hide raw requests or treat programmed shadow entries as live.
All are
detected, with zero failures or discards.
These are cache/history fixtures;
they do not enumerate complete election distributions or execute joint forks.

\paragraph{Accepted proofs in the complete live cache.}
The same source now connects actual weeding to replay verification.
\begin{theorem}[Complete-source accepted validity, machine-checked]\label{thm:prepared-live}
Use the finite field, module and sampler of the preceding theorem. Let
$(( (V,w),S),L)$ be a supported output of the prepared source from empty
initial state and live cache. Suppose both entries of
$V.\mathit{beforeTally}.\mathit{honestDecisions}$ and $V.\mathit{decision}$ are
accepted. Set $b=V.\mathit{submission}$ and take $pk$ from its public prefix.
For each component or aggregate index $i$, there exists $c$ such that
\[
 L\bigl(b.\mathit{coveredStatement}(g,pk,i),
        b.\mathit{coveredProof}(i).\mathit{commitment}\bigr)=\mathsf{some}(c)
\]
and the full covered proof is valid for that stored challenge $c$.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionProgrammedSource}{ElectionProgrammedSource.prepared_live}

Raw preparation preserves an initially empty programming record; trustee key
simulation also preserves it.
The honest-pair provenance theorem now permits
arbitrary prior caches and flags, with an empty incoming record.
When both
honest decisions accept, every recorded statement belongs to a retained ballot.
The actual malicious submission's expanded weeding therefore excludes each of
its three targets from that record.
Shadow validity follows from actual verifier
acceptance; the derived invariant transfers its stored challenges to the live
cache.
Subsequent trustee and guessing execution preserve those live answers.
No freshness, target-exclusion or cache-invariant premise is supplied to the
complete-source theorem.
\companchor{HonestPrefixOracle}{repairedCastHonestPairOracle_programmed_targets_from}

A checked specialization includes actual preparation queries in both key and
ballot domains, retaining their answers for the attacker.
A negative control
shows that raw execution preserves an incoming foreign record, prior key-cache
entry and collision flag: raw execution cannot clear an arbitrary record.
The earlier accepted-third-ballot and rejected-honest-target recurrence controls
remain; both honest acceptance decisions are necessary to this argument.

\subsection{Historical component: Joint extraction from complete programmed elections}\label{sec:programmed-extraction}
Let $\mathit{source}$ be the actual prepared source of the preceding subsection,
run from empty state.
It retains the original public result, winning bit and
private state.
The extractor samples one original path and makes three
conditional replay attempts on that path, for the two component proofs and
aggregate proof.
It returns that original output with the three witnesses.
No fresh first path is chosen for an individual proof.

\begin{theorem}[Complete programmed-source extraction, machine-checked]
Let $F$ be a finite field with its sampler, $q=|F|$, and $G$ an additive
$F$-module, both with decidable equality. Fix $g$ such that $r\mapsto r g$ is
injective, a fingerprint function, a preparation program and an attacker family
indexed by its saved preparation state. Suppose preparation makes at most $p$
hash requests, every casting callback at most $c$, and every guessing callback
at most $d$, counting all three proof domains. Put
\[
 N=p+c+d+15,\qquad
 R=\frac{9}{q-1}+\frac{11p+2c+126}{q}.
\]
The actual source has at most $N$ live ballot-hash queries. Its probability of
rejecting at least one honest ballot is at most $R$. Let $a$ be the probability
that the malicious submission is accepted in this same source. For every
threshold $\delta\in\mathbb R_{\geq0}^{\infty}$, the actual joint extractor
with parameter $N$ satisfies
\[
 \Pr[\mathit{joint}\text{ succeeds}]
 \ \geq\ \bigl(a-R-3(N+1)\delta\bigr)
                  \left(\delta-\frac1q\right)^3.
\]
Subtractions use truncated nonnegative arithmetic. On success all three
witnesses concern the same retained original submission and its actual public
key, and that original output has a supported complete-source execution.
Both honest decisions and the malicious decision are accepted on success.
If additionally $2\ne0$ in $F$, the extracted nonces and integer vote counts
sum consistently, and the malicious vote count is at most one.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionProgrammedExtraction}{ElectionProgrammedExtraction.source_bound}
\companchor{ElectionProgrammedExtraction}{ElectionProgrammedExtraction.prepared_rejection_le}
\companchor{ElectionProgrammedExtraction}{ElectionProgrammedExtraction.joint_le}
\companchor{ElectionProgrammedExtraction}{ElectionProgrammedExtraction.joint_valid}
\companchor{ElectionProgrammedExtraction}{ElectionProgrammedExtraction.joint_consistent}

\paragraph{Why the protocol premises now follow.}
The source bound is structural.
Auxiliary lowering preserves the ballot-query
budget from each actual intermediate cache.
Existing sampling, submission and
honest-program bounds compose through the source's original callbacks.
The
allowance of fifteen protocol queries is conservative; ordinary randomness and
auxiliary proof simulation do not issue live ballot hashes.
The theorem also
permits separate, tighter ballot-only callback bounds.
\companchor{ElectionQueryBound}{ElectionQueryBound.lower_bound}

In the original complete election, finishing and guessing retain the sampled
honest decisions.
Their rejection bound is therefore the historical
$9/(q-1)$ bound.
The checked full proof-simulation distance transfers this event
to the programmed source, accounting for the second term of $R$.
This transfer
does not assert that honest rejection has probability zero.

On paths where both honest ballots and the malicious submission are accepted,
Theorem~\ref{thm:prepared-live} supplies full verification in the live cache.
The derived query
budget supplies the actual replay locations.
The simultaneous selector event
is exactly this joint acceptance event on the original path.
Its probability
is at least $a-R$.
The existing replay theorem then charges
$3(N+1)\delta$ for low-success contexts on that common path and retains the
$1/q$ repeated-challenge loss in each of the three conditional attempts.
\companchor{ElectionProgrammedExtraction}{ElectionProgrammedExtraction.selection_probability}

\paragraph{Controls and remaining construction.}
A checked instance over $\mathbb Z_{257}$ makes preparation queries in both key
and ballot domains and gives both answers to an attacker with querying casting
and guessing callbacks.
Here $p=2$, $c=d=1$, $N=19$, and the rejection allowance
is $9/256+150/257$.
The joint context loss is $60\delta$.
The displayed lower
bound is positive at $a=1$ and $\delta=1/200$; this is arithmetic nonvacuity,
not a claim that actual acceptance mass is one.
A separate control returns
opposite guesses for saved values five and six under the same cached answer.
\companchor{ElectionProgrammedExtractionControls}{ElectionProgrammedExtractionControls.rejection_after_queries}

\companchor{ElectionProgrammedExtractionControls}{ElectionProgrammedExtractionControls.saved_answer_changes_guess}

Independent repair/provenance, mixed-cache and full/split replay fixtures remain,
as do the positive common-path replay and disjoint-marginal counterexample.
These fixtures do not establish a full-election acceptance mass or DDH hardness.
The complete source in this subsection samples the election secret.
The following
construction supplies the needed ciphertext input laws and share identity;
connecting its execution to that complete source is outside this component's guarantee.

\subsection{Historical component: DDH ciphertext pairing and the statement interface}\label{sec:ddh-pairing}
A challenge supplies group elements $A,T$ without their scalar witnesses.
The old honest-proof handler computes its statement from a witness, so a dummy
witness would program the wrong statement.
The exposed statement step uses the
same existing simulator.
The old handler factors through it exactly, including
its state.
The step leaves the live cache unchanged, records the supplied
statement, preserves the live/shadow invariant and makes no live hash query.
\companchor{BallotProgrammedOracle}{ballotProgrammedImpl_statement}
\companchor{BallotProgrammedOracle}{ballotProgrammedStatement_runtime}
\companchor{BallotProgrammedOracle}{ballotProgrammedStatement_inv}
\companchor{BallotProgrammedOracle}{ballotProgrammedStatement_query_bound}

Write $E(r,m)=(rg,mg+r\,pk)$ and $v\in\{0,1\}$ for the challenge vote.
The historical experiment swaps abstention and candidate zero.
Both honest
second components encrypt zero.
Given known scalars $t,a,b$, define
\[
 C=(A,vg+T),\quad
 L=(C,E(a,0)),\quad R=(E(t,1)-C,E(b,0)),\quad K=(t,a+b).
\]
The output retains both ciphertext vectors and $K$.
Here subtraction is
componentwise group subtraction.
No discrete logarithm of $A$ or $T$ is an
input to this construction.

\begin{theorem}[Challenge input laws, machine-checked]\label{thm:ddh-pairing}
Let $F$ be a field and $G$ an additive $F$-module. For every $g,pk$, vote $v$
and scalars $x,y,a,b$, setting $A=xg$, $T=x\,pk$, $t=x+y$ gives exactly
\[
 L=(E(x,v),E(a,0)),\qquad R=(E(y,1-v),E(b,0)),\qquad K=(x+y,a+b).
\]
These ciphertexts equal those in the existing historical honest-ballot
algorithm for arbitrary proof coins and hash. For finite $F$ with decidable
equality and its sampler, the
joint law obtained by independently sampling $x,a,t,b$ uniformly equals the
joint law above with independent uniform $x,a,y,b$. Replacing all four original
nonces by the historical nonzero sampler changes this law by total variation
at most $4/|F|$.

For fixed $g,pk,A,t,a,b$ and uniform $z\in F$, the output law with $T=zg$
is independent of $v$: the bijection $z\mapsto z-v$ absorbs its contribution.
For every challenge, including one not satisfying the DH relation,
\[
 L_i+R_i=E(K_i,m_i),\qquad (m_0,m_1)=(1,0).
\]
If $pk=sg$ and malicious ciphertexts $M_i$ have valid witnesses $(u_i,r_i)$,
meaning $M_i=E(r_i,u_i)$ with $u_i$ a bit, then
\[
 (K_i+r_i)\,pk=\pd_s(L_i+R_i+M_i).
\]
The left side computes the share from known sums and the malicious witness
nonce; the secret appears only in the correctness statement.
\end{theorem}

\companchor{ElectionDDHConstruction}{ElectionDDHConstruction.paired_real}
\companchor{ElectionDDHConstruction}{ElectionDDHConstruction.paired_ciphertexts_real_eq}
\companchor{ElectionDDHConstruction}{ElectionDDHConstruction.paired_ciphertexts_nonzero_distance_le}
\companchor{ElectionDDHConstruction}{ElectionDDHConstruction.paired_ciphertexts_random_eq}
\companchor{ElectionDDHConstruction}{ElectionDDHConstruction.paired_totals}
\companchor{ElectionDDHConstruction}{ElectionDDHConstruction.tally_shares_eq}

\paragraph{Controls and boundary.}
The independent multiplicative fixture uses the subgroup of order eleven
modulo 23, generator two and public key eight.
Exhaustive finite comparisons
cover 29,282 real-law cases, 2,662 arbitrary challenges and 121 random-mask
comparisons (the latter two with zero-component nonces two and five), with no
failures or discarded cases.
Six deliberate mutations
are detected.
Checked exponent-form controls retain a real instance,
arbitrary-challenge cancellation, a zero combined nonce from two nonzero
nonces, and failures from addition, omitting a malicious nonce, and using a
dummy statement.
For four nonzero draws the fixture's exact distance is
$4641/14641$, bounded by $4/11$; dropping that loss would be false.
\companchor{ElectionDDHConstructionControls}{ElectionDDHConstructionControls.real_fixture}

These input laws and the conditional share identity do not
establish complete-election secrecy.
The following subsection supplies actual
honest-pair correspondence.
Obtaining malicious witnesses before tally
publication, charging per-original-path extraction failure and the historical
key-sampling loss, and proving the full game reduction are outside this component's guarantee.

\subsection{Historical component: Actual honest submissions from challenge ciphertexts}\label{sec:ddh-honest-source}
The construction now programs a proof for each component and for their sum,
then calls the existing repaired submission algorithm.
It generates and submits
the second ballot using the board retained by the first submission.
Verification
still precedes expanded weeding; an invalid proof or reused ciphertext retains
the incoming board.
All six proof requests occur, even if a submission rejects.

Let $H_{r,s}$ be the existing programmed honest-pair source with nonce pairs
$r=(x,a)$ and $s=(y,b)$ fixed.
Let $D(A,T,t,a,b,v)$ be the new challenge source
using the pair of Section~\ref{sec:ddh-pairing}, retaining its known column sums.
A programming state contains the shadow cache, sticky collision flag and ordered
statement record; interpretation additionally retains the live cache.

\begin{theorem}[Honest-pair source correspondence, machine-checked]\label{thm:ddh-honest-source}
Let $F$ be a field with its sampler, $G$ an additive $F$-module, and both types
have decidable equality. For arbitrary $g,pk,v,x,a,y,b$, the new honest-pair
program with challenge $(xg,x\,pk)$ and $t=x+y$ equals $H_{r,s}$ as a source
computation, including all proof and raw verification requests and successor
states. Retaining the private sums additionally returns $(x+y,a+b)$.

For finite $F$, augment the original sampled source by returning those private
sums. Projecting them away gives exactly the unchanged sampled source.
For every initial programming state $S$ and live cache $L$,
\[
 \operatorname{TV}\!\left(
  \operatorname{Run}(H_{\ne0}^{+};S,L),\quad
  \begin{array}{l}
   x,a,t,b\gets U(F);\\[-2pt]
   \operatorname{Run}(D(xg,x\,pk,t,a,b,v);S,L)
  \end{array}\right)\leq\frac4{|F|}.
\]
Both distributions retain both decisions, the actual board, programming state,
live cache and private sums. No freshness, acceptance, empty-cache or generator-
injectivity premise is required for this comparison. Embedding into the
existing full election state preserves this bound against its actual honest
phase, projecting away only the newly retained private sums.

For arbitrary $A,T$, the challenge source preserves live/shadow consistency
whenever it holds initially, in both the ballot and full election state.
This includes either rejection branch.
\end{theorem}

\companchor{ElectionDDHSource}{ElectionDDHSource.honest_pair_real}
\companchor{ElectionDDHSource}{ElectionDDHSource.challenge_pair_real}
\companchor{ElectionDDHSource}{ElectionDDHSource.sampledWithSums_fst}

\paragraph{Why the two equalities differ.}
At fixed nonces the ciphertext identity rewrites every requested component and
aggregate statement, so the source computations agree.
To compare the sampled
sources, the independent second nonce pair is moved before the first ballot's
runtime.
This step uses commutation only after oracle interpretation.
It
preserves the joint output law, including caches, but does not identify the
source query trees or replay locations of the two schedules.
The next prefix
extractor must derive those facts for its actual challenge source.
\companchor{ElectionDDHSource}{ElectionDDHSource.sampledWithSums_schedule}

\paragraph{Controls and component scope.}
Six checked controls cover source equality after prior state, exact sampled-tree
projection, the full-cache bound after a key query, a supported conflicting
statement, actual invalid-first-proof rejection retaining any board, and
nonempty incoming cache/flag/record.
The independent multiplicative harness uses
fixed coin tapes with cache misses, agreeing hits and conflicting hits, and
both incoming flags.
Seeds 17, 41 and 103 give 1,800 cases: their 3,600 decisions
include 2,688 acceptances, 600 invalid-proof rejections and 312 reuse rejections.
Seven deliberate defects are detected, with no failures or discarded cases.
The harness checks fixed-tape behavior, not a full-election probability law.

\companchor{ElectionDDHSourceControls}{ElectionDDHSourceControls.projection_preserves_sampled_source}
\companchor{ElectionDDHSourceControls}{ElectionDDHSourceControls.full_loss_after_key_query}
\companchor{ElectionDDHSourceControls}{ElectionDDHSourceControls.conflicting_statement_reachable}

The following result supplies extraction from the actual
prepared submission prefix.

\subsection{Historical component: Extraction before tally publication}\label{sec:ddh-prefix}
The reduction receives public elements $g,pk,A,T$.
Its prefix runs the actual
querying preparation, samples the vote and known nonce sums, simulates the key
and honest-ballot proofs, and executes the casting callback and repaired
submission.
It stops before publishing tally shares or running the guessing
callback.
The retained output $O$ contains the public prefix, malicious ballot,
submission decision and board, preparation result, casting state, vote, known
sums and full source state.
Retaining preparation separately is necessary:
$(\mathit{adversary}\ \mathit{initial}).\mathit{guessVote}$ may depend on it even
when casting returns identical saved states.

\begin{theorem}[Prepared-prefix joint extraction, machine-checked]\label{thm:ddh-prefix}
Let $F$ be a finite field with its sampler and decidable equality, and let $G$
be an additive $F$-module with decidable equality. Suppose $g\ne0$. Let $p,c$
bound live ballot-hash requests in preparation and every casting callback,
respectively. Define $N=p+c+9$, and let $a$ be the probability that both honest
submissions and the malicious submission accept in the actual prefix, starting
with empty caches. For every $\delta\in\mathbb R_{\ge0}\cup\{\infty\}$,
\[
 (a-3(N+1)\delta)(\delta-|F|^{-1})^3
 \ \leq\ \Pr[\mathit{prefixJoint}_N\text{ returns witnesses}].
\]
Subtractions are truncated at zero. The extractor uses one original prefix
execution and three residual replay attempts. Every supported successful return
$(O,w)$ retains a supported original runtime output, with the supplied public
parameters, all three acceptance decisions, and witnesses for all three covered
statements of that original malicious ballot. If scalar multiplication by $g$
is injective and $2\ne0$ in $F$, those witnesses have consistent component and
aggregate nonces and bits, and their component bit sum is at most one.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefix}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_joint_le}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefix}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_joint_valid}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefix}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_joint_consistent}

\paragraph{Derived premises.}
The actual source has at most six live ballot hashes for honest submissions and
three for the malicious submission.
Key and ballot proof programming makes no
live ballot-hash requests.
Preparation and key setup preserve the empty record
of programmed ballot statements.
Both honest acceptances therefore place every
programmed target on the retained board.
Accepted malicious submission excludes
those targets and yields validity in its final live cache.
Source consistency,
public parameters and the joint-selector acceptance event are derived for this
source, including prior queries; none is an additional caller premise of the
extraction bound.
The challenge need not be a real DDH tuple.
\companchor{ElectionDDHSource}{ElectionDDHSource.ballot_targets}
\companchor{ElectionDDHSource}{ElectionDDHSource.honest_pair_targets}
\companchor{ElectionDDHSource}{ElectionDDHSource.ballots_targets}
\companchor{ElectionDDHSource}{ElectionDDHSource.ballots_bound}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefix}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_inv}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefix}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_parameters}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefix}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_live}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefix}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_bound}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefix}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_selection_probability}
\companchor{BallotJointReplayBound}{ballotJointReplay_selection_probability}

\paragraph{Controls and boundary.}
The checked controls instantiate querying preparation and casting over
$\mathbb Z_{257}$ at $(g,pk,A,T)=(1,3,2,7)$, where $T\ne2pk$.
Their budget is ten and their context allowance is $33\delta$.
Changing the guessing callback leaves the entire prefix source identical;
a separate control checks that the callbacks differ.
Another shows why
preparation state cannot be recovered from casting state.
At $a=1$ and
$\delta=1/200$ the displayed lower bound is positive; this arithmetic check
does not assert actual acceptance mass one.
The existing independent
provenance, fixed-tape honest-source and replay-history fixtures pass again.

\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefixControls}{ElectionDDHPrefixControls.joint_after_queries}

A positive extraction probability does not bound the distance from the original
election game.
The following result supplies repeated-attempt failure accounting
while preserving the original execution.

\subsection{Historical component: Repeated extraction retaining the original execution}\label{sec:repeated-prefix}
Let $P$ be one sampled original prefix path.
A residual joint attempt executes
three replay completions selected from $P$.
The finite loop $R_k(P)$ executes at
most $k$ such joint attempts, returning the first witness triple or failure.
It never samples another original path.
The complete extractor always returns
$((O,L),w)$, where $O$ is the original prefix output, $L$ is its actual live cache,
and $w$ is an optional witness triple.
It projects $L$ from the original raw
path output; residual caches never replace it.

\begin{theorem}[Original-preserving extraction failure, machine-checked]\label{thm:prefix-failure}
Use the finite-field, module, sampler and decidable-equality hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:ddh-prefix}, with $g\ne0$ and the same preparation/casting
live-hash bounds $p,c$. Put $N=p+c+9$. For every finite repetition count $k$ and
$\delta\in\mathbb R_{\ge0}\cup\{\infty\}$,
\[
 \Pr[\mathsf{Accepted}(O)\land w=\mathsf{none}]
 \ \leq\ 3(N+1)\delta+
       \bigl(1-(\delta-|F|^{-1})^3\bigr)^k.
\]
Here $\mathsf{Accepted}$ requires both honest decisions and the malicious
submission to accept; subtraction is truncated. The marginal distribution of
$(O,L)$ equals the original prefix runtime distribution exactly, including all
rejections and extraction failures. There is no conditioning on success.

Every supported successful return contains witnesses for the original
submission and a live cache that belongs to that same supported original
runtime. Private/live consistency is derived. Scalar injectivity and $2\ne0$
add the component/aggregate nonce and vote-count consistency conclusions of
Theorem~\ref{thm:ddh-prefix}.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefixRepeat}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_failure_le}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefixRepeat}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_repeated_original}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefixRepeat}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_repeated_valid}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefixRepeat}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_repeated_consistent}

\paragraph{Probability and interaction accounting.}
For a fixed $P$, let $s(P)$ be the actual joint-attempt success probability.
The loop has failure probability $(1-s(P))^k$ and no missing probability mass.
Outside the existing low-context event, enabled selectors give
$s(P)\ge(\delta-|F|^{-1})^3$.
Averaging over the original $P$ charges the
low-context event once by $3(N+1)\delta$.
The prefix's pointwise selector
condition follows from its actual trace, budget and live validity.
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeat}{replayRepeat_failure}
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeat}{ballotJointReplayRepeatAtPath_lossless}
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeat}{ballotJointReplayRepeatAtPath_failure_le}
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeat}{ballotJointReplayRetained_failure_le}
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeat}{ballotJointReplayRetained_original}
\companchor{BallotJointReplayBound}{ballotJointReplay_selector_eq}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefix}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_trace_verified}

If the complete original source has at most $m$ total oracle interactions on
every branch, the repeated extractor has at most $(1+3k)m$.
This includes
uniform draws.
The premise is a total-source bound, not the $N$ live ballot-hash
bound.
This theorem assumes the total-source bound; it does not derive it from primitive sampler and callback costs or account for pure operations and standard-PPT execution.
Choosing
negligible $\delta$ does not automatically allow polynomially many repetitions;
the eventual reduction must choose $\delta,k$ with explicit advantage and cost
bounds.
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeat}{replayRepeat_bound}
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeat}{ballotJointReplayRetained_total_query_bound}
\companchor{ElectionDDHPrefixRepeat}{ElectionDDHSource.prefix_repeated_query_bound}

\paragraph{Checked counterexamples and controls.}
Two independent fair residual attempts fail with probability $1/4$; reusing one
random choice fails with probability $1/2$.
Zero attempts fail even when a
single attempt would surely succeed.
An unextractable original tag retains its
original mass $1/2$.
Resampling the original instead increases the successful
tag's mass to $3/4$.
These exact controls exclude replacing the original
execution during extraction.
Two further controls instantiate the actual
non-DH querying prefix's failure bound and full original-cache distribution.
An independent rational-tape enumeration checks 1,800 cases over seeds
17, 41 and 103, with zero failures/discards and four detected defects.
This
finite experiment validates the probability decomposition, not cryptographic
replay semantics or polynomial runtime.
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeatControls}{BallotReplayRepeatControls.two_attempts}
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeatControls}{BallotReplayRepeatControls.reused_randomness_fails}
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeatControls}{BallotReplayRepeatControls.zero_attempts}
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeatControls}{BallotReplayRepeatControls.original_tag_preserved}
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeatControls}{BallotReplayRepeatControls.resampling_bias}
\companchor{BallotReplayRepeatControls}{BallotReplayRepeatControls.prefix_failure_after_queries}

The following comparison composes the complete real-challenge source with the
historical election.
The implementable DDH finisher in the later component replaces secret-based shares by extracted witnesses and known column nonces.
Source-cost derivation, the full advantage inequality and standard-PPT adequacy
are outside this component's guarantee.

\subsection{Historical component: Complete real-challenge comparison}\label{sec:complete-real}
The comparison experiment samples $sk,x\gets U(F)$ and executes the actual
public-input prefix with
\[
 (g,pk,A,T)=(g,sk\,g,xg,x\,pk).
\]
Its finishing continuation publishes trustee proofs for the correct shares
computed using $sk$, then runs the original guessing callback with both its
preparation and casting state.
This is a mathematical comparison hybrid. The implementable DDH finisher uses extracted witnesses and known nonces instead of $sk$.

\begin{theorem}[Complete real-challenge comparison, machine-checked]\label{thm:complete-real}
Let $F$ be a finite field with its sampler and decidable equality, and $G$ an
additive $F$-module with decidable equality. Write $q=|F|$. Replacing the historical
nonzero key and four honest nonces by the real-challenge construction changes
the complete programmed-source runtime distribution by at most $5/q$. This
comparison retains the final public result, winning bit, complete source state
and actual live cache. It has no acceptance or empty-intermediate-cache premise.

If scalar multiplication by $g$ is injective, and $p,c$ bound all proof-hash
requests during preparation and every casting callback respectively, then
\[
 \operatorname{TV}\bigl(\Gamma_{\mathrm{historical}},
              \Gamma_{\mathrm{real\ comparison}}\bigr)
 \ \leq\ \frac{11p+2c+131}{q}.
\]
Both distributions retain every public field, winning bit, private collision
flag and final full cache; the original game's added flag is false. Rejection
branches and the original guessing callback remain, with no bound on its later
queries. The comparison world still uses the sampled secret for its shares.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ElectionDDHRealSource}{ElectionDDHSource.source_real_distance_le}
\companchor{ElectionDDHRealSource}{ElectionDDHSource.prepared_real_distance_le}

\paragraph{Composition and source boundary.}
The honest-pair $4/q$ comparison survives the actual casting, repaired submission,
trustee simulation, publication and guessing continuation.
Replacing the key
adds $1/q$.
Composing these with the checked full proof-simulation allowance
$(11p+2c+126)/q$ gives the constant 131.
Independent key, exponent and honest-
nonce draws commute only after oracle interpretation.
The actual prefix's
query positions and extraction properties continue to use their own checked
source, rather than an inferred replay-tree equality.
\companchor{ElectionDDHRealSource}{ElectionDDHSource.after_honest_real_distance_le}
\companchor{ElectionFullSimulation}{ElectionFullSimulation.prepared_distance_le}

The exact random-draw runtime equation preserves the complete prior source state
and live cache.
The actual finishing continuation preserves the supplied prefix,
submission, decision, retained board, encrypted tally and correct secret-based
shares in every supported final output, including after guessing queries.
Neither result filters to accepted ballots.
\companchor{ElectionDDHSource}{ElectionDDHSource.run_programmed_prob}
\companchor{ElectionDDHRealSource}{ElectionDDHSource.raw_prob}
\companchor{ElectionDDHRealSource}{ElectionDDHSource.finishAfterCast_publication}

\paragraph{Controls and composition.}
At $q=257$, querying preparation and saved-state-dependent casting give
$p=2,c=1$ and allowance $155/257<1$.
Uniform key sampling returns zero with mass
$1/257$, while the historical sampler never does.
Additional controls preserve
a nonempty cache, sticky flag and recorded target across random draws, detect
cache reset, and preserve invalid-proof rejection and its retained board through
actual finishing and guessing.
The independent five-coordinate sampling fixture
enumerates 181,258 tuples for $q=2,3,5,7,11$, with zero failures/discards.
Its
exact input variation is $1-((q-1)/q)^5\le5/q$; at $q=11$ it exceeds $4/q$,
excluding omission of the key replacement from that input comparison.
The
fixture tests sampling laws; continuation correspondence is proved in Lean.

\companchor{ElectionDDHRealSourceControls}{ElectionDDHRealSourceControls.random_draw_preserves_state}
