\subsection{Historical component: Native oracle reference and private frames}\label{sec:native-oracle}
The native reference machine has three ordinary tapes over
$\{\bot,0,1\}$: work $W$, query $Q$ and answer $A$, with independent heads.
Fixed code receives only a finite label and the three current cells.
A local
command selects at most one move/write on each tape, or leaves it alone.
Native commands issue the existing raw hash/coin events and return to a finite
label.
Both the transition-table input and command domains are finite.
Returned configurations are internal states, not additional public election
observations.
\companchor{NativeOracleTape}{NativeOracleTape.heads_finite}
\companchor{NativeOracleTape}{NativeOracleTape.commands_finite}

\begin{theorem}[Native event and successor agreement; machine-checked]
Let a live configuration $c$ select a hash command with return label $r$.
Writing $\mathsf{word}(Q)$ for the contiguous query word from its current head,
the reference transition is exactly
\[
 \mathsf{step}(P,c)=
   \mathsf{hash}(\mathsf{word}(Q))\mathbin{\gg=}
   (a\mapsto\mathsf{pure}(r,W,Q,Q(a))).
\]
Here $Q(a)$ denotes the canonical answer tape containing $a$ at its origin.
The coin equation instead queries the actual coin and writes $Q([b])$.
Every supported native successor has the selected label, complete unchanged
work/query tapes and a canonical replacement answer tape. No canonical-query
or caller-supplied frame premise is required.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{NativeOracleTape}{NativeOracleTape.hash_step}
\companchor{NativeOracleTape}{NativeOracleTape.coin_step}
\companchor{NativeOracleTape}{NativeOracleTape.oracle_successor}

These are the existing dispatcher events.
Its work component is unused, so a
semantic adapter retains the reference work tape directly while reusing its
query/answer operation.
This introduces no runtime work conversion.
Complete
bounded runs compose, and halted states retain all tapes without issuing calls.
\companchor{NativeOracleTape}{NativeOracleTape.run_add}
\companchor{NativeOracleTape}{NativeOracleTape.run_halted}

Six controls cover embedded query blanks and stale cells, moved query heads,
private work, replacement of a displaced answer, both coin-driven branches and
halt.
A checked counterexample excludes aliasing work and answer: their visible
word prefixes can agree while their private cells to the left differ.
This is
an insufficient-representation counterexample, not a cryptographic attack.
\companchor{NativeOracleTapeControls}{NativeOracleTapeControls.work_answer_alias_fails}
\companchor{NativeOracleTapeControls}{NativeOracleTapeControls.moved_query}

Independent fixtures pass 3,000 native events (seeds 7/19/41, sparse cells at
$-4..4$, heads $-3..3$, hash reply lengths 0--12), detecting work-alias and
query-reset mutants; failures and gaveUp are zero.
They do not test a source
compiler.
These component fixtures do not justify normalization of conventional PPT algorithms to the native reference machine.


\subsection{Historical component: Conditional asymptotic completion prototype}\label{sec:secrecy-prototype}
This historical prototype checks the asymptotic assembly at the level of oracle programs.
It uses the original prepared election and both exact DDH tests from
the finite reduction.
Its DDH-security premises are explicit. This prototype does not establish reduction efficiency; the current theorem carries the named external efficiency assumptions described in the formal reference.
No protocol observation, rejection branch or attacker callback is changed.

\paragraph{Family and accuracy.}
At parameter $n$, let $F_n$ be the existing finite scalar field, $G_n$ its
module, with the same sampling and decidable-equality instances.
A
\code{PreparedFamily} supplies the generator, fingerprint, preparation,
saved-state types and attacker callbacks.
It includes generator/scalar
injectivity and hash-query bounds $p(n),c(n)$ on preparation and every casting
callback.
It contains no local-runtime or attacker-coverage certificate.
Write $q(n)=|F_n|$ and let $b(n)$ be the absolute winning bias of this original
prepared shared-oracle game.
For each fixed $k\in\mathbb N$, set
\[
 e_k(n)=(n+1)^{k+1},\qquad N(n)=p(n)+c(n)+9.
\]
Let $M_k(n)$ be the actual main DDH advantage at $(N(n),e_k(n))$, and $J(n)$
the actual honest-rejection DDH advantage.
These names abbreviate the two
algorithms in the finite theorem; neither is an independently supplied bound.

\begin{samepage}
\begin{theorem}[Conditional prepared-family secrecy; machine-checked]
In the family above, assume a polynomial $P\in\mathbb N[X]$ bounds
$p(n)+c(n)$ for every $n$. Assume $(q(n)-1)^{-1}$ is negligible and
\[
  (\forall k\in\mathbb N,\ M_k\text{ is negligible})
  \quad\land\quad J\text{ is negligible}.
\]
Then $b$ is negligible. Negligibility uses pinned VCVio's definition, with
real nonnegative advantages embedded by $\operatorname{ofReal}$.
\end{theorem}
\end{samepage}
\companchor{ElectionSecrecyPrototype}{ElectionSecrecyPrototype.prepared_negligible_of_ddh}

The proof derives the finite theorem's applicability rather than assuming it:
for each fixed $k$, eventually
\[
 12\bigl(p(n)+c(n)+10\bigr)e_k(n)\le q(n).
\]
The actual replay count $3456(N(n)+1)^3e_k(n)^4$ is bounded by a polynomial
which may depend on $P$ and $k$.
This bounds the number of trials and does not bound their local runtime.
\companchor{ElectionSecrecyPrototype}{ElectionSecrecyPrototype.accuracy_admissible}
\companchor{ElectionSecrecyPrototype}{ElectionSecrecyPrototype.replay_bounded}

To expose the quantifier order, fix $k$.
After multiplying the finite advantage
bound by $n^k$, its extraction allowance satisfies
\[
 n^k\,\frac{2}{e_k(n)}\le\frac{2}{n+1}\longrightarrow0.
\]
The scaled algebraic and DDH errors also tend to zero under the stated
premises.
Repeating this argument for every fixed $k$ establishes negligible
bias.
It does not choose one exponentially accurate reduction.
\companchor{ElectionSecrecyPrototype}{ElectionSecrecyPrototype.negligible_of_polynomial_accuracy}

\paragraph{Controls and scope.}
Four controls check an exact replay count, scaled rational slack, failure of
using the same linear accuracy for every degree, and the stronger fact that
$2/(n+1)$ is not negligible.
Existing finite queried-election and repair
controls are retained.
Independent arithmetic fixtures pass 3,000 cases
(seeds 7/19/41; $n=0..128$, $k=0..8$, budget $0..64$), detecting fixed-accuracy
and linear-replay mutants; failures and gaveUp are zero.
This tests arithmetic,
not cryptographic hardness.
\companchor{ElectionSecrecyPrototypeControls}{ElectionSecrecyPrototypeControls.polynomial_accuracy_fixture}
\companchor{ElectionSecrecyPrototypeControls}{ElectionSecrecyPrototypeControls.fixed_accuracy_not_negligible}

The next section proves probability-law sampler transport in both DDH worlds. The formal reference states the current attacker coverage theorem, protocol scope and external assumptions connecting the reduction to a machine-level DDH hypothesis.


\subsection{Historical component: Fair-bit transport of the exact DDH reductions}\label{sec:ddh-fair-bits}
The completion prototype's sampling obligation can be discharged at the
probabilistic-program level.
For an actual DDH reduction $A$, define
$A^{\mathrm{bits}}_s$ by replacing its private uniform-range queries with the
existing fair-bit modulo sampler at slack $s$.
The pinned
\code{uniformSampleImpl} expresses this coin-only computation in the
\code{ProbComp} DDH interface with exactly the same output law.
The real and
random DDH challenger distributions are unchanged.
\companchor{ElectionDDHFairBits}{ElectionDDHFairBits.bits_law}

Suppose every execution of $A$ on a fixed public generator and arbitrary
challenge coordinates has uniform-index query bound $B$.
The complete output
law in each DDH world changes by at most $B2^{-s}$.
Consequently the actual
two-game advantages satisfy
\[
 \bigl|\operatorname{Adv}_{\mathrm{DDH}}(A^{\mathrm{bits}}_s)
       -\operatorname{Adv}_{\mathrm{DDH}}(A)\bigr|\le 2B2^{-s}.
\]
Both world errors are retained.
Setting $s=n$ gives negligible error for any
polynomial bound on $B$, using Mathlib's polynomial-over-exponential limit and
VCVio's negligible-loss closure.
\companchor{ElectionDDHFairBits}{ElectionDDHFairBits.real_distance}
\companchor{ElectionDDHFairBits}{ElectionDDHFairBits.random_distance}
\companchor{ElectionDDHFairBits}{ElectionDDHFairBits.advantage_distance}
\companchor{ElectionDDHFairBits}{ElectionDDHFairBits.negligible_of_bits}

\begin{samepage}
\begin{theorem}[Prepared-family secrecy from fair-bit DDH tests; machine-checked]
Let $D$ be the original prepared-election family over prime scalar fields
$\mathbb Z_{q(n)}$, with its generator injectivity and pointwise preparation/
casting hash bounds. Assume $p+c$ has a natural polynomial bound and
$(q(n)-1)^{-1}$ is negligible. Suppose complete preparation, casting and
guessing query bounds $P(n),C(n),J(n)$ hold for every callback state, with
$P+C+J$ bounded by a natural polynomial. Assume the exact fair-bit main DDH
reduction is secure for every fixed accuracy degree $k$, and the exact
fair-bit rejection DDH reduction is secure; both use sampling slack $n$.
Then the original prepared-election winning bias is negligible.
\end{theorem}
\end{samepage}
\companchor{ElectionSecrecyFairBits}{ElectionSecrecyFairBits.prepared_negligible_of_fair_bits}

The full main query count is derived from the existing bound
$(1+3t)(4P+4C+78)+(4+4J)$, where $t$ is the actual replay count.
The rejection
test uses $4P+4C+78$.
Polynomial domination of both counts and the sampling
losses are proved, then composed with the preceding prototype.
No separate
reduction-budget certificate is assumed.

Five controls check adaptive tests in both DDH worlds, their combined advantage
allowance, retained modulo bias, dependence on the given public input, and a
counterexample to dropping one world's loss.
Independent enumeration passes
750 cases (seeds 7/19/41; fields 3, 5, 7; slack 0--6; two adaptive private
queries), detecting erased-bias, changed-challenger and one-world-loss mutants;
failures and gaveUp are zero.
The toy fields carry no hardness assertion.
\companchor{ElectionDDHFairBitsControls}{ElectionDDHFairBitsControls.actual_modulo_bias}
\companchor{ElectionDDHFairBitsControls}{ElectionDDHFairBitsControls.one_world_allowance_fails}

This transport theorem concerns the exact fair-bit reductions. It does not establish efficient modulo, field/group or callback execution, operand-width bounds or attacker coverage.
The
probability-law transport does not implement those operations or complete the
physical scalar sampler.

\subsection{Historical component: Complete loaded-word binary remainder}\label{sec:binary-modulo}
Its finite control uses the existing subtraction stacks plus raw and pending
input stacks.
It reverses the input by stack moves, consumes one most-significant
bit at a time, and calls the checked update through the existing stack-frame
and return-link libraries.
Two transfer passes move each result back into the
remainder stack without reversing its little-endian order.
The program's
instructions do not call host modulo, list reversal or conversion to numbers.
\companchor{BinaryModuloMachine}{BinaryModuloMachine.supports}

\begin{samepage}
\begin{theorem}[Complete loaded-word division; machine-checked]
Let $w$ be any little-endian Boolean word, including high zero padding, and
let $q>0$. Initially load $w$ on the raw input stack and canonical $q$ digits
on the modulus stack; the remainder, pending input and four subtraction
scratch/output stacks are empty. Then there exists an execution length
\[
 u\le |w|\bigl(8\,\operatorname{size}(q)+13\bigr)+2
\]
of the actual TM2 program which halts with canonical digits of
$\operatorname{bitsValue}(w)\bmod q$ on the remainder stack, the exact original
modulus retained, all input/scratch stacks empty and local memory reset.
The result has at most $\operatorname{size}(q)$ bits.
\end{theorem}
\end{samepage}
\companchor{BinaryModuloMachine}{BinaryModuloMachine.run}
\companchor{BinaryModuloMachine}{BinaryModuloMachine.result_width}

The loop invariant is $r<q$.
Each incoming bit $b$ forms $2r+b<2q$, so the
existing single guarded subtraction suffices.
Its derived bound is
$6\operatorname{size}(q)+9$.
Reading the bit and both return-transfer passes
raise this to at most $8\operatorname{size}(q)+12$ per bit.
Initial reversal
and final halt give the displayed total.
This counts TM2 ticks, with fixed
finite instruction bodies; it does not yet claim the full physical sampler's
runtime.
List reversal and modulo in this explanation interpret the executed
stack operations rather than serving as uncharged instructions.

Seven controls retain complete state: nonpalindromic input/output, padded
input, modulus one, empty input, a live startup prefix, and the general
modulus-three instance.
In particular, $6\bmod5=1$ detects reversed input,
and $2\bmod3=2$ detects reversing the returned digits into one.
\companchor{BinaryModuloMachineControls}{BinaryModuloMachineControls.input_order}
\companchor{BinaryModuloMachineControls}{BinaryModuloMachineControls.return_order}
\companchor{BinaryModuloMachineControls}{BinaryModuloMachineControls.live_prefix}

Independent fixtures pass 16,352 exhaustive cases (raw width 0--8, modulus
1--32) and 1,500 seeded cases (7/19/41; width 0--128, modulus 1--65,536).
They compose the previously validated subtraction interpreter with explicit
driver transfers and compare with integer modulo and binary strings.
Input-
reversal and return-transfer mutants are detected; failures and gaveUp are
zero.
Connecting the coin
loader below to division, modulus loading and scalar digit handoff remain,
together with physical execution/efficiency and standard-PPT coverage.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Executed chronological coin loading}\label{sec:coin-word-loader}
The fixed program \texttt{CoinWordLoader.code} has five Boolean stacks
(width, response, accumulator, output and retained modulus), four labels and
three local memory values.
It pops one width token, queries a coin, moves the
response into its accumulator and repeats.
It then reverses the accumulator
by stack moves.
This returns the first queried bit first, as required by the
original sampler's little-endian encoding.

\begin{theorem}[Loaded-width coin execution; machine-checked]
Let $W,Q$ be arbitrary Boolean words and $w=|W|$. Start at the counting label
with zero local memory, width stack $W$, modulus stack $Q$, and empty response,
accumulator and output stacks. The actual program's run for $4w+2$ source
transitions equals the following oracle computation: query $w$ coins in order,
let $b$ be their chronological response word, and return the halted configuration
with zero local memory, output $b$, retained modulus $Q$, every other stack empty,
and exact source charge $15w+9$. This is equality of oracle query trees,
before selecting a handler.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CoinWordLoader}{CoinWordLoader.run_source}

Consequently, interpreting the output word by \texttt{bitsValue} agrees with
\texttt{sampleFairBitIndex} after mapping its coin events to the raw coin port.
For every positive $q$, observing the value modulo $q$ agrees with the original
\texttt{sampleFairBitModulo} value under the same mapping.
These equalities retain
the query tree, including effects of any common handler.
Numeric interpretation
in the statements is an observation; it adds no free conversion or modulo
instruction to the program.
\companchor{CoinWordLoader}{CoinWordLoader.word_index}
\companchor{CoinWordLoader}{CoinWordLoader.run_modulo}

The initially proposed charge $15w+8$ is refuted by the zero-width case, whose
charge is nine.
The source cost model charges the maximum branch cost, including
five for the final empty accumulator branch.
Five controls pin full-state bit
order, arbitrary width payload and padded modulus retention, zero-width
no-query behavior, the failed charge claim, and a live response prefix.
\companchor{CoinWordLoaderControls}{CoinWordLoaderControls.charge_eight_fails}
\companchor{CoinWordLoaderControls}{CoinWordLoaderControls.live_response}
An independent finite interpreter passes 1,023 exhaustive words of width 0--9
and 1,500 seeded cases (7/19/41; width 0--256, modulus width 0--128).
It detects omitted reversal, an extra zero-width query and stale response
storage; failures and gaveUp are zero.

Width and modulus are loaded inputs here.
The following result connects these actual output stacks to binary division.
Operand loading and delivering scalar digits to the handler/writer are outside this component's guarantee;
the complete physical sampler and standard-PPT efficiency are separate obligations.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Executed coin-to-remainder sampler}\label{sec:coin-modulo}
One fixed oracle program now relocates the existing coin-loader instructions
and enters the existing division algorithm on the same storage.
An executable
finite-coordinate translation changes division's enumerated stacks, labels and
local memory into \texttt{Fin} indices.
It preserves every intermediate
configuration and transition, with local source charge at most 32.
\companchor{BinaryModuloCode}{BinaryModuloCode.tick}
\companchor{BinaryModuloCode}{BinaryModuloCode.run}
\companchor{BinaryModuloCode}{BinaryModuloCode.local_cost}

\begin{theorem}[Combined coin sampling and division; machine-checked]
Let $W$ be any width-token word, $w=|W|$, and $q>0$. Start the combined program
at label 15 with zero local memory, width $W$ on port two, canonical $q$ digits
on port one, and every other stack empty. Define
\[
 T(w,q)=(4w+2)+\bigl(w(8\operatorname{size}(q)+13)+3\bigr),\qquad
\]
\[
 B(w,q)=15w+9+32\bigl(w(8\operatorname{size}(q)+13)+3\bigr).
\]
There exists a derived charge function $c$ on response words, with
$c(b)\le B(w,q)$ whenever $|b|=w$, such that the actual run for $T(w,q)$ source
transitions equals the following oracle query tree: query $w$ coins in order,
let $b$ be their chronological word, and return charge $c(b)$ and the halted
configuration with zero local memory, canonical digits of
$\operatorname{bitsValue}(b)\bmod q$ on port zero, retained $q$ digits on port
one, and all six remaining stacks empty.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CoinModuloMachine}{CoinModuloMachine.run_source}

The loader's real call boundary supplies division's raw input.
A checked
label invariant keeps division outside the coin region; it returns through a
separate halt label.
The proof derives each division charge and the common
clock, using the actual response-word length.
Numeric observation of the
produced digits equals the original \texttt{sampleFairBitModulo} query tree,
with its coin events mapped to the raw coin port.
Modulo is executed here;
observation only interprets digits already produced by instructions.
\companchor{CoinModuloMachine}{CoinModuloMachine.sampler_value}

Five kernel controls retain full states and unused coin suffixes: $6\bmod5=1$,
nonpalindromic $2\bmod3=2$, unit-modulus zero, empty-width no-query execution,
and the live loader-to-division handoff.
Independent composed interpreters pass
2,032 exhaustive cases (width 0--6, $q=1$--16) and 750 seeded cases
(7/19/41; width 0--64, $q=1$--4096), detecting wrong-input-port and
restart-loader mutants; failures and gaveUp are zero.
Fixture charges use the
proved division upper bound, not a measurement of exact branch costs.
\companchor{CoinModuloMachineControls}{CoinModuloMachineControls.input_order}
\companchor{CoinModuloMachineControls}{CoinModuloMachineControls.live_handoff}

Width and modulus remain loaded operands.
The following result delivers the
produced digits to the existing scalar-prefix writer.
Serialized preparation is
connected in Section~\ref{sec:prepared-scalar}.
Physical cache-caller composition,
protocol/reduction efficiency and intended standard-PPT coverage are outside this component's guarantee.

\subsection{Historical component: Serialization of the executed scalar}\label{sec:scalar-write}
The existing prefix writer now reads the sampler's remainder port directly.
It reuses the checked framing algorithm through finite coordinate changes;
no host operation decodes the remainder and reloads its digits.
Define
$E(n)=1^{\operatorname{size}(n)}\,0\,\operatorname{bits}(n)$, with digits
in little-endian order.
On canonical input digits, the writer takes exactly
$3\operatorname{size}(n)+3$ transitions, emits $E(n)$ before an arbitrary
suffix, clears its work ports, and preserves the modulus and two extra ports.
\companchor{ScalarWriteCode}{ScalarWriteCode.run_digits}
\companchor{ScalarWriteCode}{ScalarWriteCode.local_cost}

\begin{theorem}[Two-phase scalar serialization; machine-checked]\label{thm:scalar-write}
Let $W$ be any width-token word, $w=|W|$, and $q>0$. Run the preceding sampler
from its specified initial state for $T(w,q)$ transitions. Pass all eight
result stacks and local memory unchanged to the writer, setting only its entry
label, and run it for $D(q)=3\operatorname{size}(q-1)+3$ transitions.
There is a derived charge function $c$ such that, for every response word $b$
of length $w$,
\[
 c(b)\le B(w,q)+32D(q).
\]
This two-phase computation has exactly the oracle query tree that queries
$w$ chronological coins and returns charge $c(b)$ with the halted writer
configuration: memory 2, $E(\operatorname{bitsValue}(b)\bmod q)$ on port five,
canonical $q$ digits retained on port one, and every other port empty.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ScalarWriteCode}{ScalarWriteCode.from_sampler}
\companchor{ScalarWriteCode}{ScalarWriteCode.sampleWord_source}

Observation of port five is the existing finite modulo sampler followed by
$E$, with its coin events mapped to the raw port, before choosing any
probability handler.
The bound uses $n<q$ for the actual computed remainder;
it does not assume a typed scalar or an encoding correspondence.
\companchor{ScalarWriteCode}{ScalarWriteCode.sampleWord_value}

Six kernel-checked controls cover the complete frame, wrong-endian rejection,
zero's required delimiter, and sampled two/zero with untouched future coins.
Independent fixtures pass 3,840 exhaustive and 750 seeded cases (7/19/41,
scalar widths up to 256 bits), detecting reversed digits and omitted delimiters;
failures and gaveUp are zero.
\companchor{ScalarWriteCodeControls}{ScalarWriteCodeControls.sampled_two}
\companchor{ScalarWriteCodeControls}{ScalarWriteCodeControls.omitted_zero_fails}

The theorem concerns the explicit two-phase driver.
The next result compiles
its boundary into one uninterrupted program.
The following sections connect
its encoded output to the actual adaptive caller and execute serialized
width/modulus preparation.
Complete physical cache-caller composition and the
all-PPT endpoint are outside this component's guarantee; the sampling loss is unchanged.

\subsection{Historical component: Uninterrupted coin-to-prefix execution}\label{sec:coin-scalar}
The fixed program now contains the sampler's 20 labels followed by the existing
writer's eight labels, on the same eight stacks and three local-memory values.
The sampler's terminal instruction jumps to writer entry 25; the writer's halt
is final.
No host resumption occurs between the algorithms.

\begin{theorem}[Uninterrupted scalar sampler; machine-checked]
For any width-token word $W$, $w=|W|$, and $q>0$, run the fixed program for
$T(w,q)+D(q)$ transitions from label 15, memory zero, width $W$ on port two,
canonical $q$ digits on port one, and every other stack empty.
Its complete oracle tree and accumulated charge equal those of
Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-write}, with the final label representation changed to this program's
label type. Consequently there is a derived $c$ with
\[
 c(b)\le B(w,q)+32D(q)\qquad (|b|=w),
\]
and the run queries $w$ chronological coins and returns $c(b)$ and the halted
configuration with memory 2, $E(\operatorname{bitsValue}(b)\bmod q)$ on port five,
$q$ digits on port one, and every other stack empty.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CoinScalarMachine}{CoinScalarMachine.run_eq}
\companchor{CoinScalarMachine}{CoinScalarMachine.run_source}

Port-five observation equals the existing modulo sampler followed by $E$,
before any probability handler is chosen.
All code is fixed independently of
$W$ and $q$; their preparation remains outside this loaded-operand theorem.
\companchor{CoinScalarMachine}{CoinScalarMachine.sampler_value}

The return-link proof proceeds through actual oracle transitions and returns
as soon as the source halts.
It derives source-leaf termination from the
sampler theorem and continuation termination from the writer theorem, retaining
complete configurations and charges.
Only final halted configurations are padded.
\companchor{BitOracleReturnLink}{BitOracleReturnLink.run}

\paragraph{Why continuation termination is necessary.}
A retained checked counterexample starts with a one-step halt and redirects it
to a looping coin query.
A source clock of two and continuation clock of one
produce one query in the staged execution but two in the uninterrupted execution.
Thus removing the continuation-halt condition invalidates the composition claim.
\companchor{CoinScalarMachineControls}{CoinScalarMachineControls.live_padding_fails}

Six kernel controls cover sampled two/one, empty-width zero, the actual live
return, final halted padding, and that counterexample.
Independent stack traces
pass 2,032 exhaustive and 750 seeded cases (7/19/41, widths 0--64, $q=1$--4096),
detecting a cleared remainder at entry, reversed writer digits and an extra
query after halt.
Failures and gaveUp are zero.
The complete prepared sampler is
connected in Section~\ref{sec:prepared-scalar}.
The surrounding physical cache
caller and protocol efficiency/PPT coverage remain separate obligations.

\subsection{Historical component: Cache requests with executed sampling}\label{sec:cache-coins}
A word-facing handler now probes the existing encoded cache.
On a hit, its
parsed scalar digits go directly to the existing prefix writer.
On a miss,
\texttt{CoinScalarMachine} supplies the encoded scalar word directly to the
existing insertion and chronological log-append routines.
It returns the
encoded answer, cache and log.
Probe, insertion or append failure remains
explicit as an absent result; the following theorem concerns canonical source
inputs.
For a scalar $a\in\mathbb Z_q$, write $E(a)$ for $E(a.\mathrm{val})$.
The reference exact-uniform handler is retained.
\companchor{CacheHashCoins}{CacheHashCoins.sample_eq}
\companchor{CacheHashCoins}{CacheHashCoins.writeDigits_encode}

\begin{theorem}[Executed cache sampling and full-state error; machine-checked]
Let $p,q>0$, let $K$ be any typed hash key in the existing public group, and let
$(C,L)$ be any finite cache and chronological miss log. Supply their original
encodings to the word-facing handler, with a width-token word $W$, $w=|W|$.
If $C(K)=a$, it returns $(E(a),\operatorname{enc}(C),\operatorname{enc}(L))$
without a coin query. If $K$ is absent, its exact oracle tree samples the
existing width-$w$ finite modulo sampler, obtains scalar $a$, and returns
\[
 \bigl(E(a),\operatorname{enc}(C[K\mapsto a]),
                 \operatorname{enc}(L\mathbin{+\!+}[K])\bigr).
\]
All these canonical results are present. For $W$ consisting of
$\operatorname{size}(q)+s$ tokens, the total variation distance between the
complete encoded handler output and the encoded output of the original finite
logged source, interpreted with its canonical exact-uniform scalar sampler,
is at most $2^{-s}$.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheHashCoins}{CacheHashCoins.hash_hit}
\companchor{CacheHashCoins}{CacheHashCoins.hash_miss}
\companchor{CacheHashCoins}{CacheHashCoins.hash_distance}

The comparison is tied to \texttt{ballotFiniteLoggedImpl}, including its
retained state.
Its exact challenge interpreter is the pinned library's actual
\texttt{uniformSample} on $\mathbb Z_q$.
The proof uses contraction of variation
distance through the common answer/cache/log continuation; it does not assert
exact uniformity of fixed-width modulo sampling.
\companchor{CacheHashCoins}{CacheHashCoins.hash_source}
\companchor{CacheHashCoins}{CacheHashCoins.exactChallenge_value}

Five kernel controls cover a late hit, sampled six with a nonempty old log,
zero-width sampling and delimiter insertion, exclusion of eager hit sampling,
and a concrete full-output error bound.
Independent interpreters pass 576
exhaustive cases at $q=11$, four coin bits and up to two existing entries,
detecting hit resampling, insertion of a wrong scalar word and log prepending;
failures and gaveUp are zero.
\companchor{CacheHashCoinsControls}{CacheHashCoinsControls.miss_control}

This is a one-request word-facing driver composed from the executed routines.
It still loads operands and sequences those routines through host control.
The complete adaptive host caller is connected in the next result.
Physical
cache-caller port transfers and whole-caller runtime are outside this component's guarantee.
Serialized
operand preparation and the physical sampler are connected below.

\subsection{Historical component: Complete adaptive callers with executed sampling}\label{sec:cache-caller-coins}

The caller now passes each hash to the word-facing handler and decodes its
actual returned scalar prefix for the next source continuation.
Ordinary
uniform-range requests execute the original upper bound's increment and use
the resulting range in the same scalar sampler.
A malformed result remains explicit failure.
Every later request sees
the returned cache and chronological log.
The existing codecs, source-caller
induction and adaptive sampling-error theorem supply the connection.
\companchor{CacheCallerCoins}{CacheCallerCoins.sampleUpper_eq}

For positive $p,q$, let $P$ be any existing ballot-oracle computation returning
an arbitrary type $A$, and let $S=(C,L)$ be any finite cache and miss log.
Define
$J(P,S)$ to be the original \texttt{runBallotFiniteLogged} interpreted with
\texttt{CacheHashCoins.exactChallenge}, including its original exact uniform
scalar sampler.
Let $R_s$ be the existing \texttt{runFairBitUniform} transformation
with slack $s$, and let $\mathcal E$ encode the final cache and log while retaining
the return value.
The implemented caller $H_s$ supplies width tokens of length
$\operatorname{size}(r)+s$ for each requested positive range $r$.

\begin{theorem}[Complete caller correspondence and error; machine-checked]
For every $s,P,S$, the entire coin-query trees satisfy
\[
 H_s(P,\operatorname{enc}(C),\operatorname{enc}(L))
 = (\operatorname{some}\circ\mathcal E)_* R_s(J(P,S)).
\]
No successor-state or source-correspondence premise is supplied. If the original
caller has total query bound $B$, then $J(P,S)$ has total range-query bound $B$,
and the complete observed distributions satisfy
\[
 \operatorname{TV}\!\left(
  \mathsf{Law}(H_s(P,\operatorname{enc}(C),\operatorname{enc}(L))),
  (\operatorname{some}\circ\mathcal E)_*\mathsf{Law}(J(P,S))
 \right) \le B\,2^{-s}.
\]
Here $\mathsf{Law}$ interprets sampling probabilistically. Query bounds constrain
all source continuations; they are not runtime bounds.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheCallerCoins}{CacheCallerCoins.run_eq}
\companchor{CacheCallerCoins}{CacheCallerCoins.source_bound}
\companchor{CacheCallerCoins}{CacheCallerCoins.distance}

Five kernel controls cover an adaptive miss/hit/miss trace, all-hit continuations,
a unit range, exclusion of a changed repeated answer, and full-caller error.
Independent interpreters pass 6,144 exhaustive and 300 seeded cases
(seeds 7/19/41, modulus 11, slack 0--3), including later keys chosen from earlier
answers.
Hit-resampling, cache-reset and dropped-log mutants are detected;
failures and gaveUp are zero.
\companchor{CacheCallerCoinsControls}{CacheCallerCoinsControls.adaptive_control}
\companchor{CacheCallerCoinsControls}{CacheCallerCoinsControls.hits_control}

\paragraph{Component boundary.}
Its Helios dependencies are the ballot-key/scalar codecs and original
finite logged handler; prepared-election and replay-source computations are
potential callers.
Decoding and invoking typed continuations remain host
operations, as do constructing and loading routine input records.
The sampler
now executes serialized width/modulus preparation as described below.
This
result connects complete adaptive behavior.
It does not compile the caller to
one physical machine or derive the combined execution cost.

\subsection{Historical component: Serialized operands and the complete physical sampler}\label{sec:prepared-scalar}

The sampler's local administrative input is $I(s,n)=1^s0E(n)$, using the
existing natural-prefix format $E$.
Two finite entry labels preserve the two
source interfaces.
Direct entry reads modulus $q=n>0$; uniform entry reads the
original inclusive upper bound $n$ and executes binary increment to $q=n+1$.
The code parses $E$, counts modulus digits while restoring them, and adds the
slack tokens.
A common gate checks successful preparation and complete input
consumption before entering the coin sampler.
These local input records add no
public election observations.

\begin{theorem}[Executed operand preparation; machine-checked]
Start with $I(s,n)$ followed by any suffix, empty work ports and arbitrary private
frame ports. The corresponding entry prepares literal $q$-digits and exactly
$\operatorname{size}(q)+s$ width tokens, preserves the suffix and private frame,
and clears scratch. The following clocks bound the actual preparation:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|l}
\text{entry}&q&P(s,n)\\\hline
\text{direct}&n>0&s+5\operatorname{size}(n)+8\\
\text{uniform}&n+1&s+5\operatorname{size}(n)+2\operatorname{size}(n+1)+10.
\end{array}
\]
The compiled preparation has charge at most $32P(s,n)$. Direct modulus zero
reaches explicit failure; uniform upper bound zero prepares range one.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{SamplerOperands}{SamplerOperands.run}
\companchor{SamplerOperands}{SamplerOperands.uniform_run}
\companchor{SamplerOperands}{SamplerOperands.zero_rejected}
\companchor{SamplerOperands}{SamplerOperands.charged}

Write $w=\operatorname{size}(q)+s$.
The already checked sampler clocks and charges
used in the composition are
\[
\begin{aligned}
 S(w,q)&=(4w+2)+\bigl(w(8\operatorname{size}(q)+13)+3\bigr)
                  +3\operatorname{size}(q-1)+3,\\
 C(w,q)&=15w+9+32\bigl(w(8\operatorname{size}(q)+13)+3\bigr)\\
       &\quad+32\bigl(3\operatorname{size}(q-1)+3\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]
These are source execution bounds.
The following composition also derives the
physical input-loading and compiler obligations.

\begin{theorem}[Prepared sampler and physical execution; machine-checked]
For either entry with $q>0$, supply exactly $I(s,n)$ on its raw input port and
leave work storage blank. One fixed program executes preparation, the gate,
coin loading, division and scalar-prefix writing. At clock $P(s,n)+1+S(w,q)$,
its entire query tree draws $w$ chronological coins $b$ and returns
$E(\operatorname{value}(b)\bmod q)$, retaining the modulus and clearing scratch.
Every branch has charge at most
\[
 B=32P(s,n)+5+C(w,q).
\]
The encoded observation equals the original width-$w$ fair-bit range sampler.
Starting from a physical raw input tape and blank work storage, there exists a
startup duration $u\le4|I(s,n)|+8$ such that the same complete query-tree
observation is obtained at physical clock
\[
 u+650\bigl(|I(s,n)|+B+1\bigr)^2 B G,
\]
where $G$ is the fixed compiled-program factor. Termination, source cost, input
loading and correspondence are derived for this component. The bounded oracle
adapter may use any limit: the source tree contains only coin requests.
\end{theorem}

\companchor{PreparedScalarMachine}{PreparedScalarMachine.physical_run}

\paragraph{Caller connection and controls.}
The actual adaptive caller now uses \texttt{hashPrepared} on hash misses and
\texttt{sampleUpper} on ordinary range requests.
Their equality laws preserve
its existing complete return/cache/log correspondence and sampling-error bound.
The prior arbitrary-width sampler and cache handler remain checked references.

Eight kernel controls cover literal widths and frames, carry growth, the unit
range, direct zero rejection, malformed inputs, sampled output, pre-coin gating
and physical unit-range execution retaining its coin query.
Independent operand
checks pass 6,966 canonical, 4,094 raw and 750 seeded cases.
Complete sampler
traces pass 3,059 exhaustive, 10 directed malformed and 300 seeded cases, using
the actual prepared ports without numeric modulus reloading.
Updated adaptive
caller checks also pass.
The campaigns use seeds 7/19/41; failures and gaveUp
are zero.
They detect skipped increment, lost slack, reversed digits, bypassed
gating, overwritten modulus and extra coins, in addition to the retained caller
mutations.
Runtime checks use derived upper bounds, not exact measured charges.

\paragraph{Reuse and remaining boundary.}
The preparation interface depends on the existing natural format and uniform
upper-bound convention; its potential callers are cache misses and adaptive
range requests.
It reuses the prefix parser, incrementer, stack frame/return
interfaces, scalar sampler and physical loader/compiler.
The only new data loop
constructs width tokens while restoring modulus digits.
The combined physical
sampler is a component result.
The surrounding cache caller still constructs
its input records, transports routine arguments/results, decodes and invokes
typed continuations through host code.

\clearpage
\subsection{Historical component: Sampler execution from retained caller storage}\label{sec:cache-sampler-frame}
The cache caller uses eight work ports and four retained ports: key on 8,
cache on 9, chronological log on 10 and sampler operand record on 11.
A frame translation maps the existing deterministic instructions using their
verified interface and relocates oracle request/reply ports.
Every extra word
is retained, including across coin and hash responses.
For every code, initial
configuration, frame and finite clock, the complete query tree commutes with
this translation; every leaf has the translated configuration and the exact
original charge.
This is an execution theorem, with no termination or
response-size premise.
\companchor{BitOracleStackFrame}{BitOracleStackFrame.local_cost}
\companchor{BitOracleStackFrame}{BitOracleStackFrame.step}
\companchor{BitOracleStackFrame}{BitOracleStackFrame.run}

\begin{theorem}[Sampler branch with executed caller transfer; machine-checked]
Let $q>0$, $s\in\mathbb N$ and $R=I(s,q)$. Start with $R$ only on retained
port 11, arbitrary key/cache/log words on ports 8--10, and all eight work ports
empty. A fixed program copies $R$ to input port 4 through scratch port 0,
restores port 11, and enters the preceding prepared sampler through an actual
return label. If its preceding clock and charge bound are $T_p$ and $B_p$,
the combined clock and charge bound are
\[
 T_c=2|R|+3+T_p,\qquad B_c=10|R|+12+B_p.
\]
The complete query tree draws $w=\operatorname{size}(q)+s$ chronological coins
$b$, returns $E(\operatorname{bitsValue}(b)\bmod q)$ on port 5, retains modulus
digits on 1, clears the remaining work ports, and preserves all four retained
records. Each branch has its actual accumulated charge, bounded by $B_c$.
The returned scalar word has the original fair-bit range-sampler observation.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheHashMachine}{CacheHashMachine.sample_run}
\companchor{CacheHashMachine}{CacheHashMachine.sample_value}

Both return-link termination premises follow from the executed copy and sampler
results.
The actual adaptive caller's miss branch uses this program via
\texttt{preparedHash}; its existing complete return/cache/log correspondence and
sampling-error bound are unchanged.
Initial record construction and surrounding
routine dispatch still use host operations.
\companchor{CacheHashCoins}{CacheHashCoins.preparedHash_eq}

\paragraph{Deriving the next routine's workspace.}
The complete cache-read post-state now exposes empty work on a miss.
A hit
instead retains the unread suffix and a nonzero counter, as well as parsed
scalar digits and the original query/cache.
The caller must clear or move
these actual words before entering a routine that requires empty scratch.
A kernel control proves nonempty counter storage even for a one-entry hit.

\companchor{CacheReadMachine}{CacheReadMachine.hit_run}

Seven kernel controls retain the input-placement, live-copy, full sampled
state, zero-failure and permuted-oracle boundaries; a misrouted coin destroys
a literal private word.
Independent frame tests pass 2,268 exhaustive and
600 seeded cases; complete caller-copy sampling passes 1,421 exhaustive and
300 seeded cases, with seeds 7/19/41 and the documented routing, replacement,
record-loss and cache-loss mutations detected.
Updated adaptive caller fixtures
also pass.
Failures and gaveUp are zero; Python charge checks use derived

\paragraph{Interface and remaining boundary.}
The frame lemma depends only on the existing finite oracle-machine interface.
Its immediate caller is this sampler branch; cache/replay routines can use it
when suspending with private storage.
The concrete branch uses the existing
Helios natural record and cache-caller layout.
Its input-transfer and sampling
charge are derived, but the complete probe/hit/insert/append dispatcher,
its workspace transfers and its loaded physical execution cost are discharged
in Section~\ref{sec:cache-dispatch}.
Section~\ref{sec:cache-request-input} adds
serialized request input loading.

\subsection{Historical component: Complete cache requests in finite control}\label{sec:cache-dispatch}
The complete request now executes the probe, hit writer or miss sampler,
cache insertion and chronological log append in one fixed program.
The theorem
covers canonical records of every typed finite cache, key and log.
It derives
the intermediate workspace, return flags, termination and combined charge.
The enclosing input link is proved in Section~\ref{sec:cache-request-input}.
Effective caller continuations are outside this component's guarantee.
General raw-input cache validation
is not claimed here.

\paragraph{Complete caller states.}
Write $C,K,L,V$ for the original cache, key, log and scalar codecs, and let
$R=I(s,q)$ be the retained sampler record of Section~\ref{sec:prepared-scalar}.
The following notation compresses the finite label and memory types without
changing any data.
The entry has label $0$, memory $0$ and stacks
\[
 [C,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,
   K,\epsilon,L,R].
\]
Write $F(K,C,L,R,V)$ for the halted state with memory $2$ and stacks
\[
 [\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,V,K,C,L,R].
\]
Its request observation is $(V,C,L)$; retaining the other words in a proof does
not add a protocol disclosure.
The code and port layout are fixed independently
of $p,q,s$ and the input sizes.

\paragraph{Source specification and derived bounds.}
Assume $p,q>0$, a typed finite cache $c$, key $k$, typed log $\ell$ and slack
$s\in\mathbb N$.
Put $w=\operatorname{size}(q)+s$ and let $W_w$ be the raw
chronological tree of $w$ fair-bit requests.
For a leaf $b$, let
$a_b=\operatorname{bitsValue}(b)\bmod q$, regarded as an element of
$\mathbb Z_q$.
The complete request specification is
\[
 Q(c,k,\ell,s)=
 \begin{cases}
  \operatorname{pure}F(K(k),C(c),L(\ell),R,V(a)),&c[k]=a,\\
  \bigl(b\mapsto F(K(k),C(c[k\mapsto a_b]),
       L(\ell\mathbin{+\!+}[((),k)]),R,V(a_b))\bigr)_*W_w,&c[k]\text{ absent}.
 \end{cases}
\]
The hit branch makes no oracle request.
The miss branch preserves the full tree,
not merely its tally or scalar marginal.

Here is a compressed transcription of the input-derived bounds.
Let $T_r$
be the existing probe bound, $T_s,B_s$ the copied-sampler clock and charge,
$T_i$ the insertion bound and $T_a$ the append/transfer bound.
All refer to the
actual original routines on these encoded inputs.
Set
\[
 \begin{split}
 T_a&=1+\operatorname{AppendCost}(|\ell|,\operatorname{keyBound}(p),|L(\ell)|)
         +1+3|L(\ell\mathbin{+\!+}[((),k)])|+3,\\
 T_m&=3(2\operatorname{size}(q-1)+1)+\operatorname{size}(q)+5
          +3|C(c)|+3+T_i+T_a,\\
 H&=\operatorname{height}(\mathrm{entry})+T_r A_r,\qquad
 T_h=2(H+1)+3\operatorname{size}(q-1)+3.
 \end{split}
\]
$A_r$ is the fixed probe code-access bound.
Existing deterministic stack growth
derives $H$, including the unread suffix and nonzero hit counter.
The common
request clock and charge are
\[
 \begin{split}
 T&=\max\{1+T_r+2+T_s+T_m,\;1+T_r+1+T_h\},\\
 B&=\max\{2+32T_r+6+B_s+32T_m,\;2+32T_r+4+32T_h\}.
 \end{split}
\]
These are definitions computed from the inputs and fixed routine bounds; they
are not hypotheses asserting the missing execution theorem.

\begin{theorem}[Complete loaded-request execution]
For the inputs above, let $E$ be the actual charged execution of
\code{CacheHashDispatch.code} for clock $T$ from the entry state. Then
\[
 \operatorname{fst}_*E=Q(c,k,\ell,s),\qquad
 \forall(x,d)\in\operatorname{support}(E),\quad
     x.\mathrm{label}=\mathrm{none}\ \land\ d\le B.
\]
No lookup outcome, freshness witness, intermediate presentation or cost
certificate is an additional premise.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheHashDispatchRun}{CacheHashDispatch.request_run}
\companchor{CacheHashDispatchRun}{CacheHashDispatch.miss_run}
\companchor{CacheHashDispatchRun}{CacheHashDispatch.hit_run}

\paragraph{Why the composition closes.}
The probe's miss state supplies empty work storage and the actual absence
condition needed by guarded insertion.
Its hit state supplies parsed digits,
but also retained suffix/counter words; both are cleared by executed loops.
The miss path executes answer and cache copies, insertion, chronological append
and a final log transfer.
Every routine's complete post-state establishes its
successor's entry.
Final common-clock padding is applied only after halt.
The earlier live-return padding counterexample remains valid.

\companchor{CacheRoutineCode}{CacheRoutineCode.probe_hit}

\companchor{CacheHashDispatchRun}{CacheHashDispatch.hit_tail}

\begin{theorem}[Physical execution and existing caller observation]
The actual request discharges the existing physical compiler's
\code{Within} contract for every bounded-oracle policy. From the loaded caller
layout, the compiled primitive program agrees with $Q$ at clock
\[
 \operatorname{unitCost}(\operatorname{height}(\mathrm{entry})+B)\,B\,G,
\]
where $G$ is the fixed compiler factor of this code. Projecting the request
observation gives exactly the existing adaptive caller's
\code{CacheHashCoins.hashPrepared} tree, with coins lifted to raw requests.
\end{theorem}

\paragraph{Controls and reuse boundary.}
Seven kernel controls retain a hit's unconsumed entropy, literal values three,
six and zero, chronological append, nonconstant sampling and private storage.
The actual-code native harness passes 324 directed cases for moduli $1,3,11$,
slack $0,2$, three caches, three keys, two logs and three coin tapes.
It detects
always-sample, skipped-append and wrong-port cleanup mutations.
This finite
campaign has no random seeds or discarded cases; its 20,000-step fixture cap
is not a proved runtime bound.
The general theorem supplies that evidence.

The implementation reuses Mathlib's explicit-list finite equivalences and the
project's coordinate, frame, return-link and physical-compiler interfaces.
The label tables, original codecs and twelve-port layout are specific to this
Helios caller; no new generic compiler or Lake dependency is introduced.
The theorem supplies a proved request component for the adaptive caller.
That caller still constructs its enclosing input, decodes returned words
and invokes typed host continuations.
The following result supplies request
input loading and width bounds.

\subsection{Historical component: Serialized input to a complete cache request}\label{sec:cache-request-input}
One fixed program now consumes a serialized request on a conventional input
tape, parses its four fields, checks successful completion, and executes the
complete request above.
This removes the assumed loaded-port entry for this
component.
The four-field packaging is local machine input; it changes neither
the historical protocol nor its public observations.

\paragraph{Loader interface.}
For arbitrary bit words $K,C,L,R$, let
\[
 J=\operatorname{bitFieldsEncode}[K,C,L,R],\qquad
 f(n)=3\operatorname{size}(n)+7+n(2\operatorname{size}(n)+3),
\]
and set $A=13+f(|K|)+f(|C|)+f(|L|)+f(|R|)$.
The loader entry has label $0$, memory $0$, word $J$ on port $6$, and every
other port empty.
Its successful final state, denoted $U(K,C,L,R)$, has halted
control, memory $2$ and stacks
\[
 [C,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,
 K,\epsilon,L,R].
\]
The fixed outer header is $1110001$, the original encoding of four.
Each field
uses the existing length-prefix parser.
Previously read fields are outside the
next parser's local ports; its complete post-state restores the field and clears
scratch.
The final guard requires exhausted input.

\begin{theorem}[Complete request-input parsing]
For every four bit words, there are $u\le A$ and $d\le32u$ such that the actual
charged loader run for $u$ steps returns exactly
$\operatorname{pure}(U(K,C,L,R),d)$.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheRequestInputRun}{CacheRequestInput.load_request}
\companchor{CacheRequestInputRun}{CacheRequestInput.clock_lengths}
\companchor{CacheRequestInput}{CacheRequestInput.field_run}
This is canonical-input completeness.
Payloads are opaque to the loader;
general malformed-input rejection and inner Helios validity are not conclusions
of this theorem.
It also does not execute construction of $J$ by an outer caller.

\paragraph{The guarded combined run.}
Use the typed inputs, positive $p,q$, canonical codecs and slack $s$ from
Section~\ref{sec:cache-dispatch}, with $R=I(s,q)$.
The new program returns the
loader to a guard: memory $2$ executes a reset to $0$ and enters the dispatcher;
every other memory value executes rejection.
This transition costs three.
Let $T,B,Q$ be the preceding request clock, charge and complete query tree, and
let $\iota$ rename its control labels into the combined program, preserving
halted control, memory and all stacks.
Define
\[
 T_J=A+1+T,\qquad B_J=32A+3+B.
\]
\begin{theorem}[Complete serialized-request execution]
From $J$ on port $6$ and otherwise blank work, the actual combined execution
$E_J$ for $T_J$ steps satisfies
\[
 \operatorname{fst}_*E_J=\iota_*Q,\qquad
 \forall(x,d)\in\operatorname{support}(E_J),\quad
 x.\mathrm{label}=\mathrm{none}\ \land\ d\le B_J.
\]
The success guard, complete request and final-halt padding are executed or
derived. No loaded configuration, intermediate return, or cost certificate is
a caller-supplied premise.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheRequestMachineRun}{CacheRequestMachine.request_run}
\companchor{CacheRequestMachine}{CacheRequestMachine.enter_request}

\paragraph{Width interface.}
The request bound now eliminates typed cache/log counts using their encoded
lengths.
Write $c=|C|$, $l=|L|$, $k_p=\operatorname{keyBound}(p)$,
$r=s+2\operatorname{size}(q)+2$, and let $a_r$ be the fixed probe access bound.
The following is a transcription of \code{CacheHashDispatch.loadedClock}; the
named routine costs and \code{bitListSize} are the existing proved interfaces:
\[
\begin{aligned}
 P&=\operatorname{ReadCost}(p,q,c,c),\quad
 J_l=\operatorname{bitListSize}(k_p,l+1),\\
 E&=1+\operatorname{AppendCost}(l,k_p,l)+1+(3J_l+3),\\
 D&=3(2\operatorname{size}(q-1)+1)+\operatorname{size}(q)+5+3c+3
       +\operatorname{InsertCost}(p,q,c,c)+E,\\
 H&=k_p+c+l+r,\\
 \Lambda&=\max\bigl\{1+P+2+T_s+D,\\
 &\hspace{2em}1+P+1+2(H+Pa_r+1)+3\operatorname{size}(q-1)+3\bigr\}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Then $T\le\Lambda$ and $B\le32\Lambda$.
Consequently $T_J\le A+1+\Lambda$ and $B_J\le32(A+1+\Lambda)$.
The public $p$ width remains explicit: a short encoded identity does not bound
the ambient group width.
The retained sampler record contributes to $H$.
Appending a log entry may enlarge its outer count header; its length bound uses
$J_l$, not merely the old length plus a field.
\companchor{CacheHashDispatchBounds}{CacheHashDispatch.request_clock_le_loaded}
\companchor{CacheHashDispatchBounds}{CacheHashDispatch.request_cost_le_loaded}
\companchor{CacheHashDispatchBounds}{CacheHashDispatch.private_record_omission_control}
\companchor{CacheHashDispatchBounds}{CacheHashDispatch.append_header_control}
\companchor{CacheRequestMachineRun}{CacheRequestMachine.clock_le_loaded}
\companchor{CacheRequestMachineRun}{CacheRequestMachine.cost_le_loaded}

\begin{theorem}[Physical input and request execution]
Let $H_J$ be the height of the one-port source entry and $G_J$ the fixed
primitive compiler factor for the combined code. For every bounded-oracle
policy there is a startup length $v\le4|J|+8$ such that, from blank physical
work storage and conventional input tape $J$, execution for
\[
 v+\operatorname{unitCost}(H_J+B_J)B_JG_J
\]
has observed query tree $\operatorname{some}_*\iota_*Q$, under that same policy.
The source termination/charge contract is derived from the combined run.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheRequestMachineRun}{CacheRequestMachine.within}
\companchor{CacheRequestMachineRun}{CacheRequestMachine.physical_run}

\paragraph{Independent controls and remaining scope.}
The actual loader passes 3,260 independently encoded native fixtures: 1,009
accept and 2,251 reject.
Seeds are 118, 20260914 and 198, with 128 generated
valid records per seed, plus directed and exhaustive short-word cases.
Three
mutants are detected: swapped key/log ports, skipped fourth-field guard and
ignored suffix.
Their locally minimized witnesses are respectively
$11100010010100$, $1110001000$ and $111000100000$.
The combined program also passes 127 cases at $p=23,q=11$: 36 hits, 72 misses
and 19 malformed framing cases.
A guard bypass is detected by appending one
false bit to a typed hit input.
All final ports and remaining coins are checked.
Eleven kernel controls retain the hit/miss/zero values, coin consumption, log
order, private frame and rejection gate.
Two minimized framing failures are
checked on the actual combined run: missing fourth field in 36 steps with charge
136, and trailing suffix in 42 steps with charge 161.
\companchor{CacheRequestMachineControls}{CacheRequestMachineControls.hit_control}
\companchor{CacheRequestMachineControls}{CacheRequestMachineControls.miss_control}
\companchor{CacheRequestMachineControls}{CacheRequestMachineControls.rejected_gate_not_dispatched}
\companchor{CacheRequestMachineControls}{CacheRequestMachineControls.missing_fourth_field_control}
\companchor{CacheRequestMachineControls}{CacheRequestMachineControls.trailing_suffix_control}
No case is discarded or times out; these finite campaigns do not prove a
runtime bound or general rejection.
Their caps are 20,000 and 100,000 driver
steps, respectively.

The implementation reuses the existing parser, framing, coordinate, return-link
and physical-input/compiler proofs.
Its four-field grammar, destination ports
and dispatcher choice are specific to this caller; possible callers are the
actual Helios cache/sampling reduction paths.
It assumes canonical typed payloads
only for the request theorem and adds no cryptographic assumption.
Neither this component nor
its width bounds establish all-PPT computational secrecy.

\subsection{Historical component: Bounds at original request boundaries}\label{sec:cache-request-prefix}
The request component now has a polynomial bound specialized to reachable
historical source states.
This closes its width/budget obligation for the request component;
it does not execute the enclosing adaptive caller.

\paragraph{Original-source observations.}
For a finite logged source $P$ and initial state $s=(c,\ell)$, write
$\mathcal R(P,s)$ for its original interpreter.
The analysis interpreter
$\widehat{\mathcal R}(P,s,E)$ also carries a list of events, each containing an
original request and its input state.
At request $t$ with continuation $k$, it
runs the unchanged original handler to obtain $(a,s')$, then continues as
\[
 \widehat{\mathcal R}(k(a),s',E\mathbin{+\!+}[(t,s)]).
\]
Thus cache hits and uniform requests are retained even when they generate no
fresh hash challenge.
These events are proof instrumentation: they add neither
public observations nor a claimed executed storage cost.

\begin{theorem}[Capture and source-derived storage]
Erasing the event list from $\widehat{\mathcal R}(P,s,E)$ gives exactly
$\mathcal R(P,s)$ as an oracle query tree. Every supported original result has
a supported instrumented result with that same answer and final state.
Existing events remain an ordered prefix through every supported continuation.
If $P$ has hash-query budget $n$ and the initial event list is empty, every
recorded event $(t,(c',\ell'))$ satisfies
\[
 |c'|\le |c|+n,\qquad |\ell'|\le |\ell|+n.
\]
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheRequestPrefixes}{CacheRequestPrefixes.run_eq}
\companchor{CacheRequestPrefixes}{CacheRequestPrefixes.supported_lift}
\companchor{CacheRequestPrefixes}{CacheRequestPrefixes.query_run}
\companchor{CacheRequestPrefixes}{CacheRequestPrefixes.events_retained}
\companchor{CacheRequestPrefixes}{CacheRequestPrefixes.request_captured}
\companchor{CacheRequestPrefixes}{CacheRequestPrefixes.prestates_bound}
The generic observer needs decidable key equality, with no cryptographic or
sampling-law premise.
Each original handler step grows cache and log by at most
one on a hash request and by zero on a uniform.
Induction consumes the source's
hash budget at those original boundaries.
Final-state storage bounds alone would
not establish this result.

\paragraph{Polynomial request bound.}
Let $a_r$ again be the fixed probe access bound and put
$M=10^6(a_r+1)$.
For the complete loaded clock $\Lambda_J$ from the previous
section, arbitrary natural parameters satisfy
\[
 \Lambda_J(p,q,K,C,L,s)
 \le M\bigl(\operatorname{size}(p)+\operatorname{size}(q)+K+C+L+s+1\bigr)^3.
\]
Here $K,C,L$ are payload lengths, not entry counts.
The theorem covers zero
arithmetic parameters; validity of the cryptographic request still requires
positive moduli.
Polynomial bounds on the six displayed inputs give an explicit
polynomial bound on $\Lambda_J$.
\companchor{CacheRequestPolynomial}{CacheRequestMachine.loadedClock_le_cubic}
\companchor{CacheRequestPolynomial}{CacheRequestMachine.loadedClock_polynomial}

For positive $p,q$, let $k_p$ be the existing key codec bound,
$C_{p,q}(c)$ the complete cache codec bound and $L_p(l)$ the complete log codec
bound, including their outer count headers.
Define
\[
 \mathcal B(p,q,c,l,s)=
 M\bigl(\operatorname{size}(p)+\operatorname{size}(q)+k_p+
 C_{p,q}(c)+L_p(l)+s+1\bigr)^3.
\]
\begin{theorem}[Executed request bounds on historical source states]
At every captured prestate of a source with initial counts $(c,l)$ and hash
budget $n$, the complete request clock for any typed hash key is at most
$\mathcal B(p,q,c+n,l+n,s)$. If the captured request is that hash query, every
supported leaf of its actual request-machine execution halts and has charge
at most $32\mathcal B(p,q,c+n,l+n,s)$.

For the historical explicit-nonce repaired-submission source, initialized with
empty cache and log, the previously proved attacker hash budget $n$ yields the
bound $\mathcal B(p,q,n+15,n+15,s)$ at every captured request state.
Polynomial bounds on public modulus widths, the count budgets and slack give
an explicit polynomial bound on $\mathcal B$; codec/header length bounds are
derived rather than assumed.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheRequestPrefixBounds}{CacheRequestPrefixes.encoded_prestates_bound}
\companchor{CacheRequestPrefixBounds}{CacheRequestPrefixes.request_clock_bound}
\companchor{CacheRequestPrefixBounds}{CacheRequestPrefixes.hash_request_execution_bound}
\companchor{CacheRequestPrefixBounds}{CacheRequestPrefixes.repaired_source_request_clock_bound}
\companchor{CacheRequestPrefixBounds}{CacheRequestPrefixes.requestBound_polynomial}
The clock/charge assertions use the actual request program and its established
run theorem.
They are not cost certificates for a missing caller correspondence.
Arbitrary uniform request ranges are still outside this hash-width bound.

\paragraph{Independent controls and remaining connection.}
The arithmetic gate evaluates the actual Lean clock on 788 fixtures; Python
independently computes the proposed cap.
Seeds 606, 20260914 and 118 contribute
200 cases each, alongside directed boundary/unbalanced cases.
Omitting either
loaded key length or public group width is refuted, with kernel witnesses.
The public-width mutant initially survived smaller inputs; increasing unbalanced
widths exposed it.
This was insufficient test strength, not evidence of validity.
\companchor{CacheRequestPolynomial}{CacheRequestMachine.cubic_zero_control}
\companchor{CacheRequestPolynomial}{CacheRequestMachine.cubic_loaded_control}
\companchor{CacheRequestPolynomial}{CacheRequestMachine.key_length_omission_control}
\companchor{CacheRequestPolynomial}{CacheRequestMachine.public_width_omission_control}
The observer gate checks 1,278 typed executions against independently computed
answers, cache order, log, prestates and unused randomness: all operation words
of length at most three across three caches and two logs, plus 256 cases per
seed 118, 198 and 20260914.
Mutants omitting hits, omitting uniforms or recording
poststates are all detected.
Neither campaign discards a case or gives up.

\subsection{Historical component: Resident caller entry and guarded return}\label{sec:cache-caller-entry}
The next caller region retains cache and log on the dispatcher's existing ports.
It executes the transfers needed for another request, including clearing an old
answer, and preserves a private saved-data word.
This avoids serializing and
immediately reparsing the same resident state.

\paragraph{Entry interface.}
Let $K,C,L,R,V,S$ be key, cache, chronological log, sampler record, previous
answer and saved data.
The initial local stacks are
\[
 [\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,V,K,C,L,R],
\]
with one extra port holding $S$.
The fixed code clears port $7$, executes the
original cache copy from $9$ to $0$ via scratch $2$, clears $9$, and enters the
original dispatcher.
Its request return goes through a guard: memory $2$
succeeds; memory $0$ or $1$ rejects.
The extra saved port is framed across both
deterministic operations and oracle responses.

Put
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 U&=(|V|+1)+(2|C|+2)+(|C|+1),\\
 D&=4(|V|+1)+5(2|C|+2)+4(|C|+1).
 \end{aligned}
\]
\begin{theorem}[Executed resident entry]
For arbitrary resident words, actual execution for exactly $U$ ticks reaches
the live dispatcher entry with its required stacks
$[C,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,K,
\epsilon,L,R]$ and unchanged saved word $S$. Its charge is at most $D$.
No intermediate scratch, transfer or first-return certificate is assumed.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheCallerMachineRun}{CacheCallerMachine.load_request}
The live boundary uses an exact recurrence for the existing copy instructions.
The previously retained counterexample to padding a live return still applies;
no such padding is used here.

\begin{theorem}[Resident request and return]
Take positive $p,q$, typed canonical key/cache/log and $R=I(s,q)$.
Let $F,B,Q$ be the original dispatcher's request clock, charge bound and full
oracle tree, and let $\iota_S$ rename its result labels and add saved port $S$.
The actual resident caller execution for $U+F+1$ ticks has projected tree
$\iota_{S*}Q$. Every supported leaf halts with charge at most $D+B+3$.
The complete result and saved data are retained, and the return guard is executed.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{CacheCallerMachineRun}{CacheCallerMachine.request_run}
\companchor{CacheCallerMachineSource}{CacheCallerMachine.execution_source}
The observation theorem matches the existing host caller's complete cache
request interface.
It does not compile that caller's arbitrary Lean continuation.

\paragraph{Cost and physical execution.}
The combined charge is at most $32(U+F+1)$.
At a captured original-source state
with initial cache/log counts $(c,l)$ and hash budget $n$, the resident clock is
bounded by
\[
 |V|+3C_{p,q}(c+n)+5+\mathcal B(p,q,c+n,l+n,s),
\]
using the source and codec bounds from the preceding section.
Old-answer length remains explicit until actual source decoding/production is
connected.
Let $H$ be the full resident stack height, including $S$, let
$B_r=D+B+3$, and let $G_r$ be the fixed primitive compiler factor.
The existing compiler executes the resident request at time
\[
 \operatorname{unitCost}(H+B_r)B_rG_r
\]
from its lowered resident configuration, with the same bounded-oracle policy
on both sides and observed tree $\operatorname{some}_*\iota_{S*}Q$.
Its required termination/charge contract is derived from the actual run.

\companchor{CacheCallerMachineSource}{CacheCallerMachine.source_clock_bound}
\companchor{CacheCallerMachineSource}{CacheCallerMachine.within}
\companchor{CacheCallerMachineSource}{CacheCallerMachine.physical_run}
This is physical execution from resident storage.
It does not assume polynomial
saved-context width or execution of an enclosing source initialization.

\paragraph{Independent controls and boundary.}
The native campaign checks all thirteen ports, return flags, halting and exact
coin consumption on 525 cases: 324 directed, 192 seeded (64 each for 118, 198,
20260914) and nine return-gate cases.
Its 200 hits and 316 misses include nonempty
cache/log, old answers and saved data.
All four actual-code mutations are
identified: skipped answer cleanup, skipped cache cleanup, corrupted saved port
and forced-success return.
Single-bit deletion shrinks old/saved words while
holding typed request/cache/log fixed; no global minimality is claimed.
There are no discarded cases or gaveUp results.
The observed maximum of 17,471
ticks under the 100,000 fixture cap is not the general time theorem.
Nine kernel controls retain stored three, sampled six, the complete layouts,
unused randomness, nonempty saved data and the return policy.
A second invocation
reuses the actual returned configuration with only control/memory reset; it hits
the inserted key without another coin or log append.
This is an entry control,
not a compiled general continuation.

Its specific ports and codecs are Helios

\subsection{Historical component: Executed nonzero-nonce response}\label{sec:nonce-successor}
The historical nonce sampler draws $i\in\operatorname{Fin}(q-1)$ and returns
$i+1$ in $\mathbb Z_q$.
Its response continuation is now an actual finite-control
program.
It takes the canonical natural prefix $E(i)$ followed by arbitrary
suffix $T$, with three opaque saved words $S_0,S_1,S_2$:
\[
 [\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,E(i)T,S_0,S_1,S_2].
\]
The program pushes the zero-slack delimiter, reuses the existing uniform
operand preparation (which parses and increments), clears its width markers,
and runs the existing scalar writer under port permutation
$[1,5,2,3,0,4,6,7]$.
Only fixed control and coordinates are added.

\begin{theorem}[Executed historical nonce successor]
For every natural $n$, suffix $T$ and saved frame $S$, actual execution at clock
\[
 B(n)=1+(5\,\operatorname{size}(n)+2\,\operatorname{size}(n+1)+10)
       +(\operatorname{size}(n+1)+1)+(3\,\operatorname{size}(n+1)+3)+1
\]
halts with success memory $2$ and complete stack vector
\[
 [\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,E(n+1)T,S_0,S_1,S_2].
\]
There exists its actual charged execution value $c\leq32B(n)$, with no coin or
hash requests. For prime $q$ and $i\in\operatorname{Fin}(q-1)$, the output prefix
is precisely $\operatorname{scalarEncode}(\operatorname{primeNonceValue}(i))$.
\end{theorem}

\companchor{PrimeNonceMachineRun}{PrimeNonceMachine.successor_run}
\companchor{PrimeNonceMachineRun}{PrimeNonceMachine.local_cost}
\companchor{PrimeNonceMachineRun}{PrimeNonceMachine.charged}
The return linker derives actual subroutine durations within their bounds;
padding occurs only after the complete successor halts.
Canonical input is
explicit: this is not a malformed-input rejection theorem.
The following section derives that input from the actual sampler.

\paragraph{Controls and remaining connection.}
Six kernel controls retain $0\mapsto1$ (the $q=2$ boundary), $3\mapsto4$ with
a suffix and distinct saved words, nonidentity, and counterexamples to bypassed
increment, skipped width cleanup and corrupted saved storage.

width 256 with arbitrary suffix/frame words.
No cases are discarded or gaveUp.
All three injected defects are detected and shrink to index zero with empty
suffix/frame.
These finite tests supplement the general execution theorem.

The interface requires only a canonical index and explicit saved storage.
The
successor routine itself is independent of groups and cryptographic assumptions;
its specialization uses prime $q$ and the historical nonce map.
Immediate
potential callers are the two nonce draws in honest ballot construction.
The following section closes public $q\mapsto q-1$ preparation and sampling
composition.
Subsequent group/scalar operations and the complete adaptive
caller cost are outside this component's guarantee.

\subsection{Historical component: Complete historical nonzero-nonce sampling}\label{sec:complete-nonce-sampling}
The original sampler chooses uniformly in $\operatorname{Fin}(q-1)$ and maps
its index to the nonzero residue $i+1$.
The actual implementation now starts
with the public record $I(s,q)=1^s0E(q)$ and three saved words.
It parses $q$,
executes binary predecessor, collects width from $q-1$, samples and reduces
coins, then executes the preceding response continuation.

\paragraph{Preparation and saved storage.}
For $q\geq2$, \texttt{NonceOperands} returns modulus $(q-1).\mathrm{bits}$ and
$(\operatorname{size}(q-1)+s)$ width markers, with clock
\[
 P(s,q)=s+5\operatorname{size}(q)+2\operatorname{size}(q-1)+9
\]
and charge at most $32P(s,q)$.
Its general canonical-input theorem retains
suffix and saved words.
The original parser and predecessor supply their
execution proofs; a closed-region invariant transfers the unchanged width
code.
Only the shared layout and existing width theorem are exposed.
\companchor{NonceOperandsRun}{NonceOperands.run}
\companchor{NonceOperandsRun}{NonceOperands.local_cost}
\companchor{NonceOperandsRun}{NonceOperands.charged}
\companchor{SamplerOperands}{SamplerOperands.ready_run}
The sampler uses all eight local ports, so its three saved words occupy
external frame ports $8,9,10$.
The successor consumes the returned index
on port $5$ directly, retaining modulus port $1$ and the external saved words.
Input must be exhausted before sampling; both inter-region guards execute.
Saved words describe machine state, not additional public protocol observations.

Put $m=q-1$, $w=\operatorname{size}(m)+s$.
Let $U(w,m)$ and $D(w,m)$ be the
existing scalar sampler's clock and charge, and $N= B(m-1)$ the preceding
successor clock.
Define
\[
 T=P(s,q)+1+U(w,m)+1+N,\qquad
 C=32P(s,q)+5+D(w,m)+3+32N.
\]
For saved frame $S$, write $R(a)$ for the halted success configuration with
stack vector
\[
 [\epsilon,m.\mathrm{bits},\epsilon,\epsilon,\epsilon,
   \operatorname{scalarEncode}(a),\epsilon,\epsilon,S_0,S_1,S_2].
\]
\begin{theorem}[Complete executed nonce sampler]
For $q\geq2$, from $I(s,q)$ on input port $4$, blank work and saved frame $S$,
actual execution at clock $T$ has exactly the chronological $w$-coin tree.
For each coin word $b$, the output is the complete success configuration with
nonce prefix $E(\operatorname{bitsValue}(b)\bmod m+1)$, retained modulus and
saved frame. Its actual charge is at most $C$. All subroutine halt, canonical
index and scratch/frame premises are derived from the executed routines.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeNonceSampling}{PrimeNonceMachine.tail_source}
\companchor{PrimeNonceSamplingSource}{PrimeNonceMachine.sample_source}

\begin{theorem}[Historical source and sampling loss]
For prime $q$, projecting the complete result of actual execution gives exactly
\[
 \operatorname{liftCoins}\bigl(
 R_*\operatorname{runFairBitUniform}(s,\operatorname{samplePrimeNonzero})\bigr)
\]
as a raw oracle query tree. Interpreting its coins as fair yields an actual
machine experiment whose complete result law has total variation distance at
most $2^{-s}$ from $R_*\operatorname{samplePrimeNonzero}$. The reference on the
right uses the original exact-uniform nonzero sampler.
\end{theorem}

The complete run derives the existing primitive compiler contract.
From the

Physical execution at

 \operatorname{unitCost}(H+C)\,C\,G
\]
has the same complete source observation under the same bounded-handler
policy on both sides.

width or missing runtime certificate is assumed.

\subsection{Historical component: Executing the historical nonce-pair caller}\label{sec:nonce-pair-caller}
\paragraph{Interface and source.}
The actual \texttt{drawPrimeNoncePair} source binds two nonzero nonce samples
in order.
The finite controller now invokes the preceding sampler twice,
saving the first answer before reentry.
Its resident input has empty work
ports, a canonical public sampler record on port~8, an empty first-answer
slot~9 and arbitrary private context on port~10.
These saved words are machine
state, not additional public protocol observations.

The scoped transport copies record~8 to input~4.
Between calls it copies the
actual answer from~5 to~9, clears answer~5 and modulus~1, and reloads input~4
from the retained record.
It executes the existing copy algorithm under fixed
coordinate maps; no new arithmetic algorithm is used.
Both transport entries
preserve complete configurations and derive their own clocks and charges.
Their interface accepts arbitrary resident words; the pair theorem below
supplies canonical sampler inputs and derives the nonce-width bound.
\companchor{PrimeNoncePairTransportRun}{PrimeNoncePairTransport.entry_run}
\companchor{PrimeNoncePairTransportRun}{PrimeNoncePairTransport.between_run}

\paragraph{Combined execution.}
Write $s$ for slack, $q\ge2$, $m=q-1$, $w=\operatorname{size}(m)+s$,
$N=2\operatorname{size}(m)+1$, and $r$ for the canonical sampler record.
Let $R=|r|$, $M=\operatorname{size}(m)$, $E=2R+3$ and $F=9(R+1)+2$.
Let $S$ and $B$ be the preceding single-nonce clock and charge bound.
Transcribing the controller definitions and collecting terms gives
\[
 \begin{split}
 T&=2E+2S+3N+M+5,\\
 C&=2F+2B+13(N+1)+4(M+1)+3.
 \end{split}
\]
These are bounds derived from the executed instructions, including the
success guard after the first sampler.
The initial context contributes to
physical storage cost below.
For words $a,b$ and context $h$, the result is
\[
 \begin{split}
 \mathsf{PairOut}(a,b,h)=\bigl(\mathsf{halt},2,
 [&[],\operatorname{bits}(m),[],[],[],b,[],[],r,a,h]\bigr).
 \end{split}
\]

\begin{theorem}[Complete pair execution, machine-checked]
For every $s,q$ with $q\ge2$ and every context $h$, there is a charge function
$c(u,v)$ such that $|u|=|v|=w$ implies $c(u,v)\le C$, and the actual run from
the resident input at clock $T$ is the following complete raw query tree:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 u&\leftarrow\mathsf{word}(w);\quad v\leftarrow\mathsf{word}(w);\\
 &\mathsf{return}\bigl(\mathsf{PairOut}(
 E_n(\operatorname{value}(u)\bmod m+1),
 E_n(\operatorname{value}(v)\bmod m+1),h),c(u,v)\bigr).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here $E_n$ is the existing canonical natural-prefix encoding. The two word
queries are sequential binds. Full state preservation, termination and charge
are conclusions; no intermediate frame or cost certificate is a premise.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeNoncePairMachineRun}{PrimeNoncePairMachine.pair_run}
Only the first encoded nonce's length varies between calls; it is at most $N$.
The proof composes its actual transport and second sampler before applying
clock padding.
Thus padding occurs at the final halt, never at a live return.

\begin{theorem}[Joint historical source and physical execution, machine-checked]
If $q$ is prime, discarding the private charge from the complete run gives
exactly the lifted query tree of
\[
 (a,b)\leftarrow\mathsf{runFairBitUniform}_s(\mathsf{drawPrimeNoncePair});
 \quad\mathsf{return}\,\mathsf{PairOut}(E_q(a),E_q(b),h),
\]
where $E_q$ is the canonical scalar encoding. Under fair coins, the complete
result distribution has total-variation distance at most $2\cdot2^{-s}$ from
the encoded original exact-uniform pair. For actual resident height $H$ and
fixed primitive compiler factor $G$, physical execution at
$\operatorname{unitCost}(H+C)CG$ has this same source observation under the
same bounded-handler policy on both sides.
\end{theorem}

\companchor{PrimeNoncePairMachineSource}{PrimeNoncePairMachine.pair_experiment_source}
\companchor{PrimeNoncePairMachineSource}{PrimeNoncePairMachine.pair_loss}
\companchor{PrimeNoncePairMachineSource}{PrimeNoncePairMachine.pair_within}
\companchor{PrimeNoncePairMachineSource}{PrimeNoncePairMachine.pair_physical_run}

The
physical theorem derives the compiler contract from actual branchwise halt and

It keeps context height explicit and does not initialize an arbitrary
