\subsection{Historical component: Loaded public-coordinate multiplication}\label{sec:loaded-modular-multiply}
\paragraph{Required source operation.}
The next honest proof request computes \texttt{honestProofStatement}, which
uses \texttt{encryptWith}.
Its public coordinates require powers and
multiplication modulo $p$; $q$ is the scalar order.
The checked coordinate
equations specify these operations but supply no execution cost.
The loaded
multiplier below is the arithmetic prerequisite for square-and-multiply.
It reuses the existing guarded subtraction, remainder update, copying and
frame/return interfaces.
No full-product or separate carry-adder library is
introduced.

\paragraph{Reduced addition and its preparation.}
For $x,r<p$, write $c=p-x$.
The inner controller computes
\[
 \mathsf{add}_p(x,r)=
 \begin{cases}
 r-c,&c\le r,\\
 p-(c-r),&r<c.
 \end{cases}
 \qquad\mathsf{add}_p(x,r)=(r+x)\bmod p<p.
\]
It first executes guarded $r-c$.
Underflow preserves $r$ and selects two
further actual subtractions, $c-r$ and $p-(c-r)$, with copies that preserve
$c$ and $p$.
Every subtraction intermediate fits the modulus width.
The inner interface takes canonical $r,c,p$ digits; it returns the canonical
sum on port~0, retains $c$ on~1 and $p$ on~6, and clears every other local port.
Its executed clock is bounded by $26\operatorname{size}(p)+24$; its charge
is bounded by 32 times that clock.
Only the final halt is padded.
\companchor{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic}{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic.reducedAdd_eq_mod}
\companchor{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic}{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic.reducedAdd_lt}
\companchor{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic}{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic.operand_bounds}
\companchor{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic}{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic.underflow_bounds}
\companchor{BinaryModAddMachineRun}{BinaryModAddMachine.used_run}
\companchor{BinaryModAddMachineRun}{BinaryModAddMachine.run}
\companchor{BinaryModAddMachineRun}{BinaryModAddMachine.local_cost}
\companchor{BinaryModAddMachineRun}{BinaryModAddMachine.charged}

The outer multiplier does not receive a prepared complement.
It copies its
original modulus and executes $p-x$, reaching the complete loop-entry layout
within $8\operatorname{size}(p)+8$ ticks.
This discharges the inner interface's
preparation premise using actual instructions.
\companchor{BinaryModMultiplyRun}{BinaryModMultiply.prepare}

\paragraph{Multiplier interface and invariant.}
The input layout has modulus digits on~6, little-endian multiplier word $w$ on~8, multiplicand
digits on~10 and all other ports empty.
For each multiplier bit, read from
most significant to least after an executed reversal, the reduced accumulator
is doubled modulo $p$ and conditionally increased by $x$ using the inner
controller.
The arithmetic step
\[
 a\longmapsto ((2a\bmod p)+(\mathsf{if}\ b\ \mathsf{then}\ x\ \mathsf{else}\ 0))\bmod p
\]
preserves reduction.
Starting from zero, folding over $\operatorname{reverse}(w)$
gives $x\operatorname{value}(w)\bmod p$.
This includes empty words and high zero
padding.
These arithmetic identities are separate from the execution proof.
\companchor{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic}{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic.step_lt}
\companchor{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic}{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic.step_reducedAdd}
\companchor{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic}{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic.step_mul_bit}
\companchor{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic}{BinaryModMultiplyArithmetic.fold_reverse_zero}

\begin{theorem}[Executed loaded multiplication, machine-checked]
For every $p,x$ with $x<p$ and every loaded word $w$, the actual multiplier
halts within
\[
 T=9\operatorname{size}(p)+11+|w|(34\operatorname{size}(p)+38)
 \ \le\ 38(|w|+1)(\operatorname{size}(p)+1)
\]
transitions. Its full result has success memory~2 and stacks
\[
 [\operatorname{bits}(x\operatorname{value}(w)\bmod p),[],[],[],[],[],
   \operatorname{bits}(p),[],[],[],\operatorname{bits}(x)].
\]
The code executes complement preparation, reversal, both conditional loop
routes and final complement cleanup. It derives a total charge bound $B=32T$.
No intermediate frame, width invariant or cost certificate is a caller premise.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Loaded group coordinates and physical execution, machine-checked]
For $p\ne0$ and $a,b\in\mathsf{PrimeGroup}(p,q)$, the same actual multiplier,
loaded with their public coordinate digits, returns the coordinate digits of
$a+b$ in the historical additive group notation. Its underlying numeric
operation is
\[
 (\operatorname{coord}(a+b)).\mathsf{val}
 =\bigl((\operatorname{coord}(a)).\mathsf{val}
       (\operatorname{coord}(b)).\mathsf{val}\bigr)\bmod p.
\]
For any loaded input covered above, let $H$ be its actual resident height and
$G$ the fixed primitive compiler factor. Physical execution from the lowered ready state with empty auxiliary oracle
tapes, at
$\operatorname{unitCost}(H+B)BG$ returns the same complete result under the
bounded-handler policy. Its halt/charge contract is derived from execution.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Historical component: Loaded exponentiation for encryption}\label{sec:loaded-modular-power}
\paragraph{Required source operation.}
The honest ciphertext coordinates require $g^r$ and $pk^r$ modulo $p$.
The exponent uses the scalar's canonical value modulo $q$.
The loaded controller
calls the preceding multiplier; it scans exponent bits rather than iterating
once per exponent value.
Its original base, exponent and opaque caller data
are retained so the encryption continuation can compute both coordinates.

\paragraph{Interface and invariant.}
Assume $2\le p$, $x<p$, an arbitrary little-endian exponent word $w$, and saved
word $s$.
The initial layout has canonical $p$ on~6, canonical $x$ on~11,
$w$ on~13 and $s$ on~14; every other port is empty.
The controller copies
and reverses the exponent into work port~12 and initializes the accumulator
on~0 to one.
Each iteration first checks for a pending bit, executes a square
using the checked multiplier, then pops that bit and conditionally multiplies
by the retained base.
This ordering keeps the bit in the saved frame while
the multiplier uses its local memory.
The arithmetic invariant follows the exponent prefix $n$:
\[
 a=x^n\bmod p,
 \qquad
 a\longmapsto
 \begin{cases}
 (a^2\bmod p)x\bmod p,&b=1,\\
 a^2\bmod p,&b=0
 \end{cases}
 =x^{2n+b}\bmod p.
\]
Both branches remain reduced, so every multiplication operand has at most
$\operatorname{size}(p)$ digits.
Folding over the reversed word from one gives
$x^{\operatorname{value}(w)}\bmod p$, including empty and padded exponents.
The modulus premise justifies literal initialization to one; historical prime
$p$ implies it.
\companchor{BinaryModPowerArithmetic}{BinaryModPowerArithmetic.step_lt}
\companchor{BinaryModPowerArithmetic}{BinaryModPowerArithmetic.step_pow}
\companchor{BinaryModPowerArithmetic}{BinaryModPowerArithmetic.fold_reverse_one}

\begin{theorem}[Executed loaded power, machine-checked]
Under the preceding input conditions, the actual controller halts with success
memory~2 and complete stacks
\[
 [\operatorname{bits}(x^{\operatorname{value}(w)}\bmod p),[],[],[],[],[],
 \operatorname{bits}(p),[],[],[],[],\operatorname{bits}(x),[],w,s].
\]
Writing $W=\operatorname{size}(p)$, $L=|w|$ and
$M=9W+11+W(34W+38)$ for the existing multiplier bound, its executed clock is
bounded by
\[
 T=3L+5+L(2M+12W+16)\le80(L+1)(W+1)^2.
\]
Its total charge is at most $32T$. Original operand retention, clean call/return
states and the bounds of all copied intermediate words are derived from the
actual instructions. Only the final halted result is padded.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Historical scalar action and physical execution, machine-checked]
Let $p$ be prime, $q\ne0$, $r\in\mathbb Z_q$ and
$a\in\mathsf{PrimeGroup}(p,q)$. Loading the public coordinate digits of $a$
and canonical digits of $r.\mathsf{val}$ into the same controller returns
\[
 \operatorname{bits}\bigl((\operatorname{coord}(r\mathbin{\bullet}a)).\mathsf{val}\bigr)
 =\operatorname{bits}\bigl((\operatorname{coord}(a)).\mathsf{val}^{r.\mathsf{val}}
   \bmod p\bigr),
\]
with original operands, exponent and saved data retained as above.
For any loaded input covered by the run theorem, put $B=32T$ and let $H$ be
its actual resident height, including the saved data. From the lowered ready
state with empty auxiliary oracle tapes, the primitive execution at
$\operatorname{unitCost}(H+B)BG$, with fixed compiler factor $G$, has the same
complete result under the bounded-handler policy. The required halt/charge
contract is derived from the executed power program.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Historical component: Executing both ciphertext coordinates}\label{sec:loaded-prime-encrypt}
\paragraph{Actual source and resident interface.}
The next honest proof request uses the existing \code{encryptWith} construction.
The concrete controller invokes the preceding power routine twice, saves the
first coordinate, and conditionally multiplies the second by the generator for
a true vote.
For natural coordinates $g,k<p$, $p\ge2$, little-endian nonce
word $w$, vote $v$ and saved context $s$, the initial layout has canonical
digits of $p,k,g$ on ports~6,15,16, respectively, with nonce~13, context~14
and the one-bit vote~18; all other
ports are empty.
The mathematical outputs are
\[
 \alpha=g^{\operatorname{value}(w)}\bmod p,
 \qquad
 \beta=\bigl((k^{\operatorname{value}(w)}\bmod p)
           (\mathsf{if}\ v\ \mathsf{then}\ g\ \mathsf{else}\ 1)\bigr)\bmod p.
\]
This is a loaded input interface.
The actual parser and routing from the prior
nonce sampler are separate caller operations.
\companchor{PrimeEncryptMachine}{PrimeEncryptMachine.program}

\begin{theorem}[Executed loaded ciphertext, machine-checked]
Under the preceding conditions, the actual controller returns $\alpha$ on
port~17 and $\beta$ on~0, both in canonical digits. It retains every original
input port and clears work ports~1--5 and~7--12. Its full final configuration
has halt label and success memory~2. Put $W=\operatorname{size}(p)$, $L=|w|$,
let $P$ be the preceding power bound and $M=9W+11+W(34W+38)$. The actual
execution has clock at most
\[
 E=2P+M+15W+20\le220(L+1)(W+1)^2
\]
and charge at most $32E$. Both power inputs, storage of the first result,
public-key reload, vote branch, multiplier input, success guards and all
cleanup execute in the same fixed controller. Their frames, reduced operands
and width bounds are derived; no intermediate state or cost certificate is
a caller premise. Only the final halted state is padded.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Historical encryption and physical execution, machine-checked]
Let $p,q$ be prime, $g,pk\in\mathsf{PrimeGroup}(p,q)$, $r\in\mathbb Z_q$ and
$v$ be a vote. Loading their public coordinates and the canonical digits of
$r.\mathsf{val}$ into the same controller returns exactly both public
coordinates of
\[
 \mathsf{encryptWith}(g,pk,r,\mathsf{voteScalar}(v)),
\]
with the complete retained-input and cleaned-work configuration above.
For any loaded input satisfying the execution theorem, write $B=32E$ and
let $H$ be the actual resident height, including the saved context and nonce.
From the lowered ready state with empty auxiliary oracle tapes, primitive
execution at $\operatorname{unitCost}(H+B)BG$, with fixed compiler factor $G$,
has the same complete result under the bounded-handler policy. Its required
halt/charge contract is derived from this controller's run.
\end{theorem}

The next subsection closes source parsing and transfer from the actual
nonce-pair layout.
Proof construction and complete adaptive caller cost are outside this component's guarantee.
This loaded encryption theorem does not itself
establish ballot secrecy or full honest-ballot construction.

\paragraph{Independent controls.}
At $p=23,g=2,pk=4,r=3$, the expected ciphertexts are $(8,18)$ for false and
$(8,13)$ for true.
The 210-case native campaign checks both coordinates, all
nineteen ports and candidate cost bounds against independent integer modular
arithmetic.
Seven actual-code defects are detected before testing the correct
candidate: either missing power, using the generator in place of the key,
omitting the vote product, and corrupting the first coordinate, retained nonce
or saved context.
Zero cases are discarded or give up.
Separate kernel controls
retain both complete positive states and all seven complete mutant states.

\subsection{Historical component: From raw constructor input to the first ciphertext}\label{sec:raw-prime-ciphertext}
\paragraph{Source boundary and input.}
Fix primes $p,q$, $g,pk\in\mathsf{PrimeGroup}(p,q)$, sampling slack $s\in\mathbb N$,
vote $v$ and saved word $d$.
The parameters $g,pk,v$ are direct submission
source arguments; $s$ selects the fair-bit implementation and $d$ is an
uninterpreted private saved word.
Let $x$ be the existing five-field bit encoding of
\[
 \bigl[U(p),\ \mathsf{ciphertextCodec}(g,pk),\
       \mathsf{samplerInput}(s,q,[]),\ [v],\ d\bigr],
 \qquad N=|x|,
\]
where $U$ is the natural-prefix encoding and the ciphertext codec contains the
two group-coordinate encodings.
The initial controller state has $x$ on
port~7 and every other port empty.
An executed initializer copies $x$ into
private context~14, checks the fixed field arities and singleton vote, extracts
canonical $p,g,pk$ digits and the sampler record, and clears its workspace.
It derives its complete result and charge at most $32I$, where
$I=14N^2+54N+83$.
It does not validate primality or arbitrary group membership:
the theorem specializes the actual typed input.
Parsing a larger public-key
record would not establish key generation.
\companchor{PrimeHonestInputMachine}{PrimeHonestInputMachine.input}
\companchor{PrimeHonestInputMachineRun}{PrimeHonestInputMachine.run}
\companchor{PrimeHonestInputMachineRun}{PrimeHonestInputMachine.charged}

\begin{theorem}[Initialized first-ciphertext execution, machine-checked]
Under the preceding hypotheses, the fixed outer controller executes this
initializer, its success/reset guard, fair-bit implementations of both
historical nonce draws, parsing of
the first retained scalar prefix, and the preceding ciphertext construction.
Write $J,K$ for the nonce-pair clock and charge bounds, $R=7\operatorname{size}(q-1)+9$
for routing, and $E$ for the preceding encryption clock at nonce width
$\operatorname{size}(q-1)$. Then its source clock and charge bound are
\[
 F=I+1+J+R+1+E,\qquad B=32I+3+K+6R+3+32E.
\]
The charged execution is exactly two consecutive coin-word draws of width
$W=\operatorname{size}(q-1)+s$, followed by a pure complete result and a derived
branch charge at most $B$. For each word $a$, the scalar is
$\operatorname{value}(a)\bmod(q-1)+1$; equal draws are permitted.
After forgetting the charge, the entire result tree equals the existing
fair-bit implementation of \code{drawPrimeNoncePair}, mapped to the following
full halted-success state. For its pair $(r_0,r_1)$ and
$(\alpha,\beta)=\mathsf{encryptWith}(g,pk,r_0,\mathsf{voteScalar}(v))$:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
Ports & Final contents \\
\midrule
0,17 & Canonical public coordinates of $\beta,\alpha$ \\
6,15,16,18 & Original $p,pk,g$ digits and singleton vote \\
13,14 & Canonical digits of $r_0.\mathsf{val}$; original private input $x$ \\
19,20,21,22 & Sampler record; $U(r_0.\mathsf{val})$; $U(r_1.\mathsf{val})$; $(q-1)$ digits \\
All other ports & Empty \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}\end{center}
Every intermediate frame, success guard, operand width, halt and charge
premise follows from the actual component executions. Padding occurs only
after the final halt.
\end{theorem}

\companchor{PrimeNonceCiphertextMachineRun}{PrimeNonceCiphertextMachine.scalar_clock_le}

\begin{theorem}[Physical execution from raw input, machine-checked]
For every bounded-oracle adapter limit, the actual physical machine starts
with the ordinary raw input tape $x$ and blank workspace. The source
configuration reached by loading has stack height $N$. Let $G$ be the fixed compiler factor and
$T=\operatorname{unitCost}(N+B)BG$. There is a derived startup time
$u\le4N+8$ such that execution for $u+T$ returns, under the adapter, exactly
\code{some} of the complete source result above, with the same joint coin tree.
The required termination/charge contract follows from the charged execution;
neither a successful-loader premise nor a runtime certificate is supplied.
\end{theorem}

\companchor{PrimeHonestCiphertextMachineSource}{PrimeHonestCiphertextMachine.physical_run}
The complete configuration is a proof-side observation.
Keeping the vote and
original input privately does not add a protocol publication.

\paragraph{Independent controls and remaining boundary.}
The outer gate checks twelve independently encoded full-state cases, both
votes and nonce orders, the order-two singleton case, and three fixed seeds.
Five actual-link mutations are detected.
Bypassing the initializer failure
guard consumes eight coins on a malformed input before a later rejection;
the baseline rejects before any draw.
The component gates separately check
810 routing cases, 28 initializer cases and 60 combined caller cases.
Kernel
controls retain the full state and query/residual witnesses.
The general
charged theorem supplies aggregate cost for the caller campaign, whose
per-fixture kernel checks omit that cost.
A redundant interpreter campaign
was deliberately interrupted after 48 of those 60 positives; it is not a
completed campaign.
No successful gate establishes secrecy.
\companchor{PrimeHonestCiphertextControls}{PrimeHonestCiphertextControls.canonical_0}
\companchor{PrimeHonestCiphertextControls}{PrimeHonestCiphertextControls.canonical_3}

This result stops at the first ciphertext.
The actual reduction source lowers
honest proof requests through \code{ballotFiniteProgrammedImpl}: it next draws
four full-field simulator scalars and updates the programmed cache.
This is
distinct from executing the historical prover's three nonzero proof coins.
The next section executes those four full-field draws.
Commitment arithmetic,
programmed-state updates, subsequent ciphertexts, key generation, effective
attacker coverage and complete adaptive cost are outside this component's guarantee.

\subsection{Historical component: Four programmed-transcript scalar draws}\label{sec:four-transcript-draws}
The selected reduction uses \code{ballotFiniteProgrammedImpl} for honest proof
requests.
Its \code{ballotFullSimTranscript} samples
$c,e,z_0,z_1\in\mathbb Z_q$ in that order, computes \code{ballotSimCommit},
then applies the programmed-cache update keyed by the statement and
simulated commitment.
The following prefix executes
those four samples and their storage.
Zero is permitted in each full-field
sample.
The historical real prover's nonzero proof coins belong to a separate
source path; the existing simulation and sampling losses remain necessary.
\companchor{PrimeFullFieldSource}{PrimeFullFieldSource.drawTranscriptScalars}
\companchor{PrimeFullFieldSource}{PrimeFullFieldSource.ballotFullSimTranscript_factor}

\paragraph{Interface and reuse.}
The local sampler accepts a canonical resident record for $s,q$ and arbitrary
retained ciphertext, nonce, input-context and parameter words.
Its entry has
success memory and blank sampler workspace.
It reuses the existing full-field
cache sampler through a fixed port map.
Between samples,
an executed consuming transfer saves the current scalar, clears the old
modulus workspace and returns to the same sampler.
Its general interface
accepts any $q>0$; historical group and encryption assumptions enter through
the preceding caller.
The four simulator requests are its current callers;
another full-field request needs a checked adapter to this record and frame
layout.
No general protocol compiler is provided.
\companchor{PrimeFullFieldSource}{PrimeFullFieldSource.scalar_word_source}
\companchor{PrimeHonestTranscriptMachineRun}{PrimeHonestTranscriptMachine.charged}
\companchor{PrimeHonestTranscriptMachineSource}{PrimeHonestTranscriptMachine.execution_source}
\companchor{TranscriptScalarSaveRun}{TranscriptScalarSave.run}
\companchor{TranscriptScalarSaveRun}{TranscriptScalarSave.charged_bounded}

\begin{theorem}[Four-draw execution and source law, machine-checked]
Fix $q>0$, slack $s$, and the retained words in the preceding interface.
Let $A,D$ be the existing guarded single-sample clock and charge bounds and set
\[
 L=2\operatorname{size}(q-1)+1,\quad
 S=3L+\operatorname{size}(q)+7,\quad
 F_4=4A+3S,\quad B_4=4D+15S.
\]
The actual controller at clock $F_4$ executes four successive coin-word draws,
each of width $\operatorname{size}(q)+s$, followed by a complete halted-success
state and a derived charge at most $B_4$ on every branch. Each word $w$ becomes
$U(\operatorname{value}(w)\bmod q)$, whose length is at most $L$.
The three save destinations are proved empty when entered, and the modulus
workspace is cleared before every repeated sample. The full final state
retains the prior ciphertext, both nonce prefixes and input context, with
\[
  (\mathsf{port}_{10},\mathsf{port}_{11},\mathsf{port}_{12},\mathsf{port}_{7})
       =(U(c.\mathsf{val}),U(e.\mathsf{val}),U(z_0.\mathsf{val}),U(z_1.\mathsf{val})),
 \qquad \mathsf{port}_2=\operatorname{bits}(q).
\]
After forgetting charges, the entire query tree equals the existing fair-bit
implementation of \code{drawTranscriptScalars}, mapped to this full state.
The corresponding bounded-adapter termination/charge contract is derived.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeTranscriptDrawsRun}{PrimeTranscriptDraws.charged}
\companchor{PrimeTranscriptDrawsSource}{PrimeTranscriptDraws.execution_source}
\companchor{PrimeTranscriptDrawsSource}{PrimeTranscriptDraws.within}

\paragraph{Controls and evidence limits.}
Independent full-state validation checks eleven local-sample cases and eight
four-draw cases, detecting eight and four actual instruction mutations
respectively.
It checks fresh-query counts, remaining coins and independently
computed clock/charge caps.
Omitting modulus clearing consumes too many coins
and remains live at the mutant's finite cap; this is a bounded counterexample,
not a proof of eventual nontermination.
The earlier local full-trace kernel
campaign exhausted interpreter memory and remains unpassed.
The successful
interpreter campaign uses checked state-snapshot and program-table identities
with the original instructions.
Separate kernel controls derive literal full
positive states from the general run theorem and retain paired instruction
boundary counterexamples.
These evidence scopes are distinct.
\companchor{PrimeTranscriptDrawsControls}{PrimeTranscriptDrawsControls.four_zero_scalars_full_state}


\begin{theorem}[Joint raw-input source and physical execution, machine-checked]
Under the prime-group, input and slack hypotheses of
Section~\ref{sec:raw-prime-ciphertext}, the linked controller starts from the
same raw word $x$ and blank work. It executes the first-ciphertext prefix and
then all four simulator draws. If $F,B$ are the preceding prefix bounds,
its source clock is $F+F_4$ and every actual branch charge is at most $B+B_4$.
After forgetting charges, its complete query tree is
\[
 \mathsf{liftCoins}\left(\mathsf{result}\mathbin{\langle\$\rangle}
  \mathsf{runFairBitUniform}_{s}
   \left(\begin{array}{l}
     (r_0,r_1)\leftarrow\mathsf{drawPrimeNoncePair};\\
     (c,e,z_0,z_1)\leftarrow\mathsf{drawTranscriptScalars};\\
     \mathsf{return}\bigl((r_0,r_1),(c,e,z_0,z_1)\bigr)
   \end{array}\right)\right).
\]
Here $\mathsf{result}$ preserves the preceding full ciphertext/nonce/input
state and stores the four scalars as above. All extraction, canonical-record,
return and halt conditions follow from the reached first-ciphertext state.
For every bounded-adapter limit, let
\[
 T'=\operatorname{unitCost}(N+B+B_4)(B+B_4)G',
\]
where $G'$ is this controller's fixed compiler factor. There is a derived
startup time $u\le4N+8$ such that physical execution for $u+T'$ returns
\code{some} of that same complete source result under the adapter. The loaded
source height is $N$; no prepared caller state or cost certificate is assumed.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Historical component: First simulated commitment from raw input}\label{sec:first-sim-commit}
Let $g,\alpha$ denote the public residue coordinates of the generator and
first ciphertext component.
Write $e,z_0$ for the canonical natural
representatives of the reached scalars in $\mathbb Z_q$, and define
\[
 A(p,q,g,\alpha,e,z_0)
 =\bigl((g^{z_0}\bmod p)(\alpha^{q-e}\bmod p)\bigr)\bmod p.
\]
For a typed statement over $\mathsf{PrimeGroup}(p,q)$, $p\ne0$ and prime $q$,
this is exactly the value of the first coordinate of
$\mathsf{ballotSimCommit}(\mathit{stmt},c,(e,z_0,z_1))$.
Subgroup membership supplies $\alpha^q\equiv1\pmod p$: at $e=0$, the executed exponent
is $q$, whereas canonical scalar negation has value zero.
This choice avoids
an extra zero branch and uses the existing raw-exponent power routine.
It requires neither a new field inverse nor an exact-order assumption.
The historical raw caller below retains prime $p$ and $q$.
\companchor{PrimeSimCommitSource}{PrimeSimCommitSource.first_coordinate_value}
\companchor{PrimeSimCommitSource}{PrimeSimCommitSource.complement_action}
\companchor{PrimeSimCommitSource}{PrimeSimCommitSource.complement_width}

\begin{theorem}[Complete first-coordinate execution, machine-checked]
Assume $p\ge2$, $g,\alpha<p$ and $e,z_0<q$. Let $w$ be the full reached
operand presentation \code{inputWords}, with an arbitrary retained eleven-word
frame. The controller appends fifteen blank private work ports to the original
23 ports. Write $\ell(n)$ for bit length, $F_{\rm pow},F_{\rm mul}$ for the
existing power/product clocks and
\[
 \begin{split}
 F_{\rm comp}(q)&=10\ell(q-1)+5\ell(q)+17,\\
 F_A(p,q)&=F_{\rm comp}(q)+2F_{\rm pow}(p,\ell(q))
      +F_{\rm mul}(p,\ell(p))\\
   &\qquad+22\ell(p)+13\ell(q)+54,\qquad B_A=32F_A.
 \end{split}
\]
There is $u\le F_A$ such that the actual run from $w$ halts successfully
with $\mathsf{bits}(A)$ on port~3. Every other original port is preserved and
all fifteen appended ports are empty. Padded execution for $F_A$ has this same
result and an actual charge at most $B_A$; it makes no oracle queries.
The run executes both scalar-prefix reads, $q-e$ subtraction, two modular
powers, their product, all operand transfers and final cleanup.
\end{theorem}

\companchor{PrimeSimCommitMachineSource}{PrimeSimCommitMachine.charged_source}

\begin{theorem}[Raw source and physical execution, machine-checked]
Under the prime-group and raw-input hypotheses of
Section~\ref{sec:four-transcript-draws}, let $x$ be that original input,
$N=|x|$, and $F,B$ the complete four-draw prefix bounds. The combined controller
executes for source clock $F+F_A$, with actual charge at most $B'=B+B_A$.
Its full query tree, after forgetting charges, is the same fair-bit
$\mathsf{drawSource}$ tree of Section~\ref{sec:four-transcript-draws}, mapped
to the complete preceding state with the actual first commitment coordinate
on port~3 and blank appended workspace. All operand, frame and halt conditions
are derived from supported executions of that original prefix.

For every bounded-adapter limit, let
\[
 T=\operatorname{unitCost}(N+B')B'G_A,
\]
where $G_A$ is the combined controller's own compiler factor. There is a
startup time $s\le4N+8$ such that physical execution for $s+T$, from $x$ and
blank work, observes \code{some} of that same complete source result under
that same bounded adapter.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Historical component: Zero-branch commitment pair from raw input}\label{sec:sim-commit-pair}
The second zero-branch coordinate uses the actual public key and second
ciphertext component, rather than constructing a different statement by
exchanging the generator and public key.
For typed subgroup operands, prime
$q$ and $p\ne0$, let $e,z_0\in\mathbb Z_q$ be typed scalars, with canonical natural
representatives $\bar e,\bar z_0$.
Then
\[
 \begin{split}
 (\mathsf{ballotSimCommit}(\mathit{stmt},c,(e,z_0,z_1)))_{0,2}
    &=z_0\cdot pk-e\cdot\beta,\\
 B(p,q,pk,\beta,\bar e,\bar z_0)
    &=\bigl((pk^{\bar z_0}\bmod p)(\beta^{q-\bar e}\bmod p)\bigr)\bmod p
 \end{split}
\]
is its public residue value.
The first line uses the group's additive notation;
the second uses natural residue coordinates.
The zero-message offset vanishes
in this branch.
As before, subgroup membership justifies exponent $q$ when
$e=0$ without identifying it with canonical scalar negation.
\companchor{PrimeSimCommitSecondSource}{PrimeSimCommitSecondSource.second_group}
\companchor{PrimeSimCommitSecondSource}{PrimeSimCommitSecondSource.second_coordinate_value}

The existing numeric controller is reused unchanged through a fixed port
relocation.
Local ports 0/17 and 15/16 exchange roles to supply $\beta$ and $pk$;
local scratch/output 3 goes to new port 38.
Original port 3, which holds the first
coordinate, is retained in a singleton frame.
Every other original port keeps
its role.
The verified frame library preserves the exact execution tree and
charge under this relocation, avoiding a new copying or arithmetic routine.
\companchor{PrimeSimCommitSecondMachine}{PrimeSimCommitSecondMachine.code_frame}

\companchor{PrimeSimCommitSecondOrigins}{PrimeSimCommitSecondMachine.source_outputs}

\begin{theorem}[Actual zero-branch pair and execution cost, machine-checked]
Assume prime $p,q$, typed $g,pk$, a Boolean vote, explicit slack and a saved
private word. Let $w$ be the actual complete first-coordinate source result of
Section~\ref{sec:first-sim-commit}, for any nonce/transcript source tuple.
The relocated controller starts from its 38 words and one new blank word.
For the same $F_A,B_A$ as that section, its actual charged run for $F_A$
is a pure successful result with charge at most $B_A$. It preserves every
word of $w$, including the first coordinate on port 3, and places the actual
second coordinate $B$ on port 38. All operand, reduced-value and scratch
conditions are derived from $w$.

From the original raw input $x$ and blank work, the combined caller executes
both zero-branch coordinates. Let $F_{\rm pre},B_{\rm pre}$ be the complete raw four-draw prefix
bounds and $N=|x|$. The source clock and actual charge bound are
\[
 F_{\rm pair}=F_{\rm pre}+2F_A,\qquad
 B_{\rm pair}=B_{\rm pre}+2B_A.
\]
After forgetting charges, its complete query tree is the unchanged fair-bit
$\mathsf{drawSource}$ tree, mapped to the full result with both coordinates
on ports 3/38 and all work ports 23--37 empty. For every bounded-adapter limit, physical
execution with the same adapter on both sides returns \code{some} of this
source result after a derived startup $u\le4N+8$ and
\[
 T=\operatorname{unitCost}(N+B_{\rm pair})B_{\rm pair}G_{\rm pair}
\]
steps, where $G_{\rm pair}$ is the combined controller's own compiler factor.
The theorem assumes no supplied execution, source-correspondence or cost
certificate.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Historical component: Executed canonical challenge difference}\label{sec:sim-difference}
The one-message branch uses $d=c-e$ in $\mathbb Z_q$.
Its canonical natural
representative is $(\bar c+q-\bar e)\bmod q$; truncated natural subtraction
would give the wrong result when $\bar c<\bar e$.
The existing modular-addition
controller already executes this difference from the direct digit words
$\bar c$, $\bar e$ and $q$.
For $e\ne0$, its earlier theorem applies with
$x=q-\bar e$.
At $e=0$, the claim $q-\bar e<q$ is false.
A separate proof of
the same program's subtraction-success and restoration path closes that
endpoint without changing an instruction or asserting the false premise.
\companchor{BinaryModAddMachineRun}{BinaryModAddMachine.difference_run}
\companchor{BinaryModAddMachineRun}{BinaryModAddMachine.difference_charged_source}

\begin{theorem}[Encoded scalar difference, machine-checked]
For $q\ne0$ and $c,e\in\mathbb Z_q$, start with the canonical words
$q.\mathsf{bits},U(\bar c),U(\bar e)$ and blank output/work ports. A fixed
controller copies and parses both prefixes, executes the existing difference
routine, clears the retained temporary $e$ digits and writes $U(\overline{c-e})$
with the existing prefix writer. It retains all three original inputs and
clears every temporary port. Its exact charged run is a pure successful result
with actual charge at most $B_D=32F_D$, where
\[
 F_D(q)=18\operatorname{size}(q-1)+26\operatorname{size}(q)+47.
\]
No parsing, reduced-operand, source or runtime certificate is a premise.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{ScalarDifferenceMachineRun}{ScalarDifferenceMachine.run}
\companchor{ScalarDifferenceMachineRun}{ScalarDifferenceMachine.charged_source}

Assume prime $p,q$, typed $g,pk:\mathsf{PrimeGroup}(p,q)$, a Boolean vote, explicit slack and a saved
private word. From the actual zero-branch pair result of
Section~\ref{sec:sim-commit-pair}, a fixed relocation places $U(\overline{c-e})$
in previously empty port1. Both commitment coordinates and every other
original word are retained; all temporary work is restored empty. The source
itself derives both input prefixes, their canonical bounds and every frame
condition.

From the original raw input $x$, let $N=|x|$ and let $F_{\rm pair},B_{\rm pair}$
be the complete prefix bounds. The combined caller has clock
$F=F_{\rm pair}+F_D$ and derived actual charge at most $B=B_{\rm pair}+B_D$.
After forgetting charges, the complete query tree is the unchanged fair-bit
nonce/transcript source, mapped to this full result. For each bounded-adapter
limit, the same adapter on both sides gives physical equality to \code{some}
of the source result, with a derived startup $u\le4N+8$ and
\[
 T=\operatorname{unitCost}(N+B)B G_D
\]
execution steps, using the combined caller's own compiler factor $G_D$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Historical component: Executed adjusted ciphertext operand}\label{sec:adjusted-beta}
The remaining second commitment uses $\gamma=\beta-g$.
For typed subgroup
elements, prime $q$ and $p\ne0$, its canonical public residue is
\[
 \overline\gamma=
   \bigl(\overline\beta(\overline g^{\,q-1}\bmod p)\bigr)\bmod p.
\]
Subgroup membership justifies this inverse representative; exact order is not
an additional premise of this identity.
The actual one-branch commitments are
$z_1\cdot g-d\cdot\alpha$ and $z_1\cdot pk-d\cdot\gamma$.
\companchor{PrimeSimCommitOneSource}{PrimeSimCommitOneSource.adjusted_beta_value}

\begin{theorem}[Adjusted-base execution, machine-checked]
For numeric $p\ge2$ and reduced $g,\beta<p$, start from their canonical words,
the retained $(q-1)$ digits, blank work and an arbitrary retained frame.
One fixed relocation of the existing power routine computes $g^{q-1}\bmod p$;
a second relocation lets the existing multiplier consume that inverse with
$\beta$. Two actual success/reset guards connect the routines. The complete
charged run preserves all original words, clears all temporary work and writes
$(\beta(g^{q-1}\bmod p))\bmod p$ on a new output. Its clock and charge bound are
\[
 \begin{split}
 F_\gamma(p,q)&=F_{\rm pow}(p,\operatorname{size}(q-1))
                +F_{\rm mul}(p,\operatorname{size}(p))+2,\\
 B_\gamma(p,q)&=32F_\gamma(p,q).
 \end{split}
\]
The numeric theorem needs no primality or subgroup assumption.

For prime $p,q$ and $g,pk:\mathsf{PrimeGroup}(p,q)$, for any Boolean vote, slack, saved private word and nonce/transcript tuple,
the actual reached challenge-difference result supplies every numeric operand, the $(q-1)$ word
and blank work. The same run therefore produces the actual $\beta-g$, retaining
$d$'s prefix, both preceding commitment coordinates and all original state.
The enclosing source/frame conditions are derived from that reached result.
\end{theorem}

 \end{pmatrix}.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Complete raw commitment construction, machine-checked]
Assume prime $p,q$, $g,pk:\mathsf{PrimeGroup}(p,q)$, a Boolean vote,
slack and a saved private word. From the original canonical raw input and blank
work, one finite controller executes both nonce draws, the first ciphertext,
all four simulator scalar draws, canonical $d=c-e$, adjusted $\gamma=\beta-g$
and all four actual $\mathsf{ballotSimCommit}$ coordinates. Its complete source
query tree, after forgetting charges, is the unchanged fair-bit nonce/transcript
tree mapped to the full final state. The four output words at ports 3/38/40/42
are exactly the canonical coordinate digits of the original simulator result.
The intermediate $d$ prefix and $\gamma$ value remain on 1/39; temporary work
returns empty, including scratch 41. Original inputs and saved state are retained.

Let $F_{\rm diff},B_{\rm diff}$ be the complete raw challenge-difference
bounds from Section~\ref{sec:sim-difference}. The full clock and derived
charge bound are
\[
 F_{\rm all}=F_{\rm diff}+F_\gamma+2F_A,\qquad
 B_{\rm all}=B_{\rm diff}+B_\gamma+2B_A.
\]
Each return derives the complete next state from the actual preceding source
result. For every bounded-adapter limit, physical execution with the same
adapter on both sides gives \code{some} of this full source result after
startup $u\le4N+8$ and
\[
 T=\operatorname{unitCost}(N+B_{\rm all})B_{\rm all}G_{\rm all}
\]
steps, where $N$ is the original input length and $G_{\rm all}$ is this
combined controller's own compiler factor. Source correspondence, presentation,
width and runtime certificates are derived rather than supplied as premises.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Historical component: Executed simulator hash-key serialization}\label{sec:sim-key}
The original hash key contains the complete statement and both commitment
pairs.
Its group-coordinate order is
\[
 (g,pk,\alpha,\beta,A,B,C,D).
\]
The preceding raw constructor retains their canonical residue digits on
ports $16,15,17,0,3,38,40,42$.
These words are not yet the key codec.
Writing $U(n)$ for the existing canonical natural prefix, one encoded field is
\[
 E(n,w)=U(|U(n)|)\mathbin{+\!+}U(n)\mathbin{+\!+}w.
\]
The complete flat record is $U(8)$ followed by the eight framed coordinate
prefixes.
In particular, coordinates use the field modulus $p$, not the scalar
modulus $q$.
Actual-source presentation proves their origins and width bounds;
no new group operation is required.
\companchor{PrimeSimKeySource}{PrimeSimKeySource.source_digits}
\companchor{PrimeSimKeySource}{PrimeSimKeySource.encoding}
\companchor{PrimeSimKeySource}{PrimeSimKeySource.source_width}
\companchor{PrimeSimKeySource}{PrimeSimKeySource.source_work}

\begin{theorem}[One preserved coordinate field, machine-checked]
For every natural $n$ and arbitrary suffix $w$, a fixed controller starts with
$n$'s canonical digits, $w$ and blank work. It preserves the original digits,
returns $E(n,w)$ and clears all five work ports. It invokes the existing copy,
prefix and field writers. With $s=\operatorname{size}(n)$, its clock and actual
charge bound are
\[
 F_E(n)=5s+8+\mathsf{FrameWriteMachine.cost}(2s+1),\qquad B_E(n)=32F_E(n).
\]
The complete charged run equals a pure successful result with derived charge
at most $B_E(n)$. The clock is monotone in $n$; neither a precomputed prefix nor
an encoding-execution certificate is supplied.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Full key from the actual commitment state, machine-checked]
Assume prime $p,q$, typed $g,pk:\mathsf{PrimeGroup}(p,q)$, a Boolean vote,
slack, a saved private word and the original nonce/transcript tuple. Starting
from the actual complete commitment result and one new blank output port,
eight fixed writer invocations and a fixed count-prefix step produce exactly
\code{ballotKeyBitCodec}'s encoding of the original statement and simulated
commitment. All 43 preceding words are retained; reused work ports 23--27 are
empty again. The new key occupies port 43.

The controller writes fields in reverse order because each invocation prepends
to its existing suffix. Typed residue bounds imply $n_i\le p-1$ for every
coordinate, giving the complete clock and derived charge bound
\[
 F_K(p)=8F_E(p-1)+1,\qquad B_K(p)=32F_K(p).
\]
Every source, workspace and reduced-operand premise of the numeric execution
lemma is discharged from the actual preceding result.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Original raw-input key execution, machine-checked]
Under the same prime-group, vote, slack and saved-word assumptions, let
$F_{\rm all},B_{\rm all}$ denote the complete preceding raw commitment bounds.
The combined caller derives
\[
 F_{\rm key}=F_{\rm all}+F_K(p),\qquad
 B_{\rm key}=B_{\rm all}+B_K(p).
\]
After forgetting charges, its complete query tree is the unchanged fair-bit
nonce/transcript source mapped to the full result with the exact key on
port 43. Each of the original 43 words is preserved.
For every bounded-adapter limit, physical execution from the original raw
input and blank work, using the same adapter on both sides, returns
\code{some} of this source result after derived startup $u\le4N+8$ and
\[
 T=\operatorname{unitCost}(N+B_{\rm key})B_{\rm key}G_{\rm key}
\]
steps. Here $N$ is the original raw-input length and $G_{\rm key}$ is the
combined controller's own compiler factor. Source correspondence and costs
are proved from the executed code.
\end{theorem}

The single-field gate covers 33 independently generated cases, including zero,
nonempty suffixes and width boundaries, plus five actual instruction mutations.
The key gate covers five source-consistent states and three additional seeded
canonical-digit states, seven actual mutations and a rejecting entry guard.
Every comparison includes full retained state, queries, residual tape and the
original clock/charge bounds.
Separate kernel controls pin source coordinates
and literal bytes, including an eight-distinct-coordinate example.
These are
bounded campaigns; the final raw composition uses checked finite-code and
complete return-state identities, with no additional full raw-native campaign.

This closes the first simulated ballot's flat hash-key construction.
Its
programmed-statement history has a different nested-pair codec.
Executing that
history update, sticky collision status, shadow/live cache handling, later
ballots, effective attacker continuations and complete adaptive cost are outside this component's guarantee.
Private configurations remain proof-side observations and fair-bit
sampling loss is unchanged.

\subsection{Historical component: Recovering the original programmed state}\label{sec:programmed-state-input}
The next constructor boundary needs the shadow cache $S$, separate live cache
$L$, sticky collision flag $b$ and ordered statement history $H$.
The original
saved-field codec is
\[
 F\bigl[F[S,F[[b],H]],L\bigr],
 \qquad F[\cdots]=\mathsf{bitFieldsEncode}[\cdots].
\]
Here $S,L,H$ denote their existing encoded words.
Repetitions and history order
are retained.
An arbitrary saved word does not establish this presentation.

\begin{theorem}[Exact first programming request]
For prime $p,q$, typed group elements $g,pk$, a vote, any original submission
attacker continuation, and arbitrary finite initial shadow state $s$ and live
cache $L$, the complete source factors exactly as follows: draw the original
nonzero nonce pair $r$, draw the four original simulator scalars $u$, apply
$s.\mathsf{program}$ to the actual first statement and transcript, then run the
original remaining continuation from that result and unchanged $L$. The two
remaining Alice proofs, verification, Bob, attacker submission and final
verification remain in the continuation. Empty initialization is a specialization.
\end{theorem}

\companchor{PrimeFirstProgramSource}{PrimeFirstProgramSource.source_eq}
\companchor{PrimeFirstProgramSource}{PrimeFirstProgramSource.submission_run}
\companchor{PrimeFirstProgramSource}{PrimeFirstProgramSource.submission_empty}
This is exact semantic source equality.
It does not execute saved-state
serialization or the subsequent programming transition.

\begin{theorem}[Complete state extraction from the retained input]
Let the first four raw fields be arbitrary words and let the fifth have the
nested form above, with singleton Boolean flag $[b]$. Suppose port 14 contains
this raw record of length $N$ and work ports 23--27 are empty. Execution starts
from $\mathsf{start}(\mathit{old})$: the fixed entry label, success memory 2,
and four appended blank output words. The extractor returns $S,L,[b],H$ on ports 44--47, preserves all 44 previous words,
and restores the work ports. It uses at most
\[
 T(N)=11\bigl(3\operatorname{size}(N)+7+
 N(2\operatorname{size}(N)+3)\bigr)+6N+41
\]
local steps and charge at most $32T(N)$. Each field length is bounded by the
actual raw record; no independent field-size or cost certificate is assumed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{example}[Occupied insertion does not clear its work]
On an occupied canonical key, the unchanged insertion routine retains an unread
tail, a positive binary counter and the old encoded answer. It preserves the
original cache and proposed answer and returns the collision result. The
derived occupied endpoint has a nonempty counter. Thus a surrounding caller must capture
the collision flag and clear insertion work ports 0, 4 and 5 before reuse.
At the source level, even an agreeing old and proposed challenge sets the
sticky flag. Section~\ref{sec:programmed-transition} executes that update and cleanup.
\end{example}

\subsection{Historical component: Executing the first programmed-state update}\label{sec:programmed-transition}
Let $s=(S,b,H)$ be the original finite shadow state, $L$ its separate live cache,
$x$ the actual first simulator statement, $k=(x,t)$ its full hash key and $c$
the sampled challenge; $t$ denotes the four-coordinate commitment.
The original
source operation has successor
\[
 S'=\begin{cases}S[k\mapsto c]&S(k)=\mathsf{none},\\ S&S(k)=\mathsf{some}(v),\end{cases}
 \qquad b'=b\lor\bigl(S(k)\ne\mathsf{none}\bigr),\qquad H'=x::H.
\]
Any occupied key sets the flag, including an old answer equal to $c$.
History prepends the statement and preserves repeated entries.
\companchor{PrimeProgrammedInsertSource}{PrimeProgrammedInsertSource.program_fresh}
\companchor{PrimeProgrammedInsertSource}{PrimeProgrammedInsertSource.program_occupied}

\begin{theorem}[Executed cache update and collision cleanup]
For nonzero moduli $p,q$, a typed finite cache, typed key and challenge, arbitrary
live/history words and Boolean flag, initialize the local insertion wrapper
with success memory, the canonical cache/challenge/key words and empty work.
Its complete execution returns the encoded $S'$, singleton $[b']$, unchanged
challenge, key, live word and history word, with every insertion work word empty.
Let $I$ bound the existing insertion cost on the actual cache and $D$ bound
$1+|S|+|c|+|k|+|L|+|H|$, where bars denote lengths of the existing encoded bit
words. The wrapper halts within
\[
 T_{\rm ins}(I,D)=I+3(D+5I)+5,
 \qquad \text{charge}\le32T_{\rm ins}(I,D).
\]
The factor 5 is the maximum stack access count of the unchanged insertion
code, kernel-checked by the durable insertion gate driver. Occupied residue lengths follow from that code's actual execution growth.
The source specialization derives $I,D$ and every initialization premise.
\end{theorem}

The surrounding preparation copies shadow port 44 to work port 23, clears 44,
and enters the existing local insertion layout.
The wrapper captures the return
status before clearing residue.
Its full result changes only ports 44 and 46;
live 45, ordered history 47, challenge 10, key 43 and the remaining frame persist.
For original raw length $N$, the actual source supplies
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 I&=\mathsf{CacheInsertMachine.cost}(p,q,N,N),\\
 D&=1+3N+\mathsf{keyRecordBitBound}(p)+\mathsf{groupRecordBitBound}(q).
 \end{aligned}
\]
These bounds use cache entry count at most encoded cache length at most $N$.
They are uniform over the original sampled tuple.

\begin{theorem}[Nested statement encoding and ordered prepend]
Suppose ports 0, 17, 15, 16 respectively contain canonical binary digits of
$\beta,\alpha,pk,g<p$, ports 23--29 are empty and history 47 is the canonical
counted list $F[H_0,\ldots,H_{n-1}]$. From its entry with success memory, the history
routine updates only port 47 to
\[
 F\bigl[F[F[U(g),U(pk)],F[U(\alpha),U(\beta)]],H_0,\ldots,H_{n-1}\bigr].
\]
It preserves all other words and clears its work. Its clock is the sum of four
existing natural-field writers, two pair-field writers, one statement-field
writer, the derived history count parsing/increment/prefix budget, and nine
fixed control steps. Charge
is at most 32 times that clock. For an enclosing length bound $N$, its padded
clock requires the actual old history length to be at most $N$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Original raw execution through the resident update]
For prime $p,q$, typed $g,pk$, vote $v$, arbitrary finite initial $s,L$ and sampler
slack, start with only the canonical original raw input and blank work.
The composed caller executes the original nonce/transcript draw tree and reaches
$\mathsf{sourceResult}(s,L,\mathit{out})$, whose ports 44, 46, 47 encode
$S',b',H'$ above and whose port 45 still encodes $L$. It preserves all other
resident words from the preceding state extractor. The full query-tree equality
uses the same fair-bit sampler and introduces no additional queries. Its charge
bound adds the extractor caller, preparation, insertion and history bounds.
The physical execution theorem derives startup at most $4N+8$ and uses the
composed program's own compiler factor; the same bounded adapter occurs on both
sides of that equality.
\end{theorem}

\paragraph{Component boundary.}
Port 14 still contains the \emph{original} raw input with the old saved state.
Re-extracting it after this update would restore stale data.
The next subsection executes
repacking and returned-proof encoding with enlarged storage bounds.
Remaining
proof requests, ballot assembly, verification and effective attacker
continuations still need their execution proofs.
Preserve the enclosing
auxiliary-domain cache.

\subsection{Historical component: Encoding the returned proof and saved state}\label{sec:program-output}
The first proof request returns a proof as well as the updated state.
For commitments $(A,B),(C,D)$ and the original sampled scalars $c,e,z_0,z_1$,
its two branches are
\[
 P_0=(A,B,e,z_0),\qquad P_1=(C,D,c-e,z_1),
\]
where subtraction is in $\mathbb Z_q$.
The prior execution already computed
the canonical prefix for $c-e$.
Encoding uses that resident value; it does not
replace modular subtraction by natural truncated subtraction or by $c$.
\companchor{PrimeProgramProofSource}{PrimeProgramProofSource.program_return}
\companchor{PrimeProgramProofSource}{PrimeProgramProofSource.scalar_values}
\companchor{PrimeProgramProofSource}{PrimeProgramProofSource.encoding_resident}

The original proof codec has seven nested pairs:
\[
 F\bigl[F[F[U(A),U(B)],F[U(e),U(z_0)]],
        F[F[U(C),U(D)],F[U(c-e),U(z_1)]]\bigr].
\]
The updated saved state has three:
\[
 F\bigl[F[S',F[[b'],H']],L\bigr].
\]
Here group coordinates use modulus $p$, scalars use modulus $q$, and $S',H',L$
stand for their existing encoded words.
The two cache roles remain distinct.
The source interfaces derive all resident operands and blank work from the
preceding executed programming result.

\begin{theorem}[Preserving pair construction]
For arbitrary words $a,b$, initialize the pair writer at its entry with success
memory, inputs $a,b$, blank output and four blank work words. It returns
$F[a,b]$, retains both inputs, restores all work and halts with success memory.
For $|a|\le L$ and $|b|\le R$, it executes within
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 T_{\rm pair}(L,R)&=2(L+R)+\mathsf{FrameWrite.cost}(L)\\
   &\quad+\mathsf{FrameWrite.cost}(R)+7,\\
 \text{charge}&\le32T_{\rm pair}(L,R).
 \end{aligned}
\]
These length premises concern the actual words; the typed callers derive them.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{BitPairWriterMachineRun}{BitPairWriterMachine.run}
\companchor{BitPairWriterMachineRun}{BitPairWriterMachine.charged_bounded}
The writer copies the right input into temporary storage, writes its field,
then does the same for the left input and writes the fixed two-field count.
It reuses the existing copy and field routines.
Arbitrary-word construction
is not a claim that every constructed record decodes as a cryptographic object.

\begin{theorem}[Updated storage bounds and stale-record exclusion]
For prime $p,q$ and any typed initial shadow state and live cache, let $N$ be
the original raw input length. After the first programming request, the shadow
cache and ordered history each have at most $N+1$ entries; the live cache has
at most $N$ entries. Existing codec bounds therefore bound their enlarged
encoded records. The new saved-state encoding differs from the old one on
\emph{every} call: even an occupied key prepends a history entry.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeProgramRepackSource}{PrimeProgramRepackSource.source_counts}
\companchor{PrimeProgramRepackSource}{PrimeProgramRepackSource.source_lengths}
\companchor{PrimeProgramRepackSource}{PrimeProgramRepackSource.saved_length}
\companchor{PrimeProgramRepackSource}{PrimeProgramRepackSource.saved_changed}
The retained live word also keeps its original direct length bound $N$.
No
old-payload bound is silently reused for the enlarged shadow cache or history.

\begin{theorem}[Original raw execution through encoded return]
For prime $p,q$, typed $g,pk$, a vote, arbitrary finite initial shadow state and
live cache, and sampler slack, the composed caller starts from the canonical
original raw input with blank work. It executes the same fair-bit nonce and
transcript draw tree as the preceding caller. For every sampled tuple, its
result appends the original returned-proof encoding on port 48 and updated
saved-state encoding on port 49, preserving all 48 preceding resident words.
All numeric operands, empty workspace and enlarged record bounds are derived
from that preceding execution. No source presentation or cost certificate is
supplied by the caller.

The deterministic encoding clock adds four natural-field writer bounds, eight
pair-writer bounds, the eight temporary-word length bounds and eleven fixed
steps. Encoding charge is at most 32 times that clock. The raw caller adds the
preceding caller's charge. Its physical theorem derives startup at most
$4N+8$ and uses the composed code's own compiler factor, with the same bounded
oracle adapter on both sides.
\end{theorem}

\paragraph{Continuation interface.}
The new saved word is available, but port 14 still contains the original raw
input with its old saved state.
Reentering the whole prefix would also resample
the nonces and repeat the first proof.
The next actual continuation must retain
those nonces and the first proof, execute the other-component request at
$(\mathsf{false},r_2)$ and the overall request at $(v,r_1+r_2)$, and allow the
modular sum to be zero.
Later ballot assembly, Bob's construction, verification,
attacker execution and complete adaptive costs are outside this component's guarantee.
The enclosing
auxiliary-domain cache must remain framed.


\subsection{Historical component: Continuing with the retained nonce pair}\label{sec:second-transcript}
After the first proof $p_0$, the actual source requests the other component
and then the overall proof at witnesses
\[
  (\mathsf{false},r_2),\qquad (v,r_1+r_2).
\]
It preserves $p_0$ and threads the updated programmed state through these
requests.
Neither request resamples Alice's nonce pair.
Let $D_q$ be the original
four-full-field-draw transcript distribution.
For an honest statement $w$ and
draw tuple $c$, write $T(w,c)$ for the existing simulator transcript at $w$.

\begin{theorem}[The exact remaining proof requests]
For prime $p,q$, typed $g,pk$, arbitrary nonce pair $(r_1,r_2)$, first proof $p_0$,
finite initial programmed state $s$, live cache $L$ and continuation $K$, the
original two-request computation has exactly this source tree:
\[
\begin{aligned}
 c_1&\gets D_q,& (p_1,s_1)&=s.\mathsf{program}(w_1,T(w_1,c_1)),\\
 c_t&\gets D_q,& (p_t,s_t)&=s_1.\mathsf{program}(w_t,T(w_t,c_t)),\\
 &&&\mathsf{run}\bigl(K(p_0,p_1,p_t),s_t,L\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]
Here $w_1,w_t$ abbreviate the statements of the two witnesses above.
Specializing $K$ to the original post-proof body retains Alice's ballot assembly
and verification, Bob's constructor, both honest decisions, the attacker and
final submission, including every rejection branch.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeRemainingProgramSource}{PrimeRemainingProgramSource.remaining_program_bind}
\companchor{PrimeRemainingProgramSource}{PrimeRemainingProgramSource.continuation_eq}
\companchor{PrimeRemainingProgramSource}{PrimeRemainingProgramSource.continuation_run}
This is an equality of the original probabilistic source computations.
It does
not itself execute the remaining body in the finite machine.

\begin{example}[Nonzero components can have zero total nonce]
For $p=7,q=3,g=2,pk=4$, choose $r_1=1,r_2=2$ and $v=\mathsf{true}$.
Both component nonces are nonzero, but $r_1+r_2=0$ in $\mathbb Z_3$.
The other-component ciphertext is $(4,2)$; the overall ciphertext is $(1,2)$.
A positive-nonce precondition on the overall-proof entry would exclude this
actual source case.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}[The second ciphertext and fresh transcript prefix]
Let $p,q$ be prime, $g,pk$ typed group inputs, $v$ a vote, $s,L$ arbitrary
finite initial programmed and live states, and $\sigma$ the sampling slack.
Let $I=\mathsf{input}(g,pk,\sigma,v,\mathsf{saved}(s,L))$ be the original
\code{PrimeHonestInputMachine.input} encoding, $A_q$ the existing first nonce/transcript
tuple source, and $F_\sigma$ the existing fair-bit modulo interpretation.
Write $o=((r_1,r_2),c_0)$ for the first source tuple.
The combined caller satisfies
\[
 \mathsf{fst}\mathbin{<\$>}\mathsf{run}(M,\mathsf{clock}(I,\sigma,p,q),\mathsf{start}(I))
 =\mathsf{liftCoins}\left(\begin{array}{l}
   o\gets F_\sigma(A_q);\\
   c\gets F_\sigma(D_q);\\
   \mathsf{return}\ R(o,c).
 \end{array}\right).
\]
The result $R(o,c)$ retains every word of the first returned-proof state and
adds the ciphertext $\mathsf{encryptWith}(g,pk,r_2,0)$, its nonce digits,
the false component vote, four canonically encoded scalars and the scalar
modulus. Every supported machine result halts with charge at most
\[
 B=B_0+6R_\mathrm{route}+32E_\mathrm{encrypt}+B_\mathrm{draws}+6.
\]
Here $B_0$ is the preceding raw caller's bound, $R_\mathrm{route}$ uses
$q-1$, $E_\mathrm{encrypt}$ uses the derived nonce width
$\mathsf{size}(q-1)$, and $B_\mathrm{draws}$ is the existing four-draw bound.
The two fixed control transitions contribute the final six units.
\end{theorem}

remaining source; it does not assume an execution theorem for that remainder.
The overall nonce sum, subsequent ballots, verification, attacker execution
and complete adaptive cost are outside this component's guarantee.


The independent instruction gate covers eight distinct numeric fixtures, nine
mutations and one entry rejection across two runs.
A 120-second host timeout on
seed 606 interrupted the first run after all directed cases and mutations had
passed.
The isolated seeded replay passes seeds 118, 606 and 20260914 with a
300-second host allowance; controller and driver hashes, fixtures and modeled
cost caps are identical.
The earlier timeout remains an incomplete campaign.
Seven fixtures match typed source layouts with selected finite initial states;
the zero-nonce fixture tests the numeric boundary only.
Every completed case checks
all stored words, exact coin consumption, unused tape suffix and zero hash
queries.
There are no discards or gaveUp.
Independently specified typed endpoint
controls distinguish wrong nonces/votes, stale saved state, lost first proof and
reused scalar outputs.
No extra whole-raw native campaign is claimed for the
checked caller composition.

\subsection{Historical component: The second proof's first commitment}\label{sec:second-first-commit}
Keep the preceding notation $o=((r_1,r_2),c_0)$ and write the fresh tuple as
$c_1=(c,e,z_0,z_1)$.
The next statement is
$w_1=\mathsf{honestProofStatement}(g,pk,(\mathsf{false},r_2))$.
Its first simulator commitment has public coordinate
\[
 A_1=\bigl(g^{z_0}\alpha_1^{q-e}\bigr)\bmod p,
 \qquad \alpha_1=g^{r_2}\bmod p.
\]
Here $g,\alpha_1$ denote their canonical public residues and $e,z_0$ their
canonical scalar representatives.
When $e=0$, the executed exponent is $q$;
the typed subgroup law justifies this representative.
The power bound uses
$\mathsf{size}(q)$.

input is not reparsed.
Let $R_A(o,c_1)$ retain all 59 words of $R(o,c_1)$,

\begin{theorem}[Resident and raw execution of the first p1 commitment]
Let $p,q$ be prime, $g,pk$ typed group inputs, $v$ a Boolean vote, $s,L$ arbitrary
finite initial programmed and live states, and $\sigma\in\mathbb N$.
For every typed $o,c_1$, the relocated local controller $N$ satisfies
\[
 \exists a\leq 32K(p,q),\quad
 \mathsf{run}(N,K(p,q),\mathsf{start}(R(o,c_1).\mathsf{stk}))
 =\mathsf{pure}(R_A(o,c_1),a),
\]
where $K$ is the existing \code{PrimeSimCommitMachine.clock}. Its bound includes
complement preparation, both powers, multiplication, transfers and cleanup.
For the original input $I$ and fair-bit sources $A_q,D_q$ defined above, the
combined raw caller $M_A$ has the same source-tree equality as the preceding
theorem with result $R_A$. Every supported result halts with charge at most
\[
 B_A=B+32K(p,q).
\]
The physical corollary derives startup at most $4|I|+8$ and execution bound
$\mathsf{unitCost}(|I|+B_A)\,B_A\,\mathsf{globalFactor}(M_A)$, using the same
bounded oracle adapter on both sides.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Historical component: Completing the second proof's commitment computation}\label{sec:second-all-commit}
Retain $o,c_1,w_1$ from Section~\ref{sec:second-first-commit}.
Write
$\beta_1$ for the second coordinate of $w_1$'s ciphertext and let
$d=(c-e)\bmod q$ be the canonical scalar representative.
The remaining
simulator coordinates are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 B_1&=pk^{z_0}\beta_1^{q-e}\bmod p,\\
 \gamma_1&=\beta_1g^{q-1}\bmod p,\\
 C_1&=g^{z_1}\alpha_1^{q-d}\bmod p,\\
 D_1&=pk^{z_1}\gamma_1^{q-d}\bmod p.
 \end{aligned}
\]
These are the original group expressions
$z_0pk-e\beta_1$, $\beta_1-g$, $z_1g-d\alpha_1$ and
$z_1pk-d(\beta_1-g)$ in the additive source notation.
Subtraction in
$\mathbb Z_q$ is canonicalized before exponentiation; the controller computes $(c+q-e)\bmod q$ on canonical natural
representatives, rather than the truncated difference $\max(c-e,0)$.
The zero values $e=0$ or
$d=0$ use exponent $q$, justified by the typed subgroup law.

\begin{theorem}[All p1 commitments from the original input]
Let $p,q$ be prime, $g,pk$ typed group inputs, $v$ a Boolean vote, $s,L$ arbitrary
finite initial programmed and live states, and $\sigma\in\mathbb N$.
Let $I=\mathsf{input}(g,pk,\sigma,v,\mathsf{saved}(s,L))$.
For every typed $o,c_1$, the resident continuation executes from
$R_A(o,c_1).\mathsf{stk}$ to $R_4(o,c_1)$ without oracle queries, with charge at
most $96K+S+32G$. Here $K$ is \code{PrimeSimCommitMachine.clock} at $(p,q)$,
$S$ is \code{ScalarDifferenceMachine.cost} at $q$, and
$G$ is \code{PrimeAdjustedBetaMachine.clock} at $(p,q)$.
The combined raw caller $M_4$ satisfies
\[
 \mathsf{fst}\mathbin{<\$>}\mathsf{run}(M_4,\mathsf{clock}(I,\sigma,p,q),\mathsf{start}(I))
 =\mathsf{liftCoins}\left(\begin{array}{l}
   o\gets F_\sigma(A_q);\\
   c_1\gets F_\sigma(D_q);\\
   \mathsf{return}\ R_4(o,c_1).
 \end{array}\right).
\]
Every supported result halts with charge at most
$B_4=B_A+96K+S+32G$. For every bounded-adapter limit, physical execution has
derived startup at most $4|I|+8$
and remaining bound
$\mathsf{unitCost}(|I|+B_4)\,B_4\,\mathsf{globalFactor}(M_4)$, with the same
bounded oracle adapter on both sides.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Historical component: The second proof's original hash key}\label{sec:second-key}
Let $w_1,c_1,R_4$ be as in Section~\ref{sec:second-all-commit}.
The next source
operation programs the key
\[
 k_1=(w_1,\mathsf{ballotSimCommit}(w_1,c,(e,z_0,z_1))).
\]
Its serialized form is the original flat record of eight canonical group
coordinates, in the order
\[
  (g,pk,\alpha_1,\beta_1,A_1,B_1,C_1,D_1).
\]
Each coordinate is first encoded as a canonical natural prefix, then framed by
its encoded length; the whole record begins with its field count eight.
This is the cache-key codec from Section~\ref{sec:sim-key}, not the nested
statement codec used by the history update.

\begin{theorem}[Original raw execution through the second key]
Let $p,q$ be prime, $g,pk$ typed group inputs, $v$ a Boolean vote, $s,L$ arbitrary
finite initial programmed and live states, and $\sigma\in\mathbb N$.
Let $I=\mathsf{input}(g,pk,\sigma,v,\mathsf{saved}(s,L))$.
For every typed $o,c_1$, key serialization executes from $R_4(o,c_1)$ to
$R_K(o,c_1)$, preserving all 70 preceding words and writing
$\mathsf{ballotKeyBitCodec}(k_1)$ on port 70, without oracle queries.
Its charge is at most $32T_K(p)$, where
\[
 T_K(p)=8\,\mathsf{NatFieldWriterMachine.clock}(p-1)+1.
\]
The original raw caller has the same source-tree equality as
Theorem~14.155, with $R_K$ in place of $R_4$ and its own actual code and clock.
Every supported result halts with charge at most $B_K=B_4+32T_K(p)$.
For every bounded-adapter limit, physical execution from the original raw input
and blank work has startup at most $4|I|+8$ and remaining bound
\[
 \mathsf{unitCost}(|I|+B_K)\,B_K\,\mathsf{globalFactor}(M_K),
\]
using the same bounded oracle adapter on both sides.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Historical component: One complete programmed proof request}\label{sec:reusable-proof-request}
This component executes the second and overall historical requests with one proved procedure, its reentry routine and a composition of the two distinct source requests.
The remaining election continuation is outside this component's guarantee.

Let $p,q$ be prime, $g,pk\in\mathsf{PrimeGroup}(p,q)$, $v$ a Boolean,
$r\in\mathbb Z_q$, and $s,L$ finite programmed and live states.
No nonzero
premise is imposed on $r$.
Define the statement and sampled transcript by
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 S&=(g,pk,\mathsf{encryptWith}(g,pk,r,\mathsf{voteScalar}(v))),\\
 \tau_c&=(\mathsf{ballotSimCommit}(S,c,e,z_0,z_1),c,(e,z_0,z_1)).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here $(c,e,z_0,z_1)$ is the original full-field transcript draw.
The notation
compresses the nested tuples in \code{PrimeProofRequest.transcript}.
The initial configuration $I$ is the canonical resident encoding of these
inputs, with blank working/output slots and two arbitrary retained byte words.
It is specified by \code{PrimeProofRequest.start}; raw-input preparation is not
part of this entry contract.

\begin{theorem}[Complete resident request execution]
For every such input and slack $\sigma\in\mathbb N$, the fixed finite code $M$
satisfies
\[
 \pi_{\mathrm{cfg}}\bigl(\mathsf{run}(M,T,I)\bigr)
 =\mathsf{simulateQ}(\mathsf{liftCoins})\left(
 R\mathbin{<\$>}\mathsf{runFairBitUniform}_{\sigma}
       (\mathsf{drawTranscriptScalars}(q))\right).
\]
Every supported run result halts and has charge at most $B$. The configuration
$R(c,e,z_0,z_1)$ contains the original encoded proof returned by
$s.\mathsf{program}(S,\tau_c)$ and the encoding of its successor state paired
with the unchanged live cache $L$.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeProofRequest}{PrimeProofRequest.execution_source}
\companchor{PrimeProofRequest}{PrimeProofRequest.run_support}
\companchor{PrimeProofRequest}{PrimeProofRequest.fullResult_return}

The clock $T$ and charge bound $B$ are sums of the existing draw/encryption,
commitment/key, programming and output-encoding clocks and charges.
The latter
two use the current state's derived bounds
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 N_i&=\max\{|\mathsf{enc}(s.\mathsf{cache})|,
                 |\mathsf{enc}(L)|,|\mathsf{enc}(s.\mathsf{programmed})|\},\\
 N_o&=\max\{|s.\mathsf{cache}|,|s.\mathsf{programmed}|,|\mathsf{enc}(L)|\}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
No execution or cost certificate is supplied by the caller.
Literal state
identities connect the stages; semantic realization equality is not used to
infer a structural presentation.
Successor-size bounds follow from actual
cache insertion and history growth, including occupied-key and sticky-bad
behavior.

\subsection{Historical component: Historical reentry and the architecture assessment}
\label{sec:execution-architecture}
The bounded assessment asks whether one complete request implementation removes
repeated proof work.
Its acceptance test is the actual second component request
at $(\mathsf{false},r_2)$ followed by the overall request at $(v,r_1+r_2)$,
with current state and prior proofs retained.
The complete combined run/source/cost theorem now checks, alongside the
component theorems and independent bounded test below.
The assessment ends before further protocol construction.

Let $U(n)$ denote the existing scalar-prefix encoding.
Reentry takes a mode $t$,
natural $r_1,r_2<q$, a vote $v$ and 58 resident words $W$.
Its entry premises are
\[
 W_2=\mathsf{bits}(q),\quad W_{20}=U(r_1),\quad W_{21}=U(r_2),\quad
 W_{18}=[v],\quad W_{23},\ldots,W_{29},W_{37}=\epsilon.
\]
Set $N=\max_i|W_i|$.
Define $W'$ by clearing fields
\[
 \{1,2,3,7,10\text{--}13,23\text{--}29,37\text{--}40,42,43,49,50,51\},
\]
preserving all other fields, and then writing
\[
 (W'_{50},W'_{51})=
 \begin{cases}
 (U((r_1+r_2)\bmod q),[v]) & t=\mathsf{true},\\
 (U(r_2),[\mathsf{false}]) & t=\mathsf{false}.
 \end{cases}
\]
\begin{theorem}[Executed historical reentry]
Under these entry conditions the fixed reentry code starts at
\code{PrimeProofRequestReentry.start}$(t,W)$, with memory 2 and entry label
1 for total or 0 for p1. It runs for its defined clock $T_R(q,N)$ to the
halted configuration with memory 2 and words $W'$. Its actual
charge is at most $32T_R(q,N)$; it makes no oracle requests.
\end{theorem}
\companchor{PrimeProofRequestReentry}{PrimeProofRequestReentry.charged}
The statement compresses the literal endpoint and clock definitions.
Both
historical endpoints derive the displayed entry premises; full configuration
identities present the next request's canonical start.
The nonce sum may be zero.
Earlier component ciphertexts/proofs and original nonces/vote survive the total
request's defined endpoint.

\begin{example}[A rejected operand map]
An earlier layout supplied the intended one-bit nonce sum to the prefix writer's
input instead of its counter. Three actual writer steps then emitted $U(0)$ and
left the intended value 1 untouched. The corrected counter layout emits $U(1)$.
A zero-sum case alone cannot distinguish these layouts. The checked negative
and positive controls retain this representation defect.
\end{example}
